PREFACE 

One of the most pertinent trends in the evolution of the social studies curriculum comprises the attempt to induce in the student the desire for a realistic understanding of the world in which he lives and the effort to make possible the realization of that desire. City and state courses of study, textbooks, reference materials, and the reports on social studies of various commissions refer to the importance of the students' acquiring such an understanding. The 

Report of the Commission on the Social Studies of the American Historical Society holds it possible and desirable for everyone to gain accurate knowledge and informed opinion, including a realistic comprehension of the tensions and the problems, as well as the ideals and traditions, of today. The desired result is that all decisions be based upon reason, with due regard for the implications involved. 

Citizenship in Our Democracy has been planned and written with care, its main object being to help the student attain authentic knowledge and informed opinion about certain aspects of present-day group life. The book lends itself to many uses in various effective plans of teaching elementary civics in the junior high school.

Part One, The Individual Citizen and the Group, presents materials for the purpose of establishing the significance of groups in our lives, the fact that iii

iv 

Preface groups render services, and an understanding of how groups proceed in rendering their services. 

Part Two, Individuals through the Agency of Groups Provide Services Requiring Coöperative Action, presents materials for the purpose of contributing to an understanding of group action, and of problems in several aspects of living together, such as health and security.

Part Three, Individuals Working through the Agency of Government, presents materials for the purpose of establishing the facts and principles of the procedures of democratic government. 

Part Four, The Development and Meaning of the 

Constitution, presents materials for the purpose of making the Constitution and its development meaningful.

Part Five, Achieving Our Ideals, presents materials for the purpose of indicating the more significant ideals of American democracy and their meaning in our lives.

Throughout the book, the citizenship of the individual, as a member of civil groups and of society as well, is interpreted in its broadest meaning. It is believed that knowledge of the structure of governments should follow from a study of how governments function. The relations of the individual to the group, the relations of the group to the individual, the presence of rapid change, the ideals of democracy, the necessity of coöperation, and character-building are regarded as keynotes. The glossing over of reality is avoided, and every effort is made to present

Preface 

V accurate information, ideas, goals, and processes involved in intelligent living together today and in the future.

The dominant idea throughout is the responsibility of the individual to participate in the effective functioning of the principles and ideals of democracy in all areas of human activity. The chief requisite for this attainment is that each individual think for himself. Complete and accurate information as well as the ideals of the group must be utilized. 

In making use of  with groups of students it is suggested that: (1) Extensive use be made of the local environment and contemporary problems of living together to provide as many direct experiences as possible. (1) Extensive use be made of the local environment and contemporary problems of living together interests, and the needs of the group. (3) Emphasis be placed upon the necessity of each individual's thinking his way through problems, utilizing complete and accurate data. (4) The necessary attention be devoted to making words meaningful to the students. Words are merely the symbols for meanings. 

(5) The lists of Things to Do and Learn at the end of each chapter be regarded as suggestions, and that the teacher and the students select from them appropriate ones, recognizing the advisability of supplementing the list. (6) Many other books and sources of information be utilized by the group. The responsibility of select-

vi 

Preface ing other books and sources of information has been placed in the hands of the teachers, because of the continuous appearance of new materials and the wide variation in the availability of material in different localities. Each teacher should make use of all appropriate materials supplied by the community, the local school board, and the state. She should also be alert in her search for new material.

The authors wish to acknowledge, and to express thanks for, the valuable assistance given them by the work of many of the recognized leaders in the social studies curriculum development, many students in their classes, and several junior high school teachers of the social studies. Mr. R. C. Morrison provided helpful suggestions and materials for the illustrations. Without this assistance, Citizenship in Our 

Democracy could not have been written.

CHAPTER I INDIVIDUALS LIVE, WORK, LEARN AND PLAY IN GROUPS 

The shrill whistle from the mill caused Tom Williamson to awake with a start. He rubbed his eyes and tried to think. What were his plans for the day? Then he remembered. This was the day school started! He sat lazily in his bed for a few minutes, thinking of the good times he had had during the summer-swimming, playing baseball, learning to drive the car, attending the recreation school with all the boys, helping Ed Gregory deliver his papers, and having the nice trip with the family. All this had to end now. He sighed regretfully as he realized that the winter months would offer other things.

As Tom hurriedly dressed he thought of the day and the activities at school. Things would be centered around the new consolidated junior high school which was opening today. The new building was a spur to Tom's enthusiasm. The new football field, the tennis courts, the new science room, and new auditorium should make school seem a different place.

The odors from the kitchen assured Tom that breakfast was in the making. He called to his younger sister, Margaret, as he went by her room; 1

then sat down by his father who was reading at the breakfast table.

A few minutes later the whole family was busily engaged in eating breakfast and talking of plans for the day. Mr. Williamson had additional duties on 

Monday for the civic league, making it necessary to remain an hour longer in the office. He couldn't miss a meeting because plans for paving a street in town were being discussed, and each member of the committee must be present. 

A family group at breakfast.

Mrs. Williamson was intent upon arranging for the use of the car, as she was going to visit a friend in the hospital and then do some necessary shopping for the house.

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 3 

Margaret was going to meet two friends of hers who lived in the neighborhood, as they walked together to the elementary school every morning if it could be arranged. The girls were eager for school to begin, for they wanted to see the new teacher who had taken the place of Miss Franklin, their former teacher, who had moved away. 

Tom had made no definite plans to join his friends, as he must ride the bus to the new junior high school. 

This was a new feature added to the daily routine, one which would be fun sometimes and then again a disadvantage, particularly if he missed the bus some rainy morning. The school bus served his community and several others and Tom knew enough about schedules to be aware that every student must be at his appointed place on time. 

One hour later Tom found himself on the new school ground shouting to old friends and playing ball. The bus trip had been fun. There were certain regulations they must observe if they were to ride the bus. The driver was careful but had talked frankly to the students. He was responsible for their safety and they in turn must do as he asked them, or it might be disastrous for all. 

Tom was amazed at the large number of students walking and playing on the school ground. It made the old elementary school seem small and far away. 

He recognized some of the students from neighboring communities that he had seen at track meets and swimming matches. He thought it would be nice to know some of the new students, for he was some-

what bored by having to be with the same crowd all the time.

When the bell rang for the students to assemble in the building, Tom had a queer feeling of strangeness. He hadn't given his teachers a thought before this time, but now he must see them and begin work again.

The superintendent welcomed them in the new auditorium, introduced the teachers, and told of some procedures they should follow. The students then went to their classes. 

CASTLEBERRY BUS. 

A modern school bus. 

The day was uneventful but interesting. As 

Tom rode home that afternoon, he recalled the high points of the day. They all liked the new cafeteria. 

It was fun to eat away from home for a change

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 

5 and sit with a group of boys. Of course, they had to stand in line and take their turn at being served, and they couldn't be rowdy in the dining room, but it was fun just the same. The science teacher and the health education director appealed especially to Tom. Just the way they talked and acted had made him know the importance of fair play. He hoped he could make good for them, and, most of all, he hoped the year would continue as well as it had started.

As the bus sped along, passing many familiar sights, Tom had a feeling of satisfaction. He didn't feel strange or small any more. It was like being a part of a large organization. 

We live in groups. This little story about Tom 

Williamson might be the story of almost any one of us in school today. We are so familiar with such everyday occurrences that we do not often think of these things as affecting our lives, our thoughts, and our characters very much. However, each of us is surrounded by similar conditions. We do not live to ourselves.

We do not work or play alone. We are members of many groups: home, school, clubs, church, neighborhood, community, nation. There are very few people who live entirely to themselves today; in fact, it would be nearly impossible in our modern world. 

Even though a person lives in an apartment or a single room with no relatives or friends near, he does not live entirely to himself. Other people are living in the same house, or near by. The food he

eats usually passes through other hands than his own before it is ready for his use. He seldom makes his clothing, and he does not raise sheep, shear their wool, and weave the cloth out of which his clothing is made. If he is sick, he may call a doctor. If he is in danger, he may call the police. If he attends a play, a ball game, or a lecture, he may invite a friend to accompany him.

Most of us are members of families, with whom we live. Our homes are thus occupied by a closely related group. Each of us is a definite part of such a group. From babyhood, each one of us lives in an intimate family association.

The family is not the only group in which one lives. Few families in our land today live very far from other families. Thus neighborhoods exist, composed of groups of families. Each person is a part of the neighborhood in which he lives. In each neighborhood there are many groups that are not families. In turn, each neighborhood is but part of a larger community, composed of many more persons, sharing many activities of life within the community group.

Did you know that there are over 3,000,000 boys and girls who are attending junior high school?

There are about 28,000,000 children in the United States enrolled in schools (1930 census). Of this number, about 3,000,000 are in the seventh and eighth grades, and live, as Tom does, in some kind of family group and go to school each day, surrounded by varying numbers of classmates.

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 

7 Our country is made up of millions of neighborhoods, more or less similar to the neighborhood in which Tom lives. In turn, these neighborhoods make up thousands of communities, some of them parts of large cities, and some of them districts of common interests in one great countryside. Not all communities have the same interests. In one region, farming is the chief occupation of the people; in another, mining; in another, fishing. Those people who live in the farming community might not feel very much at home if suddenly transplanted to the mining region. But wherever they might go, they would find people living near one another, with family groups making up the larger neighborhood and community groups.

So far, we have been talking about our own country, the United States. But there are many other countries in the world. There are about 2,000,000,000 people on the earth. The yellow and brown races compose over half of these; the white race over onethird; and the black race the remainder. People speak about 130 different languages and belong to more than 60 nations. Do the people in other lands live as we do? We know that their customs may be different, and that they may speak languages which are not like ours. Do they have family groups, neighborhoods, and communities? Yes, everywhere we find people grouped together by blood ties, common interests, and common means of livelihood.

We work in groups. Do you remember that Tom

realized the necessity of being on time for the school bus, found it necessary to await his turn in the cafeteria, and that on the first day of school he realized the importance of fair play? He was beginning to understand the meaning of coöperation and willingness on the part of every boy and girl. We not only live in groups; we also work in groups. 

You have seen a baby who was just learning the use of his hands and feet try to do something all by himself. Perhaps it was an attempt to put on his shoes. He would work and tug at his task, and if he did it successfully, how delighted he would be! 

And how much more delighted he would be at the praise of his mother or other members of the family. 

Although we are all pleased by the successful completion of an individual piece of work, we, like the baby, work best under the inspiration of the group around us. If someone else has a similar individual task, how we like to measure our own ability by his, and how we like to compare the finished products of our labors! An individual task often becomes unattractive simply because one must do it by oneself. Washing dishes with someone to help may not be entirely delightful, but how much more pleasant it is than doing it alone!

There are many kinds of work which cannot be done by one person alone. The carpenters work together to raise the rafters of a new house. The reporters, copyreaders, linotypers, and editors all work together to get out a daily newspaper. The men who sail the seas have to work together to get

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 9 

a big modern steamer across the ocean and into port. 

None of these kinds of work can be done by one person alone. Of course, one might build some sort of house without help, but what a long, hard job it would be! One might assemble information enough for a newspaper, and eventually set up the type to print it, but not all in one day! Men have sailed the seas alone, but not in big ocean steamers! 

Tom's teachers said that coöperation is necessary. 

Suppose the carpenters, in erecting a house, begin to lay the floor. One man wants to do something else first. Another wants to rest awhile before he is ready to help. Still a third does not approve of the work already done. He thinks it would have been better if it had been done some other way. The other carpenters have other suggestions. Do you think the floor would be laid very soon? Suppose the sailors on a ship argue about their destination or their respective tasks. Would the ship sail safely and smoothly into port on scheduled time?

Many people working together make it possible for us to have food, clothing, shelter, amusements, and many other services. Perhaps these people do not work side by side. Perhaps they do not even see each other's work, or even know each other. 

Your breakfast toast is the result of the work of many people, all coöperating to produce the bread. 

Your mother depends upon the grocer to have a fresh loaf of bread for her. He depends upon the man who delivers it to him from the bakery. The delivery man depends upon the bakers to have it

ready for him when delivery time comes. The bakers depend upon the flour merchant who provides the freshly ground flour from the mill. The millers depend upon the farmers who raise the grain and the merchants who handle it before it is brought to the mill. 

Did you have orange juice or bananas at breakfast? Not only workmen in our own country, but people in faraway tropical lands have worked to produce these fruits and make them available for you. The same things are true of your clothing, your furniture, and your other possessions. Many hands have had a part in the preparation of each thing that you use.

People work in groups because it makes the work easier and more pleasant. They work in groups because they can accomplish together tasks which would be impossible to do separately. They work in groups because they have learned the value of cooperation and teamwork. All over the world, we find people working together. 

In the family, the little child is happy in doing his share about the home, helping his mother or caring for his own wants so that others may have more time for other tasks. At school, we work together under the guidance of our teachers to accomplish the greatest good in acquiring knowledge and in improving our abilities. In the neighborhood, work is divided so that each one supplies his share. 

One is a grocer; one a baker; one a clothing merchant; one a bookkeeper.

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 11 

Each one's work aids the others in the neighborhood; each one is aided by the work of others. In larger communities, composed of several neighborhoods, the workers depend upon one another. People in far distant lands in all parts of the world 

Interesting activities at school. have a part in work which affects our everyday lives. All over the world individuals work together in groups to make available the goods and services that individuals use.

We learn in groups. In our modern world we have all the learning of past ages. Although the world has a vast store of education and culture, no person could profit from that knowledge without help. He needs the cooperation of parents, teachers, writers,

artists, and all others who have learned before him. 

Imagine the condition of a child who has been separated from everyone else. How would he learn to talk, to read, to understand the things in the world, if no one helped him?

Our education begins at birth, and is secured by associating with other people and by learning how others lived before us. Think how many things a child has learned by the time he is five years old! 

Did he learn by himself? No, he has learned from those about him. Some things, of course, he has learned by himself. But how much more he knows and how many more things he is able to do because of his association with others! So far in his life the groups to which he belongs have been limited to family, friends, and neighborhood. When he enters school, he becomes a member of another group, composed of children of his own age and experience. He finds some members of the group with greater knowledge and ability than his. Daily contacts with them enlarge his knowledge, his abilities, and his desire to learn. He finds other members of the group who do not know all the things that he knows, and to them he displays his learning, and thus improves his own ability by teaching them. Under the teacher's guidance a whole new world is opened to him. Through reading, he may come in contact with the knowledge of the human race. Naturally, he cannot read it all. He would not have the time in a lifetime. He must depend upon someone to help him select. His being a member of a group is of

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 13 

tremendous help to him. Because of widely varied interests, others may read books which he has not read. In the class he may profit from the knowledge and experiences of others. 

Our opportunities to learn are endless. Wherever we are, we may hear or see something which is new to us. The opinions we form are based upon our contacts and our experiences. We gain certain beliefs from family traditions and customs. The opinions of our parents and older members of our families influence our thinking. Our churches influence thoughts and beliefs about life and behavior. We learn much from our daily associations in the neighborhood. People who live near one another discuss work, styles, manners, morals, and many other things. Our friends, with whom we play and work, also help us to learn more and to form opinions which become a part of our education. We do not learn alone any more than we live alone or work alone. We learn in groups. 

We play in groups. A long time ago someone who had to be all by himself for a long period of time invented a game which he called "solitaire." 

By playing this game he managed to pass away many lonely hours. Many people today play the game to amuse themselves when they have no one to play with them. But even though it is a "oneman game," we have heard players talk of beating "old Sol" as if he were a real person. Not many people play alone from choice, and if a person has no one to play with, he may invent an imaginary

companion. Little children with few playmates often have imaginary playmates who are as real to them as the actual people about them. Almost everyone finds pleasure in being with others. We like to share with our friends our enjoyment of a moving picture, a football game, or a circus. These are all forms of recreation, or play. 30 

A modern school playground.

The most common form of recreation is some kind of game. There are parlor games which are played by small groups around a table in the house. There are outdoor games which are played by individual opponents or partners. Such a game might be tennis, or croquet. Then there are games which have opposing teams of players, such as baseball or football, in which large groups take part. There are numberless folk games, such as "hide and seek,"

We Live, Work, Learn and Play in Groups 15 

"blind man's buff," and various forms of "tag."

All of these games are played by groups of players. 

We do not play alone, any more than we live alone, work alone, or learn alone. 

Study carefully the illustration, Home and Community, at the front of this book. It shows that the family depends upon many groups and individuals for its clothing, food, education, health and many other necessary services.

1. Make a list of all the groups to which you belong. 

2. Find out how consolidated schools are organized, and list some of the advantages of consolidation over small community schools. 

3. Find out the regulations for a school bus, and the qualifications a driver should have. 

4. Write an imaginary story about a boy who had to live entirely alone for a year, with no contacts with anyone else. 

5. Read stories of family or neighborhood life in other lands, and tell the class some interesting points of similarity and contrast with your own life. 

6. Make a breakfast or dinner menu and show how many people have contributed to the production of the foods you have listed. 

7. Make a table of recreations and sports. Show the number of players who may participate in each.

CHAPTER II GROUPS RENDER SERVICES TO INDIVIDUALS 

Tom Williamson is proud of his home and parents and little sister. He is glad he lives in a home of his own. Tom had never thought much about his good fortune until two of his playmates next door lost their parents in an automobile accident. They did not have any relatives to take care of them and they had to go away to live in a Children's Home. This made Tom realize how much better off he is than some other boys and girls in the world about him. 

Tom comes home from school each day to a pleasant room, a good supper, and a comfortable bed. 

Perhaps his former neighbors have good food and good beds where they live now, but Tom has many things which he knows they do not have any more. 

He has a mother and a father who live with him and who are interested in him. He has companionship and affection. He is sure that these familiar things. are gone from the experiences of those children who lost their parents and their home, and went away to live with many other children.

The services of the family group. The family group looks after our material comforts. We need food, clothing, and shelter. Our personal needs are supplied by the family. The child's parents care for 16

him when he is a baby, watch over him when he is sick, furnish him with food and clothing, and give him all the comforts which they can afford. Not only that, the family gives him kindness and affection, companionship and pleasures, which make the family circle very dear to him. A happy home is something to which every child is entitled, and its influence upon the child's life and character is very great. In the ideal home the parents and children have good times together and are interested in the experiences of one another. A family group at work and play. 

Present-day homes are not so independent as the homes of our grandfathers. Providing food and

clothing used to be almost entirely family tasks, and each member of the family had his special jobgardening, milking, taking care of the poultry, shearing sheep, weaving cloth, or preparing home-produced articles for use. Today, large amounts of food and most of our clothing are purchased from merchants. 

Still, the procuring of these things is handled by the parents. The modern boy may be sent to get a bottle of milk from the store instead of being sent to milk the family's cows, but the service to the family group is the same.

Teaching the child to talk and to understand the words of others is the first educational service of the family group. Most of us cannot remember a time when we were unable to talk. We seem to speak and understand our English language naturally, and we seldom stop to think that we had to learn to speak our own language and that it requires effort, training, and practice. It is sometimes surprising to boys and girls to hear a little child, two or three years old perhaps, speaking a language which they cannot understand. "But," exclaims someone, "of course the child would speak French. His parents are French, and that is the language they speak in their home." That child is learning his native tongue just as our children here in American homes learn to speak English. Of course, we know how necessary it is to speak the language of our country, and to be able to understand those about us. We could not work or play together if we did not speak a common language.

Most of the individual's opinions and tastes are results of family training and associations. Children usually like what their parents like, and express the same preferences that they hear expressed at home. If the mother or father is interested in music or art, the children of that family are usually trained to love and appreciate good music or fine art. If "Dad" is a football or baseball fan, son is likely to be, also. If parents read many books and magazines, children usually like to read extensively. In many cases, a child may express a firm belief about politics, religion, or fashion simply because of the opinions of the older members of the family. It is not difficult for us to realize that the family serves as a guide in many of our tastes and opinions.

Our first ideas of right and wrong are gained in our homes. We learn honesty or dishonesty, selfishness or unselfishness, obedience or self-will, through the training and behavior of our family group. Parents may teach lessons of behavior by stories or commands, and they may set examples before their children by their daily lives and actions. A boy who is taught the highest ideals in his home is a strong character wherever he may be. One who is unfortunate enough to have few high ideals placed before him may develop into a weak character or one who is really vicious or criminal. Characterbuilding is one of the most important services of the family group.

Habits of work and conduct are formed largely in the home. If a child is trained to do his home tasks

carefully and promptly, he is forming habits which will stay with him all of his life. If he is allowed to do his tasks in a slipshod manner, or to put them off until the last possible minute, he is developing habits of carelessness and delay which will be very difficult to break in later years.

Religious beliefs are usually developed in the family circle. The child's first impressions of God are acquired from his parents. If they are very religious, the child gains an early faith in God and the Bible. 

Even though he may drift far from his early teaching in religion, the childhood lessons which he has learned in the home will remain with him. People who have had no religious training in the home seldom become very religious in their adult lives. 

Every home is similar to a government, with its rules made on a coöperative basis. Children as well as parents must obey these rules. Unless a person lives all alone, he cannot always do just as he pleases without inconveniencing others. In order to make home pleasant and orderly, we must have authority and obedience. We learn our first rules in the home, and if we learn them well, we find it much easier to obey the rules of other groups. Consideration for others is the basis of home rules. Even a family of two or three persons must have some rules or system of conduct, or one individual will impose upon the others and make conditions difficult and unhappy. The larger the family group, the more necessary we find it to have rules and government directing the behavior of its members.

American children have more freedom than any other children in the world. Among the Chinese disobedience or disrespect is almost unknown. In most foreign countries parents are much more strict than American fathers and mothers. In our own pioneer days, family rules and commands were enforced without consulting the children's wishes very much. Now, parents guide very small children, but try to give them a voice in deciding matters as soon as they are old enough to understand. 

Think how many family groups there are in the world! In the United States, with 130,000,000 people, there are over 25,000,000 family groups. In that number of our homes, children are being trained, their habits are being formed, and their characters are being molded in the ideals which they will follow all their lives. Over the world, there are many millions more people than there are in the United States. And there are family groups in every nation, rendering services to the individuals who make up each family.

The services of the school. Outside the family group the school influences the lives of more individuals than any other community agency. Education is America's largest public enterprise. More than 

28,000,000 of our American children are attending school each school day of the year. The schools of the United States cost many millions of dollars each year and require the services of approximately 

1,000,000 teachers. Education is the greatest force in the development of any nation or people.

Schools help in many ways to enrich the lives of those who attend. They provide individuals with their first tools of learning: reading, writing, and arithmetic. These tool subjects are not enough to know, however. They merely serve as means of acquiring more knowledge which in turn increases earning capacity and makes life more enjoyable. 

Possibly the greatest contribution of the schools is the preparation they give to individuals for earning a living. They make it possible to understand world civilization and culture and, through this knowledge of the past, to build a better present and future. They also train us for service to others. An educated person does not cease to learn when he leaves school: education has given him basic facts, and trained him in the ability to think and to make decisions of his own. His life becomes a continuous process of learning, and intelligent thinking and doing. 

These contributions of education have not always been present in our schools. There is a great difference in the school you attend and the one your forefathers attended. Their school equipment, teaching methods, and subjects taught have little resemblance to those of the present-day school. In the period around the year 1800, for example, the average

American citizen received less than 100 days of schooling in his entire lifetime. Now, most boys and girls attend school from 150 to over 200 days in a single year.

The United States is famous for its public schools. 

They are part of a democratic system of government.

One of the first things the early American settlers did was to consider schools. These pioneers were primarily interested in religion and they soon learned that the cause of religion would suffer if the people could not read and write. For that reason the first textbooks were the Bible and the Bay Psalm Book. 

The first schools were established in the homes; then later, in larger towns, the education of boys was made possible in schools operated independently. 

Massachusetts led the way, in 1647, in giving educational opportunities to all within her colony. Other 

New England colonies, with the exception of Rhode Island, followed this example and laid the foundation stones for public schools of the future. Leaders accepted the principle of public education and declared that "schools and means of education shall ever be encouraged." The idea of taxing people to pay for schools grew very slowly and was the cause of much argument among the leaders in each community. Today, there are free public schools throughout America paid for by taxation.

The services of the church. The churches are group organizations which include many of the people of our land. Religion plays an important part in the lives of any people. Churches are almost everywhere. People recognize them as agencies for good wherever they may be. Let us see how the church serves the individual. First of all, it helps a person to live right, to have a due regard for others, and to conduct himself properly. The church builds ideals and upholds the nobler things in life. It seeks

to develop good behavior and righteous character. 

Although our religion has its beginning in the home, it is developed and strengthened in the church. 

Everyone recognizes the great moral influence of the churches. Religion is as old as the human race. It has progressed in its spiritual thoughts and ideals as the civilization of the race has progressed, and in the world today, spiritual thought has risen far beyond the simple beliefs of early man. 

A group in Sunday school.

In the second place, churches give religious education to people. The most common form of this training is the Sunday school, which had its origin about one hundred and fifty years ago in England.

At the beginning of the Industrial Revolution, many small children worked in factories and had little opportunity to attend school. Some of the people decided to teach these children to read and write by having simple lessons for them at the churches on Sunday. Soon this idea developed into religious training, and churches everywhere adopted the practice. 

Although it is not necessary today for churches to teach reading and writing, they have Sunday schools to teach religious beliefs and practices. The influence of the Sunday school is widely felt. It has been estimated that over sixteen million boys and girls are regular attendants in these schools. The churches usually have classes for older people as well as for young people. They often maintain vacation schools which meet on week days after the regular public school sessions close. Some churches support parochial schools, academies, and colleges with courses similar to those of the public schools. These schools usually have religious courses in addition to the customary school subjects.

The educational work of churches often reaches far beyond the communities in which the churches are located. Mission schools are sometimes maintained in the foreign sections of the great cities, among the tenement districts of the very poor, and far away in other lands. The world-wide educational work of the church is varied in scope. Missions may have doctors, nurses, or trained workers in industry or farming, as well as instructors in religion. In 

China and India, the church missions have helped

thousands who otherwise would have had no medical attention. Rules of sanitation and health are taught, and better living conditions are thus brought about. 

Natives are trained in better methods of agriculture and other occupations. 

Another service of the church, in its own community as well as in foreign missions, is caring for the needy or unfortunate. Church homes or hospitals are often supported by individual churches, or by several working together. When floods or tornadoes cause heavy damage and destruction in our own or other lands, the churches send money and sometimes workers to aid the suffering victims of the disasters. 

People usually give liberally to funds raised by churches to help the poor, the sick, or the injured. 

Churches serve their communities by their social activities. They offer pleasant and profitable means of recreation. Modern church buildings often have, in addition to the auditorium and classrooms, wellequipped kitchens and parlors, reading rooms and libraries, lecture rooms and play rooms. In crowded city communities, where people live in small apartments with little place for sports and games, some churches have swimming pools, bowling alleys, and gymnasiums. Instead of remaining open only at the regular church services, these churches keep open all the time, and the people who live near them, both young and old, may join in social gatherings, enjoy wholesome entertainments, and have contacts with a wide circle of friends.

Although the churches may differ in belief and

practice, they render a great service to people by teaching the worship of God and service to mankind. All churches try to uphold high ideals and good morals. All furnish religious education, aid to the unfortunate, and beneficial recreation and pleasure for their members.

The services of special groups. In addition to the groups which we have mentioned already, there are many special groups, such as clubs, bands, societies, leagues, and lodge organizations. Nearly everyone belongs to some such group, and most of us belong to more than one. These groups are not usually so permanent as those of family, school, or church. The little girls of a neighborhood may organize a sewing circle through summer vacation, or the boys may have a gang which meets for a time to play baseball on a vacant lot. The homebuilders of a neighborhood may belong to a civic league which is working for certain local improvements. In school, one may belong to a debating society, which may continue for several terms, or hold membership in a club organized by the teachers at school or by the parents in the neighborhood. 

One may join the Boy Scouts, or the Camp Fire

Girls, and become a member of a nation-wide organization. There are thousands and thousands of these special groups in our country. Most of them serve some good purpose, and in doing so, render services to their members.

In the first place, membership in these groups is voluntary. One need not join unless he desires, and

he may withdraw if he wishes. There may be a certain prestige gained by membership in a particular club, or there may be some advantages which one desires. People who are associated in these groups are usually held together by common interests, and they benefit from contacts with one another. 

If the purposes and needs for the organization disappear, the group parts company and the club ceases to exist. If one's needs or desires should change, he may withdraw from the organization and join another more to his liking. 

Special groups and organizations direct the attitudes and beliefs of their members. Thus, they are a source of education and training, however informal they may be. They have rules, sometimes definitely stated, as in a Boy Scout manual, and sometimes unwritten and merely understood, as one may observe in a neighborhood gang of boys. They are usually coöperative organizations, whose members learn to work together, to share duties and pleasures, and to follow leaders. They thus develop attitudes of fair play and responsibility, and often give members training in leadership which may serve in much greater capacity in later life. 

Some organizations are purely recreational, and members come together for entertainment and pleasure. Baseball clubs and bridge clubs would fall into this classification. Other groups may have recreation and entertainment, and at the same time serve a much greater purpose in character-building and training for service. The Young Men's Chris-

A group enjoying a park. tian Association and the Young Women's Christian

Association are examples, as also are the Boy 

Scouts and the Camp Fire Girls. School clubs often furnish education along special lines, such as singing, debating, drawing, or reading. Members learn many things which would not be profitable for a whole class in school, or which would take too much time to be done in regular school hours. Also, they may learn parliamentary procedure and rules of conduct that will be of benefit to them in other organizations which they may join at some later time. 

Through club membership we learn the value of coöperative action. The neighborhood civic league

may accomplish much in local improvements by working together. Singly, the members would get little done. And as neighborhoods work through concerted action, so larger groups also may work. 

Nationwide movements are often started in clubs. 

Farmers sometimes belong to coöperative marketing organizations, formed to aid them in selling their produce. Merchants form associations to promote common interests. Bankers coöperate in the handling of checks and other business. Cities have "clean-up campaigns," with temporary organizations to direct the work. Many communities have organized drives for charity, such as the various Community Chest organizations. All these show the value of coöperative action. 

Thus, we see that both temporary and permanent groups composed of voluntary members render valuable services to many people. They furnish education and training, influence opinions and attitudes, build character, and accomplish many things by coöperative action. Every country has many, many groups of people banded together for work or play in such organizations as these we have described. 

The services of neighborhood and community. By neighbor we usually mean someone who lives near us. A number of neighbors together would make a neighborhood. Just as in the case of gangs or clubs, neighbors are drawn together by common interests and for the advantages of group action. Perhaps the neighborhood is in a fertile farming region. The families that live there are all interested in the

production and marketing of their crops. They have discovered the importance of working together, and thus the neighborhood serves each individual living in the group. Perhaps it is a mining or lumbering region, or a village close to the sea where the residents are engaged in fishing. Wherever the neighborhood may be, it is a more or less closely bound group rendering services to its members. 

There are millions of neighborhoods in the United States, many very similar in interests and services, and many very much unlike others. Rural communities have many points of similarity, whether the people raise cotton or sugarbeets. Ranch communities are much alike, wherever they may be. 

City communities may share the conveniences of common transportation or communication services, or other public utilities, and yet be entirely unlike. 

In one section of a city may be found the homes of the factory workers; in another, the homes of the professional and business men. Usually the neighborhood is composed of people of similar financial standing or culture, since they were drawn together in the first place by similar interests.

The attitudes of the neighborhood affect the thinking and the actions of the people who live there. 

The neighborhoods guide the development of beliefs and opinions. This is shown sometimes by the conflicts of groups who live on "opposite sides of the railroad track." Feuds between neighborhoods are often promoted by the actions of people who have developed different attitudes by constant repeti-

tion of the opinions and beliefs of their neighbors. 

The financial standing of one neighborhood may set it above or below that of another neighborhood because of differences in living standards. 

Neighborhood talk or gossip may influence the opinions or actions of the individuals in the district. 

Certain morals may be set as the standard. There will be little change from the standard; for we are always making decisions based upon the possible opinions of the neighbors. Certain recreations will become customary in a neighborhood, as will certain kinds of work. In one district, tennis may be the most popular game; in another, horseback riding or golf will be more common. Young people of one community may be interested in learning mechanical industries; in another, most of the boys and girls may study business or professional vocations. 

Neighborhoods often set the standards of styles of dress and behavior, and even the selection of one's friends. So we see that we are influenced in both thought and action by the communities in which we live.

Not only do communities influence our opinions and ways of acting, but they render service by division of labor. Some people furnish supplies for others through groceries, dairies, or department stores. Some serve others as skilled workmen, such as shoe-repair men and automobile mechanics. Some are doctors, lawyers, or ministers. Some offer forms of entertainment, as theater operators or radio performers. The wants of the whole community are

thus supplied by the specialized work of those who live within the group.

Neighborhoods were the first of our common groups to recognize the value of public schools. 

Churches also were first established as neighborhood projects. As these organizations for the good of the group developed, it became necessary to select some members with authority to act for all. In this way, community government began. We have already seen that rules are necessary in the family group and in the school, church, and club. Rules are necessary in communities also to insure cooperation and accomplishment of real service. In pioneer communities little was ever accomplished until law was established. Although some people may consider rules and laws as restraints to keep them from doing as they please, they soon find that wise ones are very helpful. For example, regular times for opening schools, shops, and stores make them much more effective and dependable. The traffic laws prevent many accidents. Laws against theft are made not only to keep the thief from unjustly taking something, but to protect property as well. The welfare of the group is considered in the making of community laws, and these rules render valuable service to the people who live in the community.

When the community definitely organizes a government, it becomes an incorporated town, with a charter from the state. Those who live in towns or cities receive many services from the community.

Police and fire protection are furnished the residents of the town. Health and sanitation are safeguarded. Schools, libraries, and churches are maintained. Parks and recreation facilities are provided. 

Transportation service is usually available by street car or bus. Streets are paved or improved, and the town government provides for their up-keep and cleanliness. The town is lighted at night and traffic is regulated where it is necessary to protect people from accident. Thus, we see that each of us benefits from the services rendered by the neighborhood and community in which we live. 

The services of county, state, and nation. When 

Tom Williamson was a small boy, he liked to write his address something like this: "Tom Williamson, 1015 Washington Street, Middletown, Union County, Texas, United States of America, World." This was an unconscious recognition of the large number of groups to which Tom belonged. First, there was his family group, whose house was on Washington Street. The community was Middletown. This town was in Union County, which was a division of the State of Texas. In turn, Texas was but a part of the United States, which was a part of North America. Of course, America is but a part of the world in which we all live.

The county, the state, and the nation all render services to each individual who lives within their borders. The highways on which we travel were made possible for us by the county, state, and national government. The postman who brings

Courtesy U. S. Bureau of Public Roads 

A road to Mt. Vernon, Virginia.

Courtesy U. S. Bureau of Public Roads 

The same road after being improved by the government.

letters and new magazines to the door is a representative of the postal service provided by the national government. The foods that we buy are inspected by government officials, to see that they are pure and wholesome. Weights and measures are made standard by the national government, so that they may be the same all over the nation. 

One of the most important services of the nation is looking after the health of its citizens. The United 

States Public Health Service prevents many dis eases from entering this country from foreign lands by maintaining quarantine stations. In the same way, it prevents the spread of disease from one part of the country to another. It makes sanitary surveys, and investigates epidemics, child hygiene, industrial sanitation, and water supply. It regulates the manufacture and sale of serums and antitoxins used to prevent or cure disease. It publishes books and pamphlets giving information about health. 

In similar ways the states and counties safeguard the health of every community. Also, the state employs inspectors of factories, to see that they are sanitary, that they have safety devices where dangerous machinery is used, and that they are in proper condition. The national government has safety inspectors also. Just as the local community often has hospitals and homes for the poor and helpless, so the states provide asylums and hospitals to take care of those who cannot care for themselves. 

A very helpful service of the national government is that of the Bureau of Standards. This department

has skilled workers and scientists who devote much time to research and to the testing of products. They discover many facts which enable manufacturers to produce better goods, often at much lower costs. 

New materials for the making of many things, new uses for by-products of industry, and technical experiments as to durability and usefulness make this work invaluable to manufacturer and consumer alike.

The Department of Agriculture gives aid to agriculture and ranching. It introduces new varieties of plants into the United States. It tests soils and climates, experiments with methods of cultivation, and aids the farmers by many publications and bulletins. It surveys the livestock industry and makes studies of animal diseases. It aids in stamping out epidemics among livestock, and in developing better breeds. The Forestry Bureau protects the forests of the nation from fire and disease, and plants thousands of new trees each year. The Weather 

Bureau records wind velocity, progress of rainstorms, and other information about weather conditions, and makes forecasts based upon the observations of trained workers in every part of the United States. These are just a few of the many services of government bureaus. 

Many states maintain a police force to aid local peace officers in keeping peace and order and in tracking dangerous criminals. The national government also has officers who investigate crime and arrest criminals. Both state and nation have courts

to enforce the laws, try those who violate them, and set penalties. Not only does our nation protect us from criminals in our own land, but it maintains an army and navy to protect us and our commerce in time of war.

We have seen that each neighborhood or community has schools. But, if one should move from one community to another, one would find schools of similar classification offering almost the same subjects. This is because the state regulates and directs the schools within its borders, spending annually millions of dollars for buildings, trained teachers, and books. There are colleges and universities maintained by the state, as well as elementary schools and high schools. The national government also has schools, such as the Military 

Academy at West Point, the Naval Academy at 

Annapolis, and the Indian schools.

In the dry western parts of our nation, irrigation plants and huge dams have been constructed by the government. By this means it has brought many barren acres under cultivation, and has thus aided not only the people who live in these regions, but many others who use the products which come from the fertile irrigated fields. In the easily flooded valleys of the Mississippi and Ohio rivers, the government has built levees and is working constantly to better the control of floods. The United States has opened up rivers to navigation, dug intracoastal canals, and deepened and widened ship channels; and along the coasts, has improved harbors and built

lighthouses, beacons, and buoys to aid commerce. In many, many ways, our state and nation render service to the people who dwell beneath their flags. 

Our health, our lives, our property, are all protected. Our occupations are often rendered easier and more profitable by government investigations and aid. Transportation on excellent highways and communication by a swift and efficient postal system are possible because of our government. At work or at play, at home or abroad, the nation serves its citizens. Swimming pool and playground afford recreation to individuals and to groups. 

Summary 

People do not live alone in this world of ours. 

They are members of ever-widening groups which serve them in thousands of ways. The very young

child is a member of a family group first of all. 

There he receives his earliest training and education, his habits and his beliefs. The family group is a part of the neighborhood, and that in turn is a part of a larger community group. The communities which make up our towns and cities are parts of the states which compose the nation. Every individual is a member of each of these divisions. In addition, he comes in contact with other groups in church, school, clubs, leagues, and other organizations. Each group to which he belongs renders services to him which are valuable aids to his living, and exerts influences which determine his character, his usefulness, and his happiness throughout life.

1. Write an imaginary story about a family group, showing how each of the members benefits by the services of the family. 

2. Contrast a family of early American pioneers with a modern family. This might be done in a story or a play. 

3. Read Whittier's "Snowbound" and note the pleasures and recreations of a happy family group. 

4. Read about family life in China or some other foreign land and contrast it with life in American homes. 

5. Outline the services of the school and show how each benefits you as an individual. 

6. Write a conversation which takes place between two boys. Suppose one wants to quit school and the other is trying to persuade him to continue his attendance.

7. Name some ways in which religion has aided education, art, and literature. 

8. Give some specific examples of aid given unfortunate people by a church in your community. 

9. Report on the origin and development of Sunday schools. 

10. Make a poster or booklet showing the value of a certain club in your school. 

11. Have a Boy Scout in the class tell why he considers his membership in the Boy Scout organization of value to him. 

12. Make a report on the children's work of the Young Men's Christian Association, Young 

Women's Christian Association, or some other national organization with which you are familiar. 

13. Organize a club in the class and work out a list of objectives which you wish to accomplish. 

14. Study the work of certain civic organizations, such as the Credit Union, Community Chest, or similar bodies which may be in your community. 

15. Describe neighborhoods which are very different in their interests, as a farming region, a fishing region, and a lumbering region, and show how they are similar in the services they render. 

16. Draw cartoons or posters showing the services of the neighborhood grocer, druggist, repair man, etc. 

17. Make a list of the services you receive from your community government. 

18. Make an outline chart of the services rendered by the state or national government. 

19. Let a number of members of the class each choose a different branch of work in government service and have each one tell the class in a short talk why he would like to work in that department.

CHAPTER III HOW THE GROUP WORKS 

The necessity for control. Two boys are talking as they stand in front of the school building. “Jim, don't you see I can't give up my paper route for the summer even if it does mean that I can't go on the camping trip with you?" "Did you ask the circulation manager if some dependable boy had asked for a job?" asked Jim. "Yes, and he said. no one knows the route as I do. I must stay so I can have the job next winter." A decision was made, based upon good reasoning by the boy who had two opportunities before him. 

All of our actions are the results of decisions, guided by our minds or our wills. In this way each individual controls his own actions. Whether he acts hurriedly or only after careful thought, he acts because he has made a decision. Many things may influence his decision. Perhaps previous experience has shown him that very pleasant results may follow his action. Perhaps he is influenced by the experience of someone else. Or he may act because of the command of some other person. But in every case, his action is the result of the control of his mind over his body, and he must think and make a decision before he acts. This is a very simple example of control, and yet, just as the individual controls his 42

actions by his own decisions, so are the actions of a group controlled. 

Photo by Underwood & Underwood

The United States House of Representatives. 

We know that no individual lives all to himself. 

Therefore, no one can do just as he pleases at all times without conflicting with persons who live near him. Certain forms of control are necessary to secure coöperation within the group. In many instances, we find no organized form of government in small groups, but control exists nevertheless. The baby in the family group soon learns that there are things which he must not do. His mother directs his energies into safe and interesting channels and trains him to obedience for his own sake as well as for that

of other members of the family. As he grows older, he recognizes the authority of the parents in directing his actions and his behavior. 

It is true also that rules are needed to govern the conduct of other groups, in order to direct the actions of the individuals within the groups to serve the best interests of all. It may be that one person in a group makes all the decisions. The father in the family may control all the members without any assistance. 

Much more frequently, the father and mother may discuss problems and plans, and together may reach decisions. Perhaps older children in the family enter into the discussions, and decisions are made after all have made suggestions and criticisms. In clubs, in communities, and in state and nation, there must be leaders to make decisions and work out plans for the actions of the members of the group. 

How groups control individuals. We all recognize that rules of behavior are necessary. But perhaps we do not realize that groups control us in many other ways besides through laws governing our conduct. One of the chief forms of control lies in the opinions or beliefs which each group develops in its members. We do not feel this form of control. One of us may say, "I believe just as I please, or "I have an open mind and you will find me easy to convince." But this is seldom true. Of course, one believes as he pleases, but one's beliefs are usually the results of his contacts with others from earliest childhood. Ancestry, or family history, establishes certain attitudes and opinions within the family

group. One becomes so familiar with these beliefs from constant contact with them, that they color one's thoughts and actions whether he is conscious of them or not.

Our manners, treatment of others, and observance of rules of conduct-in fact, our whole social behavior, is controlled largely by the influence of those closely related to us in the family group. The attitudes we have toward other races and other nationalities are often results of family attitudes. 

One may hear a friend say, "Oh, I have always liked to talk to foreigners. There was such an interesting family of Italians that lived near us when I was a small child. My mother used to ask them to show us their clothes and things they brought from Italy, and they were always giving us some of their strange foods and telling us about their old home across the sea." One's whole attitude toward other nationalities may come from a friendly interest developed in such a way years before.

In similar ways, one's religious beliefs and morals are influenced by the family group, and in setting certain standards, the family exercises control of its individual members. The church also influences our thinking about life. Our attitudes toward right living and toward the welfare of others are developed in us through this training in church. 

Opinions are often influenced by a family's financial condition; for the possession of money, or the lack of it, provides different experiences. 

The neighborhood contributes its part to our

beliefs and actions, because the general culture and economic condition of the neighborhood establish its social attitudes. The talk and gossip of the neighbors influence our opinions and to some extent direct our beliefs regarding morals, recreation, choice of friends, kinds of work, and styles of dress. 

We have already discussed the influence of certain clubs and organizations as builders of character. 

They develop our interests and guide our actions, and thus exert upon us a very great amount of control.

School contacts help also in building up opinions and attitudes. Coöperation, fairness, respect for rules, care of property, and personal responsibility are developed through school training. Association with others broadens our outlook and widens our interests. School spirit develops our sense of fellowship. Perhaps we have not thought of all these principles and traits of character as controlling us. But our actions are usually results of our thoughts and beliefs, and all these things which influence our thinking become means of control. 

How group regulation affects an individual. In primitive life, the person with the greatest physical force maintained control over his associates by cunning and superior strength. Those who followed the leader obeyed through fear of his keen eyes and strong arms. This type of control developed exceptional power in one person. The others of the group were weaker and entirely dependent upon the leader. If the leader lost his life through accident

or treachery, fighting was sure to break out in the group to determine his successor, and the majority of the members were helpless in taking control, since they had had no training or experience in making decisions for themselves. 

Throughout the history of mankind, the conditions just described were true of families, tribes, and nations. The father reigned supreme in the family, and required absolute obedience from every member.

The tribal chief allowed no commands but his own to direct the actions of the tribe. The ruler of the nation was an absolute monarch. His word was law, and no one dared to think for himself, if his thinking conflicted in any way with the king's decrees. This kind of government discouraged individual ambition and made people blind followers of the leader who had set himself over them.

Control on this basis did not encourage effort, coöperation, or qualities of leadership within the group. Gradually men would come to see the unfairness of a harsh dictator, and war would follow. The unjust leader would be overthrown, and another who had promised reforms would take his place. Finally, in some nations, the people gained a few rights and privileges in making decisions for their group. 

From ancient Greece we get the words autocracy and democracy (the rule of one man as contrasted with the rule of the people). From those ancient days down to the present time, there has been constant struggle between autocracy and democracy. 

It is not our purpose here to discuss that struggle

from the viewpoint of history, but we want to see how these two forms of control affect individuals. 

We have already given a brief description of autocracy. Now let us consider democracy briefly. If the group is small, every member may have a part in discussions and decisions. Even so, there must be a leader to maintain order and direct the actions of the others. This leader may assume command through recognition of his superior ability by the others of the group, or he may be chosen by vote, by lot, or by some means of appointment. He holds. his position with the consent of the other members, not because of his own power, but because he is the majority's representative. His duty is not to dictate, but to carry out the wishes of the group as a whole. The larger the group, the less possible it is for every member to take part in its control. Therefore, it becomes necessary to select a few leaders. to carry out the wishes of the majority. The form of government described here is a representative democracy as practiced in our city, county, state, and national governments today.

Democratic groups encourage the development of ambition and initiative in order that they may have better leaders. The people within the groups do not have to be blind followers, for they may help to select their leaders and may have a voice in the decisions which control the group. Clubs and voluntary organizations are usually maintained on a democratic basis. The officers are selected by the members to carry on business for the whole group.

Superior ability and careful training mean much to the individual in this situation. 

Democracy in the home has not developed so rapidly as democracy in other groups. The tendency of allowing the younger members of the family to take part in decisions was not general in this country until recent years. Yet today, more and more, the actions of the members of the family are based upon coöperation and mutual interests rather than upon the autocratic commands of the father of the family. 

Democratic family life is much more common in America than in any other country in the world, even at the present time. The values of training in coöperation, fair play, responsibility, and self-confidence seem to be worthwhile reasons for encouraging democracy in the home.

How group rule affects the group. Securing for all the rights of freedom and happiness has been the goal of many of the most popular leaders in our history. The unfair treatment of the common man by autocratic rulers has raised just opposition. The rights of the individual must be preserved, but the welfare of the whole group must first be considered. 

To be sure, there have always been laws against criminals who injure or destroy property or who take human life. To that extent, the whole group has been protected from injury, and the acts of the individual have been restricted. In recent times, many people have seen that the group welfare is more important than individual welfare even though no criminal act is involved.

For example, let us consider the problem of unemployment. There are many people in our country today who cannot find work to do. Those who have been fortunate enough to remain employed during the long period of economic depression have helped the less fortunate members of society by contributing to the taxes which provide relief funds. In that way the government has considered the welfare of all and has made another step toward social democracy in our nation. A representative of the people at work. 

Public education is a product of group action which provides for the welfare of society as a whole. 

It has not been very many years since there were no public schools. Parents were expected to take care of the education of their children. Those who had

no children, or those whose children were already grown felt no responsibility for the training of other people's children. If their parents were unable or unwilling to educate them, that was unfortunate, perhaps, but of no concern to anyone else. Yet many of these same people who had no children of their own, or who were unwilling to assume the expense of training others, had worked hard to make this a democratic nation and to give the people a voice in the government. They began to realize that the next generation must be educated if they were to remain democratic, for those who grew up in ignorance could not govern themselves. For the welfare of the whole group, they had to educate all the children. Public schools are excellent examples of social democracy. 

Summary 

A group works in much the same way that an individual does. In either case, decisions must be made to control action. The influences which lead to the decisions may be varied but control is necessary. 

In some groups the leader acts alone; in others, all the members or selected representatives take part in plans and procedures. The more democratic the group, the more ambition and initiative are developed among its members. Equal rights and privileges are extended to everyone within the group, but individual freedom may be assured only when the welfare of the group is not endangered.

1. Make talks to the class showing how democracy works in an ideal home, in an ideal school, in a club, or in a community. 

2. Trace the development of public education in a summary outline. 

3. Read to the class the Athenian Oath and show how it sets forth the obligations of an individual in a democracy. 

4. Write a story about two people with very different home surroundings and show how they differ in opinions and beliefs. 

5. Find some information about present-day dictators, and contrast the rights and privileges of the people in their nations with those in the United States. 

6. Dramatize two scenes in homes or clubs which contrast autocratic rule and democratic rule. 

7. Make a list of services of the national government which are aids to democracy.

PART TWO INDIVIDUALS THROUGH THE AGENCY OF GROUPS PROVIDE SERVICES REQUIR- ING COOPERATIVE ACTION
