CHAPTER IV PROVIDING EDUCATION FOR OURSELVES AND OTHERS 

Why you go to school. Did you ever think why you are spending a good part of your early life in school-from six to eight hours a day, five days in the week, eight to twelve months in the year? Your fathers and mothers, acting together with other fathers and mothers in your community, build fine schools, buy modern equipment, and employ trained teachers to run the school. Stop and ask yourselves, "Why are we in school?" Let us see how well we can answer this question. Some would answer, “To learn to make a living." That is a part of the answer, but only a small part. We go to school for many reasons. Perhaps no two persons could agree entirely on why we go to school. How many aims or purposes can we think of that an education will help us to accomplish? 

1. To become citizens. In a democratic government controlled by the people, such as ours, we need to be educated in order to have a good government. 

We need persons trained to think and to examine facts, to form opinions and act upon them for the best interests of society as a whole. Through schools, the social group acting together tries to train its young people to improve on what has been done in 55

the past. Our government will be enlightened only as our people are enlightened. 

2. To enrich our lives. We go to school to develop appreciation for the good and beautiful around us. 

We learn to appreciate good music, good books, beautiful pictures, and all the beauty of nature. 

When we learn to use and appreciate the best things in life, our lives will be much richer and happier. 

3. To acquire certain skills. The basic purpose of our schools is to teach young people to read and write and acquire certain other skills. We become more skillful in drawing, painting, carving, and many other types of manual training. Did you ever think how many things you do every day that are the result of learning to read and write? 

4. To gain desired knowledge. Mankind has learned many things in the past that should be passed on to children. This knowledge about the activities of mankind in the past has been recorded in books. We have books that tell us about man's discoveries in medicine, chemistry, physics, geology, astronomy, communication, transportation, navigation, mechanics, agriculture, government, inventions, and all of his other activities. By building our education upon our knowledge of the past, we are able to progress more rapidly. 

5. To prepare to make a better living. It is a well-known fact that an educated person has a better chance to be successful in life, in a material way, than an illiterate one. Many of the business con-

cerns today are requiring an applicant to have at least a high school education. Professional men, such as doctors, lawyers, ministers, and teachers, cannot qualify for their life-work without college or university training. The trend today is toward requiring more education for all of the better positions. 

6. To achieve desirable attitudes. Our schoolwork should develop desirable attitudes toward everything that may make our lives happier and more complete. We are living in groups. Our activities are mainly coöperative activities. Do we have the proper attitude toward our group? How do we regard work and play? How do we feel toward other races of people? What is our attitude toward selfishness and wrongdoing? Are we enthusiastic about doing our best at all times? Are we learning to put into practice the Golden Rule?

These reasons for going to school comprise only a partial list. How many aims and purposes can you think of that show why boys and girls should be in school? Ask your parents and your teacher to make suggestions and then make as long a list as possible. 

Being a desirable citizen in school. The Constitution of the United States defines a citizen as any person born or naturalized in the United States. You are a citizen when you are born. There are two kinds of citizens, good citizens and bad citizens. An undesirable school citizen is always a disturbing influence in the group. Use the following list in checking up on yourself to see what kind of school citizen you are.

A good school citizen will 

1. attend school every day possible 

2. always give his best effort in the classroom 

3. play hard when he plays 

4. hand his written work in on time 

5. not idle over his lessons 

6. concentrate on what he is doing 

7. be attentive during recitation 

8. do his own work and will not help others cheat 

9. respect the rights of others 

10. be obedient to his parents and teachers 

11. be responsible for school property and help to keep his school surroundings clean and beautiful 12. take part in school activities, such as debate, declamation, chapel programs, athletics, musical organizations, the school paper, and glee clubs 13. take pride in his school, and try at all times to uphold its good name 14. not give up because a task is hard. 

Obligations of the school. The boys and girls of 

America, if they are good citizens and do their part as outlined in the preceding topic, can expect our schools to do certain things for them. Some of the obligations of the school are as follows: 

1. The building and grounds should be safe and healthful. The state governments should set up safety standards in building construction and provide for frequent inspection in order to eliminate dangerous hazards to life and health. "Eternal vigilance is the price of safety." 

2. The school yard and school building should be neat and well-kept. How can one learn to appreciate

the beautiful and good when in ugly surroundings? 

3. The school is partly responsible for your health.

You need exercise in order to be healthy. You need relaxation from your studies at regular intervals. 

4. The school should teach you how to live harmoniously in the society of today. When you graduate from school you should know something about the conditions that confront you in your community, your state, your nation, and the world. You are living now. You should know what is happening today.

You should think and have opinions of the presentday world. Its policies in the future will be for you to determine. 

5. You should have the best teachers your community can afford-teachers interested in their profession and in your welfare, teachers who have a sympathetic understanding of your needs, teachers in whom you have confidence.

Governing schools. Our schools are governed in different ways. School government is principally an affair of the state. There are no uniform ways of setting up school governments in the forty-eight states that make up the Federal Union. The head of the public schools in the state is the State Superintendent or Commissioner. He has general authority in enforcing the laws of the state legislature concerning our public schools. He also keeps the state legislature informed of the needs of the schools. He assists in making plans for the schools, provides means of supervision, and advises with school officials.

To a certain extent, each school community attends

A small school. A large school.

to its school problems in its own way. In each district or community, a board, elected by the qualified voters, looks after the school's business affairs. This board usually elects the superintendent, the principals, and the teachers. It determines the policy of the school, and provides for its growth and for its general welfare. It usually leaves the actual conduct of affairs to the superintendent.

The superintendent as a rule recommends the selection of teachers, places teachers in the system so they can do their best work, attends to the buying of materials, supervises the maintenance of buildings and grounds, and has general control of all policies concerning the school.

Paying for public education. The maintaining of the public educational institutions in the United States is a big business. Approximately one out of every four persons is in school. In 1935, it was estimated that there were 6,700,000 high school students alone. It takes a large amount of money to operate school systems; the construction and equipment of buildings, teachers' salaries, books, supplies, fuel, and repairs, head the list of the expenditures in firstclass schools. These range from an average of $25.11 in the southern states to $141.43 in the state of New 

York. Where does all of this money come from?

It is collected in the form of taxes of various kinds. 

The local tax pays the biggest part of our educational bill in most communities.

Depressions affect our schools. Probably no other institution in the United States was so seriously af-

fected by the depression of 1929 as our public schools. 

It hurt them in several ways: 

1. Thousands of rural schools had to close their doors because the people in some states could not pay their taxes. In many other places, both city and country, teachers were not paid for months. 

2. The educational services of the school were retarded. Libraries were not kept up-to-date with new books and many established classes such as music, physical education, art, and industrial work were discontinued. Many schools combined classes and overburdened their teachers with more work than they could do well. 

3. School enrollments grew rapidly during the depression. Especially was this true in the high schools. There were probably two reasons for this. 

One was unemployment, and the other was the fact that there had arisen a demand for high school graduation for all. The situation of educating more students with less money confronted us. 

4. To the extremely poor economic situation was added the unemployment of thousands of teachersunemployed not because of rendering poor service, but because school communities did not have the money to pay them. 

5. The schools were forced to adopt a defensive attitude and program. Enemies of the public school system seized the opportunity to destroy or hamper school activities. The principle that people should pay taxes to educate other people's children was

debated. Some thought that junior and senior high schools should not be maintained with tax money. 

Others thought a five or six months' school term was enough. Many people advocated the elimination of all the "fads and frills" in education, designating as such, music, physical education, home economics, athletics, and industrial arts; they preferred the teaching of the old traditional "three R's" rather than courses that had been started in our schools because of the growing need to teach our boys and girls how to live in a modern society. It is the belief of modern educators that it is best for the school to offer a variety of experiences so that the student may learn to live more effectively.

All of these effects of the depression make conclusive the fact that public education was retarded by many, many years, and that countless children were deprived of the opportunity to secure an adequate education. Education is something that we cannot wait to secure. If we do not receive a formal education when we are young, the chances are we shall never acquire one. We can postpone to a more favorable time highway and street construction, the building of naval ships and army airplanes, or the pursuit of recreational activities; but we cannot postpone the education of a child without placing a penalty upon both the child and society as a whole. 

The New Deal in education. In the period 19331935, the financial situation in our schools became better. For one thing, improved economic conditions increased tax collections. The federal government,

Two types of school classrooms.

through the Works Progress Administration and Civil Works Administration, spent over $182,000,000 in constructing and repairing school buildings. It also appropriated $75,000,000 in 1937 for the National Youth Administration to help needy boys and girls between the ages of sixteen and twenty-five to continue their education. Added to this, there has been a recent movement to revise the methods of teaching, so that the youth of America may have a greater opportunity than ever before of learning how to live in a modern world. For this reason, schools are undertaking to educate our young people to spend their leisure time profitably. To combat crime, character education is being advocated. To understand the modern society we live in, social studies have been expanded, and more emphasis is being placed on the study of modern economic, social, and governmental problems. Perhaps in the near future we may see a lengthening of the period of education. The junior college maintained in some of our cities is a step in that direction. For the many who failed to secure an adequate education when they were young, the adult education movement is fulfilling a real need.

1. Write a paper on "Why I Go to School." 

2. Make a list of the acts of a bad school citizen. 

3. Ask your father and mother to describe the schools they attended. Report to the class and compare their schools with the one you are attending.

4. Find out just what your community is spending on education and report to the class. 

5. Make a list of the courses you would like to see added to your school's curriculum. 

6. Compare the cost of education in the United

States with what we spend for motion pictures, sports, cigarettes, cosmetics, and gasoline. 

7. Debate the question: Resolved: That all schools should have athletic teams. 

8. Make a list of the most valuable courses offered in your school. Be able to defend your position. 

9. Make a floor talk on "The Effect of the Depression on Our Schools." 

10. Make a report to the class on "School Life One 

Hundred Years Ago." 

11. Make a survey of your school grounds and report to your class what is needed.

CHAPTER V KEEPING OURSELVES AND OTHERS HEALTHY 

Our changing ideas about disease. We have not always known as much about the causes and treatment of diseases as we know today. In ancient times it was believed that diseases were caused by demons that infested the bodies of the sick. The priests and medicine men were the doctors. There are some such superstitious people today who have similar beliefs and use somewhat the same methods in treating the sick.

During the Middle Ages, barbers did most of the surgical work and the principal remedy prescribed for diseases was bleeding. This remedy consisted of cutting a vein, drawing off what they considered impure blood, and filling the patient with an equal amount of water. George Washington, called the 

Father of Our Country, was bled twice while suffering from pneumonia that resulted in his death. 

This treatment weakened him in his fight against the disease. 

How long do we live? You have a better chance of living your allotted seventy years than your forefathers had because more is known about the causes, prevention, and treatment of diseases than was known only a few years ago. Three hundred years ago the average span of life was only twenty years. 67

Courtesy du Pont Company

A research laboratory. 

Sixty years ago the average span was forty years.

Now, due to the efforts of scientists working in obscure laboratories and to the watchful eyes of physicians and public health officials, each baby that is born has a very good chance of living from fifty-five to sixty years. This gradual increase in the average life span corresponds directly to the growth in education, and the general spread of knowledge throughout the country.

Some great health workers. We should study the lives of some of those great men who have made it possible for us to live so much longer. Mankind owes them a great debt. Dr. Crawford W. Long, of 

Georgia, in 1842, was the first physician to make use of an anesthetic to prevent pain during an operation. This made it possible for the patient to sleep

while undergoing an operation. Appendicitis would still be a fatal disease had not this discovery been made. Two American dentists, Dr. Horace Wells, in 1844, and Dr. W. T. G. Morton, in 1846, first used anesthetics in dental work. Louis Pasteur, the great French scientist, found that diseases are caused by tiny germs, so small that one must use a powerful microscope to see them. He taught us how to pasteurize milk, and how to treat a person bitten by a rabid dog. Through his work we have learned that one diseased person may transmit a disease to many others. 

Joseph Lister, an English surgeon, discovered that few of his patients died when he used carbolic acid in connection with his operations. From this discovery modern antiseptic methods have been developed. Walter Reed, an American, learned that yellow fever is transmitted by a mosquito. This discovery made possible the digging of the Panama Canal by promoting more healthful living conditions in the Canal Zone. Many of the early scientists died from the very diseases they were trying to conquer. They were indeed heroes. 

The advantages of being well. There are two great advantages of being well and free from sickness at all times. In the first place, we derive more satisfaction from living if we are free from pain and suffering; in the second place, sickness is very costly both in time and money to ourselves and to society. 

It is estimated that on any one day in the year there are from 2,000,000 to 3,000,000 people in the

United States ill from preventable diseases. These sick people must lose time from their work and they must also pay for medical care. The total loss represented by sickness in this country is about $2,000,- 000,000 a year. That is about $16 for each one of us. Yet, the pain, suffering, and worry make us more unhappy than the cost in dollars. All of us should assist the public health officials in their efforts to conquer the enemies of health.

The spread of diseases. If we are to escape the enemies of health, we must learn how diseases are spread in order to find some method of conquering them. Truly, "an ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure." Modern prevention of disease is said to have started with the publishing of Pasteur's germ theory in 1877. Since that time slightly less than one hundred different germs have been discovered that are responsible for most of our diseases. 

If we could keep these germs from getting into our bodies, we should not have these diseases. Since we cannot see the germs which cause our sickness, we must know where they come from and how they enter our bodies in order to close the gates through which they enter. 

Where disease germs come from and how they enter our bodies. There are many kinds of germs. 

They grow in different places and enter our bodies in various ways. The germs which cause diphtheria, whooping cough, measles, colds, and scarlet fever grow in the mucous membranes of our nasal and throat passages. These germs are thrown out into

the air by coughing and sneezing. Typhoid germs thrive in milk, water, and food; they enter our bodies through our mouths. Malarial fever germs are placed in our bodies by the bite of a mosquito which breeds in swampy places. The germs which cause lockjaw or tetanus enter our bodies through cuts or breaks in the skin. Most germs, however, enter the body through the mouth or nose. For this reason, the public health officials are very careful in inspecting the foods and liquids which we eat and drink.

Preventing the spread of diseases. Since science has made available much information concerning germs that cause sickness, it is the responsibility of individuals to prevent the spreading of diseases. 

In order to do this, each infected person must realize a responsibility for doing his part. But individuals alone cannot conquer disease germs. Our government, working through its health officers, must help in order that we may win the fight. What are some of the things which our government does to help in keeping disease germs from spreading? It quarantines people who have certain contagious diseases. 

It disinfects houses and places where dangerous germs are located. It tries to eliminate disease carriers, such as mosquitoes, flies, and rats. It sets up rules to prevent diseased people from handling the food we eat and drink. In many localities compulsory vaccination is required so that the people will be immune from such diseases as smallpox and typhoid fever. Serums and vaccines have been so

developed that today smallpox, diphtheria, typhoid, rabies, tetanus, and perhaps scarlet fever and measles, can be completely eliminated. Even certain types of the dreaded disease, pneumonia, can be treated by the use of a serum. 

Caring for the sick. The state, in a modern health program, not only attempts to prevent the spread of disease, but sometimes finds that it must take care of sick people. Whenever a diseased person becomes a danger to others near him, or when a sick person cannot afford medical care, the state will care for him while sick. Many states maintain sanatoriums where people who have tuberculosis may receive treatment. The United States government maintains a leper hospital in Louisiana where those who are afflicted with leprosy must go for treatment. All progressive counties and cities have some place where people with certain diseases may go for treatment. 

By placing diseased persons in hospitals or under the care of physicians, the cities have found that the spread of disease can be lessened, and often entirely prevented.

Results of our fight against disease. Many of the diseases that formerly caused high death rates have been almost conquered. Leprosy is no longer the prevalent disease it was in ancient times. In New 

York City, 401 persons of every 100,000 died of tuberculosis in 1871. Fifty years later only 89 persons in 100,000 died of that disease. Today, tuberculosis is successfully treated by the simple remedy of complete rest, good food, and fresh air, and as a

result, the death rate is now down to 70 for every 100,000 people in the United States. Smallpox killed 

124 per 100,000 population in New York City in 1872. From 1917 to 1926, not a single person in that city died from smallpox. Between 1900 and 1920 the death rate from diphtheria in the United States was reduced from 43 to 15 for every 100,000 persons, and today it is below 5. These and other health gains have meant the saving of over 700,000 lives a year in the United States, which shows what a nation with a capable health service can do to protect us from contagious diseases.

Making it easier to be healthy. The national, state, and local governments employ hundreds of people who spend their time finding ways to make it easier for us to be healthy. The United States Public 

Health Service spends much of its time in learning the sources and habits of the various disease germs. 

These germs are kept in small glass bottles and strengthened with their favorite foods. After they multiply and become strong, the laboratory experts find out what will kill them. In this way serums and antitoxins are discovered and perfected.

The federal government works to prevent the shipping, from one state to another, of foods that contain harmful preparations that are unfit for people to eat. To do this special work, the government maintains inspectors at packing houses, canning factories, and other places where food is prepared. 

These inspectors often have to destroy food because it would be injurious to health. During 1939, the

government planned to spend more than $20,000,000 in its efforts to keep us well. 

The state, county, and city governments all cooperate with the federal government and with each other in giving us a public health program. Some health problems affect only the people of one city or community. Other problems-for example, contagious diseases-may affect the people of a whole state or possibly the whole nation. Hence, on account of constant travel, the health of the people in one city might depend upon the alertness of the health officials in distant cities or states. If the health officials in one state or city find a new way to control a disease, the health officials in other states should learn this new method. For these reasons, city, state, and federal health officers work together in solving their health problems. 

Protection of food and drink. Public health officials supervise the supply of much of the food we eat and the liquids we drink. They inspect the animals to be slaughtered for food and forbid the sale of those in a diseased condition. The government also forbids the shipping of diseased animals and food that contain germs. They inspect our food markets and require the owners to keep the food free from filth. Foods are examined for their purity, and the sale of impure food is forbidden. Some states require those who handle foods to pass periodic health tests to see if they are infected with a communicable disease.

Since disease germs grow so rapidly in milk, our

public health officers pay very close attention to our dairies and those places where milk is sold. Milk is graded according to certain standards of purity. 

The seller is required to mark the bottle so the buyer will know what grade or standard he is buying.

Since cows sometimes have tuberculosis or other diseases, dairy cattle are inspected; when they are found diseased the sale of their milk is forbidden. 

Courtesy International Harvester Company

A modern farm kitchen. 

The water supply of a city must be closely watched, since disease germs often live in water. Drinking water for larger cities is usually taken from rivers or lakes, often located many miles away. This surface water is filled with germs and bacteria injurious to health. To remove the germs, the water is run through a purifying plant. Health officers make

regular tests to determine whether the water is safe for the people to drink. If something injurious to health is found, further studies are made to find the source of the germs and to determine what can be done to purify the water. The state health departments have done much toward helping the people in rural areas to have pure drinking water. They show them how to protect their water supplies and how to test the purity of the water. Despite all the efforts of public health officials, many cases of typhoid are still traced to drinking water from polluted surface wells, springs, and small streams. 

Protection against health nuisances. Piles of trash and tin cans, weeds on vacant lots, stagnant water pools, and outdoor sewage disposal often become health hazards. This is because germs and germ carriers, such as rats, flies, and mosquitoes, live and grow in such places. Pig pens and cow lots become health nuisances if they are not keep clean. Pools of water and tin cans filled with water serve as breeding places for mosquitoes. Since many people are careless about keeping their premises sanitary, it becomes necessary for the city and state to make rules forbidding health hazards. Our health officials investigate and correct unsanitary conditions in alleys, vacant lots, and other places. Oil is poured on pools of water to prevent the breeding of mosquitoes. 

In most towns it is against the law to spit on the sidewalk or in public buildings. The use of common drinking cups and common towels is also forbidden. 

Protection against contagious diseases. Public

health officers should be notified when anyone in the community becomes ill with a contagious disease. If the disease is a highly contagious one, such as smallpox, the health authorities will declare a quarantine.

Spread of diseases is prevented in many instances by requiring the use of vaccines and serums. In many places, children must be vaccinated for smallpox before entering school.

Some cities maintain hospitals where people with contagious diseases may be isolated and given expert medical attention. Many people are now advocating some form of socialized medicine. This was the main and most interesting topic of discussion at the annual meeting of the American Medical Association in 1938. 

Protecting our health through housing regulations. Nearly all kinds of disease germs live and thrive in rooms and houses where there is a lack of light and fresh air. For this reason, health officials are interested in the building codes adopted by the cities. In the larger cities, the tall buildings must be constructed in such a way as to permit the sunlight to reach the streets. Sleeping rooms and bath rooms must be well ventilated. Proper sanitary appliances must be used in connection with the water supply and sewage disposal. The government is advocating the destruction of unsanitary and unhealthful tenement houses, and is encouraging the building of modern, well-ventilated houses. 

Protecting our health through education. Public schools furnish a good illustration of what the state

GOOD HEALTH HABITS REQUIRE THAT YOU WASH YOUR HANDS BEFORE YOU LEAVE THIS ROOM 

Cleanliness. and community are doing to help us live healthier lives. The school serves as a health agency. In many of the modern school systems a daily examination is given, and children suffering from severe colds or

fever are sent home. Periodic examinations of the teeth, eyes, nose, heart, and lungs are made in order to detect physical defects and to help the pupils correct them. Pupils are taught how to care for their bodies and how to keep themselves and others healthy.

There are other agencies that are educating our young people in the ways of healthier living. Some of these agencies are the Boy and Girl Scouts, the Camp Fire Girls, and the publications of the public health departments. Some state health departments furnish the schools with phonograph records on which are recorded health lessons attractively presented. 

Protecting ourselves through licenses. Certain standards are set up by the state for those who practice medicine or for those who care for the bodies of others. Before anyone can be a physician, a nurse, or a dentist, he must secure a license from the state. Some states maintain medical schools where students are trained to be skilled doctors, surgeons, and nurses. Barbers and beauty parlor operators are required to have licenses and to undergo periodic health examinations. These people would be especially dangerous if they should work while infected with a contagious or transmissible disease. 

Protecting ourselves through vital statistics. One of the duties of a health department is the keeping of records of the births, deaths, and diseases of the people of the state or community. This information not only tells the public officials about health conditions existing in the community, but it makes pos-

sible the setting up of proper safeguards to prevent epidemics. If the doctors in a certain community should report several cases of typhoid fever to the health department, a search would be started immediately to determine the source of the disease. If it should be found that all those afflicted with the fever 

An operating room. secured their milk from the same dairy, the dairy and all those working in it would be carefully examined. By this method the source of the disease would be located. If the vital statistics should show that more people are dying in a city of a certain population than are dying in other cities of about the same size, the health department would try to determine

the cause. Health statistics keep our officials constantly on the lookout for means of improving the health conditions of the community. 

Preventing the entrance of diseases from foreign countries. Public health officials watch the shores and borders of the United States to keep out all diseases from foreign countries. Were it not for the unceasing watchfulness of these officers, immigrants and sailors might bring into this country deadly diseases which would kill great numbers of our people. The five great plague diseases-cholera, typhus, yellow fever, bubonic plague, and smallpoxhave been almost eliminated. If one person afflicted with any of these diseases should pass the quarantine officers, he could start an epidemic of sickness and suffering. So well do these health officers do their work that not one case in a dozen years escapes them to injure the American people. 

Our personal responsibility. After studying what is being done by the public officials to make our communities more healthful places in which to live, let us see what our part in the health work should be. Each person should learn and practice the rules of health taught in the schools, in the girls' and boys' camps, in the publications of the health departments, and in the laws and ordinances laid down by the state and city. We should coöperate with the public health authorities in their efforts to make the community more healthful. We should help to keep our houses, yards, and streets clean and sanitary. We should keep water from collecting in cans and should

destroy the breeding places of flies. We should stay at home when we have a contagious disease. We should avoid spitting in public places. We should use a handkerchief when sneezing. We should report to the local health authorities the existence of any health hazard in our neighborhood. If we will do our part and encourage the health officials to do theirs, we shall live longer and happier lives.

1. Make a list of all the old superstitions you can find about diseases and the treatment of diseases. 

2. Make a report on how the Indians treated diseases. 

3. Give an oral report on the life and work of each of the following: Louis Pasteur, Joseph Lister, Walter Reed, W. T. G. Morton, Horace Wells. 

4. Make a chart, showing how many diseases are transmitted and the best way of preventing their spread. 

5. Debate: Resolved: That all children should be vaccinated against smallpox before being allowed to enter a public school. 

6. Find out from your local health officer the rules governing quarantine in your state. Make a report to the class. 

7. Make a survey of your school premises and make a report to the class concerning health hazards found. 

8. Make a survey of your community and prepare a chart showing such health hazards as stagnant pools where mosquitoes might hatch, places where flies might breed, piles of cans, etc.

10. Make a trip to your water department and see what your community is doing to guarantee you a pure water supply.

12. Make a written list of all the rules of hygiene you should observe to help protect the public health. 

13. Make a list of other health rules followed in your home. 

14. What is meant by "common towel" and "common drinking cup"?

CHAPTER VI PROVIDING BEAUTY FOR OURSELVES AND OTHERS 

Our love of beauty. Everyone enjoys looking at beautiful scenery, pictures, or buildings. The famous waterfalls at Niagara draw thousands of visitors each year to view the beauties of the rushing water, foaming spray, and magnificent gorge. The majestic peaks and massive walls of the Grand Canyon of the Colorado attract people from all over the world. The snow-capped Alps of Switzerland, the fiorded coastline of Norway, the volcanic wonders of Hawaii, and many other famous beauty spots the world over are visited yearly by eager and interested tourists who enjoy the grandeur of nature. Art galleries are maintained to preserve beautiful paintings and sculpture. 

Architects have constructed buildings of such beauty that they also attract visitors, just as beautiful natural scenery does.

People often travel long distances to view beautiful landscapes, or visit famous art galleries, cathedrals, and monuments. Sometimes they do not realize that there are things of beauty much nearer home. 

Other people who cannot travel to distant places may gratify their love of beauty by careful planning of their home surroundings. A few attractive pictures on the walls, some growing plants at the windows, orderly and convenient arrangement of fur- 84

Providing Beauty for Ourselves and Others 85 

nishings, may make even simple homes beautiful. 

Not only the interior of the home may be made more pleasing to the eye, but the lawn and garden may be made attractive by simple landscaping. 

Even very young babies have a love for beauty which remains with them all their lives. They find pleasure in everyday objects and scenes. They express delight over bright colors and shining toys.

They enjoy looking at the trees, the flowers, the moon, and the stars. This response to beauty which is a part of us should be directed and developed so that we may have a deeper appreciation of familiar things as well as recognition of the new and unusual. 

Beauty in the community. Outside the home it is more difficult for the individual to make his surroundings beautiful, but with the help of his neighbors, the community may be much improved. Most 

American communities grew up without plans. 

Pioneers had little time to consider beauty. They wanted a place to work, a place to sleep, and land to provide necessities. Even if they did make plans, they could not foresee present-day conditions. They did not know that cities would grow so large or that modern machinery would bring about so many problems of factories, housing, and traffic. Houses were built and streets laid out without much thought of future needs. Later, factories were built without regard for residential districts. Factory workers lived in dingy houses near tall smokestacks and facing narrow, crowded streets. Beautiful river banks or seashores became factory sites or shipping

Beauty in the community.

Providing Beauty for Ourselves and Others 87 

wharves, and the natural scenery of the communities was marred. 

People soon began to realize the value of beauty in their surroundings. They believed that streets should be clean and well paved, that residences should not be fouled or darkened by soot from factories, and that there should be beautiful public buildings to serve the whole city. After many years of replanning to correct old mistakes, and planning more carefully for things of the future, we see the United States today becoming a country of beauty. 

There are many ways in which neighborhoods may improve the beauty of their own districts. 

Lawns, sidewalks, and parks may be kept clean. 

Grass, shrubbery, and trees may be planted to beautify unsightly spots. Junk-yards and other unattractive places may be walled in to present a more attractive appearance. Billboards may be banned from the roadside, and streets and vacant lots kept clean. 

Every person in the community should feel the same responsibility for the appearance of the neighborhood that he does for his own home. He should feel pride in the parks and public buildings, and in helping to maintain the beauty of the whole district. 

City planning. Although most of our cities grew without a plan, there are many opportunities at the present time for planning future growth and for remodeling unsightly, inconvenient, and unhealthful districts in cities. Many cities have planning commissions to study the needs of the community and to provide for future growth. Interest in city plan-

ning has been increased by a realization of the difficulties brought about by heavy traffic, huge factories, and poor housing conditions. People have a better knowledge of health and sanitation, and realize the necessity for relieving crowded conditions. 

The city should be divided into zones, with residential, manufacturing, and commercial districts. 

Transportation terminals, including railroad stations, bus stations, and airports, should be located where traffic is not easily congested. 

Highways should lead into the city on broad, straight streets, so that travelers can find their way without difficulty. Narrow streets should be widened.

Public buildings, such as the city hall, post office, auditorium, and library, should be in convenient groups. Parks, public squares, and playgrounds should be provided.

Natural beauties of the city should be restored, and made accessible to the citizens. An example of this may be seen in the lake front at Chicago. Land, once occupied by factories and shipping interests, has been reclaimed today and made into park areas for the people of the city. 

Examples of city planning. Citizens of the United

States point with pride to the beauties of their national capital. Before it was built, Washington was planned by a distinguished Frenchman, Major

L'Enfant, who came to America with Lafayette to fight in the Revolutionary War. Appointed by President Washington to plan the capital city, L'Enfant visited famous cities of Europe, and conferred with

Photo by Underwood & Underwood 

Washington, D. C., a well-planned city.

several great American leaders before he submitted the plan which later was carried out in almost every respect. With its wide streets, beautiful parks, stately buildings, and magnificent monuments, Washington is one of the most beautiful cities in the world. 

Chicago has spent millions of dollars to cut broad avenues and boulevards through the city; to improve its harbor; to fill in the lake shore and create parks and bathing beaches; and to build museums, art galleries, a city stadium, and a civic center. 

New York has recently built some model tenement houses, with careful attention to heating, lighting, and sanitation. There are thousands of people still living in ugly and unhealthful slums, but a beginning has been made to remedy these wretched conditions. 

Cleveland, Pittsburgh, Philadelphia, Detroit, Los

Angeles, Salt Lake City, and other large cities have made extensive plans for improving conditions and for beautification. Smaller towns and cities have planning commissions and have done many things to make their communities more beautiful. 

Rural planning. Many rural districts are being encouraged by local organizations or county and state directors to clean up and beautify farm homes and small towns. The federal government recently has 'tried some experiments in rural housing projects. It. also has encouraged clean fields and pastures, and strong and well-kept barns and fences. 

In these days of rapid travel, highways are necessary links to bind our country together. Millions

Providing Beauty for Ourselves and Others 91 

A farm home. of dollars are spent annually for the construction and maintenance of roads. So rapidly have these highways been built, little has been done to beautify them. In fact, the countless billboards, hot-dog stands, unattractive gasoline stations, and other commercial interests have destroyed the natural beauty along the highways. In some parts of the country, public opinion has been so aroused by the unattractiveness of the roadsides that campaigns have been organized to make them cleaner and more inviting. 

Recently, state highway departments have become interested in landscaping the roadsides. There are several reasons for this. One, of course, is to increase beauty and convenience. Another is to decrease driving hazards and make the highways safer. Roadside parks have been constructed where travelers may

rest and break the monotony of a long trip. Edges of curves have been planted with shrubs so that the driver may see at a distance that the highway turns. 

Grass and shrubs are being set out on embankments to prevent soil erosion. Intersections and side roads are marked by trees and shrubs. In all these ways the highways are made safer as well as more attractive.

National parks and forests. The government of the United States has set aside certain areas and beauty spots in the country for the use and enjoyment of the people of the nation. To prevent the rapid destruction of the forests and to help control floods and soil erosion, national forests are reserved under government control. People may travel through them and enjoy their beauty, but the trees must not be injured or cut, and wild animals and birds must not be hunted or disturbed.

National parks are located in regions of great natural beauty, scenic wonder, or healthful climate.

Some of the best-known parks are the Yellowstone National Park, which lies in Wyoming, Montana, and Idaho, where the famous hot springs and geysers are located; the Rocky Mountain National Park in the heart of the Colorado mountains; the Yosemite

National Park in California, famous for its high waterfalls and big trees; and Crater Lake Park in 

Oregon, where a beautiful blue lake nestles in the giant crater of an extinct volcano. Other national parks include the Grand Canyon of the Colorado, Bryce Canyon in Utah, Glacier Park in Montana,

Providing Beauty for Ourselves and Others 93 

Hot Springs in Arkansas, Mesa Verde in Colorado, and Mount McKinley Park in Alaska. 

Photo by National Park Service 

Hot Springs National Park, Arkansas.

Many states also have set aside park areas. Famous battlefields have been made into beautiful parks. 

Regions with healthful mineral waters have been protected and reserved for the use and enjoyment of the citizens of the nation. Canyons and mountains, lakes and deserts, forests and seashores, have been made accessible to the people by states and the nation. Modern American people have so many beautiful places to improve, preserve, visit, and enjoy, that it is easy to understand why "See America First" is a popular slogan.
