CHAPTER VII PROVIDING RECREATION FOR OURSELVES AND OTHERS 

The recreation of our forefathers. It is as natural for human beings to play as it is for them to eat and sleep. People in all ages and in all places have used much of their time in play. The peasantry developed their folk dances to give expression to their playful moods. From the earliest times people have expressed their feelings in dancing. When man learned to grow grain and tame animals for his use, he did not have much leisure time. Most of the people had to work, hunt, and fish to make a living. There existed even then a small number whose living was made by others and whose life was filled with much leisure. This was during the ancient and medieval period of our history. In Rome, during this time, great crowds attended the public baths, hippodromes, and amphitheaters. They attended chariot races as well as human and animal fights. 

In the Middle Ages, our forefathers in England spent their spare time in hunting with trained hawks. 

This sport was called falconry. They also held tournaments in which the knights jousted and fought with one another. Nomads and savages in olden times raced their horses or their canoes.

In colonial times in America, a great part of the people lived out-of-doors, close to nature. Their 95

most important leisure-time activities were quiltingbees, log-rollings, husking-bees, and matching their skill against one another in rifle-shooting contests. 

Hunting and fishing supplied a large portion of their food. Their outdoor life took care of their need for recreation.

The invention of power machinery caused an increase in leisure time. Man began to work regular hours. Machinery allowed him to make his goods so rapidly that he did not have to work long hours to produce the things he wanted. For this reason he began to have more and more leisure time. This was especially true of those who lived in cities and towns. Today, one of the great problems is how to spend wisely the greater leisure time which has steadily increased with the use of machines and other inventions of civilization. 

Our need for play. Most of our forefathers lived in the country or in small villages. People at that time followed occupations that required physical work, and kept them out-of-doors at least a part of the time. The woods and fields were near by for the children to use as playgrounds, and there was plenty of play room. Today, over half of our people are living in cities. Their occupations do not usually require vigorous exercise. Those who work in factories, offices, and stores, doing the same thing day after day, soon use up their energy and become dull unless they have an opportunity to play during their leisure hours. Several things may be done to restore our energy and enthusiasm. We must eat the proper

food and secure enough sleep. We must devote a part of our time to wholesome physical exercise, play, or entertainment. Because so many people live in crowded cities, it would be impossible for each person to provide himself with the necessary means Enjoyable recreation provides entertainment for the group. of recreation. This makes play a social problem to be handled by organized society for the benefit of all. To understand what society is doing to solve the problem of recreation, we need to know what our national government, state governments, and local communities are doing to provide recreational facilities for all the people. 

The costs of recreation. Our municipal, state, and federal governments are spending nearly $200,000,- 000 per year to provide recreation for the citizens. of the United States. In addition, the American people spend about $2,500,000,000 each year on commer-

cial amusements, such as motion pictures, radios, theaters, and clubs. We spend about $380,000,000 for luncheon and social clubs. Our total bill for games, sports, and outdoor life amounts to $880,000,- 000. Even our fishermen spent about $150,000,000 in 1938. So you see, we are spending a large part of our annual income for recreation. 

What the national government is doing to provide recreation. The national government has set aside large tracts of land to be used as parks. The national park system now includes a total area of 15,000,000 acres. Roads leading to them have been built and camp sites and hotels have been provided. In 1910, 199,000 people visited these parks; and in 1938, this number grew to nearly 4,000,000. Of course, the automobiles, trailers, and good highways have made this possible.

The national government has also set aside 130,- 000,000 acres as national forests. The Civilian Conservation Corps have been employed to make more available the attractions of the national forests. The members of the C. C. C. camps are removing fire hazards, building bridges, roads, and trails, clearing underbrush, and otherwise helping to make possible a greater use and enjoyment of these areas of beauty. 

The national government has assisted the states in building better highway systems. Good roads and improved automobiles have brought about a great increase in highway travel. We can now travel long distances at small cost. From three to four hundred miles is an average day's travel. Many cities have

provided places where the automobile traveler can camp at small expense. It is said the American people spend their summers on the highways. Perhaps in a few years we may be able to go from California to Alaska on one continuous good road. We may soon have a good road to the southern tip of South America.

What our states are doing to provide recreation. 

Many of our states are building state parks for use in camping, hunting, and fishing. Roadside parks have been built for the traveler to use. Twenty-one states now have laws that allow communities to provide at public expense such things as parks, swimming pools, and concerts. States also have passed laws to allow local communities to vote bonds to pay for playgrounds. Some states have laws that allow cities to compel real estate dealers to designate parks and playgrounds in developing new additions. Many states have passed laws regulating hunting and fishing, thus conserving our wild life. Some of them have laws that make compulsory the teaching of physical education in their public schools. Among other benefits, children in physical education classes learn how to play organized games. 

Recreation in our cities. It is in our cities that we find our biggest problems in recreation. Places for recreation must be provided for a large part of the city population. While adults, as well as children, must have means of wholesome recreation, the recreational problems of children present the greatest difficulties. City officials are beginning to realize

that playgrounds must be provided or streets and alleys will be used for play. We are making a real effort to meet the problem. 

Organized play had its beginnings on the school yard. Modern towns now have parks with playgrounds where play is supervised. The school yard is still used in many places as the community play center. Nearly all cities, both large and small, now maintain some kind of municipal playground. In 

Play on the school ground. 

1878, Boston opened the first organized playground. 

The city furnishes also playground equipment and supervisors who regulate its use. The supervisor organizes and umpires games, prepares schedules,

discourages loafing and insolence, coöperates with doctors and school authorities, and administers first aid.

The kinds of recreation to be found in a modern community. In addition to its parks and playgrounds, a modern community provides for many other kinds of recreation. Let us study some of them in order to see what a wide choice we may have in selecting our means of recreation. 

1. Games and contests. Originally, parks furnished a place for people to stroll during their leisure time and to enjoy nature, feed the squirrels, and rest on the park benches. The modern public park provides all the old attractions and in addition a place for various sports and games. Swimming pools are provided. Tennis courts are found in great numbers. Baseball diamonds have been added.

Indoor baseball played out-of-doors is becoming more popular. This game does not require so much skill or equipment as does baseball. Its popularity has caused many cities to provide lighted fields where it may be played at night. Pingpong and skating rinks are becoming popular. Many of the larger cities maintain golf courses, while private golf courses are found in all cities and in many towns. In the northern states, places for safe ice skating in winter are provided. 

2. Growing interest in sports. The World War brought about a demand for strong bodies for all. 

In 1916, there were 743 golf courses in the country; and in 1930, there were 5,856, a gain of over 800%.

The United States Lawn Tennis Association estimates that we have more than 1,200,000 public park tennis players in the United States. Like indoor baseball, tennis and golf can be played by people of all ages and by both men and women. A sport for individuals or groups. 

The past twenty years have seen a remarkable growth of interest in sports from the standpoint of the spectator. Football, basketball, and baseball have become so popular that the need for larger stadiums, grandstands, and gymnasiums has been increasing each year. 

3. Hunting and fishing. Hunting and fishing are as old as civilization. This was man's first way of making a living. Today, hunting and fishing are among our most popular means of recreation and

large sums are spent each year for ammunition and equipment. More than seven million people in the 

United States secure hunting or fishing licenses each year. Government hatcheries are maintained to raise fish for our streams. The United States Government and various state governments have passed laws to prevent the extinction of game and game fish. 

In this way, we hope to preserve the sports of hunting and fishing for future generations. 

4. Reading, music, and drama. Not all people enjoy spending their spare time at play. Some prefer to stay at home and read good books. For them many libraries have been built. Mr. Andrew Carnegie spent a good part of his large fortune in building public libraries. Traveling libraries reach people in out-of-the-way places. In most cities one who is musically inclined has the opportunity of joining a musical organization. In some cities municipal bands are maintained. A person who loves music can derive much pleasure from playing in a band. 

The Little Theater movement for amateur actors is spreading, and it gives those who are interested in acting a chance for much pleasure. 

Other recreational agencies. Some of our most valuable organizations for young people are the 

Boy and Girl Scouts and the Camp Fire Girls. They teach boys and girls how to spend profitably their leisure time. They develop worthwhile hobbies and teach health rules. Another important group is the 

4-H Club organization for farm boys and girls. It is sponsored by the federal, state, and county gov-

ernments to teach those in the rural districts how to secure greater profit and pleasure from their work and play.

The Young Men's Christian Association and the Young Women's Christian Association are valuable organizations for the young people. Along with the 

Scouts, Camp Fire Girls, Knights of Columbus, and other organizations, they sponsor summer camps. 

There are now about two thousand summer camps in the United States, where boys and girls learn in various ways how to care for themselves. They also learn woodcraft, swimming, canoeing, hiking, and interesting games.

Motion pictures. One of the favorite forms of entertainment for Americans is the motion picture show. There are approximately 25,000 motion picture houses in the United States, and they are attended by more than 100,000,000 people each week. 

The movies have a great influence upon the lives of boys and girls. Our people copy the styles shown in the movies. They use the slang and language of their favorite actors. They may be uplifted by beautiful scenes, or on the other hand, they may be wrongfully influenced by some of the pictures. Motion pictures may cause children to lose much needed rest by over-stimulation. Many cities have a board of censors to pass on the worthiness of pictures-to encourage the good ones and discourage those that might have an evil influence.

Radio. Radio has increased in popularity even more than motion pictures. We now have more than

600 radio broadcasting stations licensed in the United States. The large broadcasting chains offer a variety of the best talent at all hours of the day and evening. 

Nearly one-half of the families of the United States. have radio receiving sets. Programs are paid for by different companies to advertise their products. 

Many advertisers sponsor special programs to entertain boys and girls. The radio not only furnishes excellent entertainment but it is a great educational tool as well. The United States government makes rules which govern broadcasting.

Planning for the future. Since recreation is growing in popularity, and since the American people have more leisure time, more land and water should be acquired for public use. Cities should plan ahead in their building programs and purchase adequate grounds for playgrounds and parks in advance of their needs. After a section of a city is built, prices of real estate become so high that it is difficult to secure enough land for recreational purposes. Our schools are teaching young people how to spend their leisure time. Commercial amusements should be supervised in order to keep them clean and wholesome. Conservation of our game and fish is a necessity. Highways should be beautified by removing objectionable sign boards. Slum sections in the cities should be cleared. When a tree is cut down another should be planted to take its place. A place to play should be available for everyone.

Our responsibility. Each individual should study his own needs in selecting his recreation. His selec-

tion should be purposeful. If he is weak physically, an outdoor game would be a good form of recreation. 

When a boy, Theodore Roosevelt was very weak. He became interested in boxing as well as in forms of 

Learning to swim. outdoor exercise. He developed into a very strong, energetic man. We should develop several leisuretime interests. This would prevent us from having limited interests. Along with some outdoor interest such as sports, gardening, or fishing, we should develop an interest in some of the arts, such as music, literature, or drama. If the weather or a slight sickness should prevent our going outdoors, we would

have an indoor interest for exercise, relaxation, or entertainment.

An important aspect of the profitable use of leisure is that of carefully budgeting our time. The budgeting of time requires careful thought just as does the budgeting of money.

1. Report to your class on what your community has done for you in providing places to play. 

2. Work out in class what additional parks and playgrounds are needed in your community. 

3. Find out what your community is doing to provide books for those who want to read. 

4. Make a list of all the musical organizations in your community. 

5. After talking with an older person, compare sports of today with sports of his day. 

6. Make an oral report to the class on your trip to a national park. 

7. Write a paper on My favorite hobby. Tell why you selected this hobby. 

8. What courses have you taken in school that might help you solve your leisure time problems? How? 

9. Make a list of all the worthwhile leisure-time activities you have read about. 

10. Make a complete list of the recreational needs of your community.

CHAPTER VIII PROVIDING SAFETY FOR OURSELVES AND OTHERS 

When prehistoric man thought about safety, he thought about defending himself against cold, hunger, and wild animals. He was in no danger of being killed by a live wire or an automobile. Today, most of our dangers are caused by man-made things. For this reason most of our dangerous accidents could be prevented. But when we think about the many people who are injured in accidents, we know that they are not being prevented. 

The number of people killed and injured in accidents. During the past dozen years, about 1,120,000 persons have been killed by accidents in the United States. This great number has been killed during the lifetime of many of our present-day school children. Most boys and girls twelve years old know of some friend or relative who has been killed or injured in an accident. During this same time, about 

7,000,000 people have been injured in accidents. 

Approximately 270,000 of these have been permanently injured.

What we pay for accidents. It is estimated that we are paying about $7,500,000,000 a year for accidents, other than those which happen in traffic. This means that accidents in this country are costing us 108

about $19,000 each minute of the day. If this were all the cost, it would not be so bad. When we think about the cost in suffering, pain, and worry which these accidents cause, we can see that we are paying too much for our carelessness.

What kinds of accidents do we have? A careless person can have an accident at almost any time and at almost any place. We sometimes have accidents even when we are cautious. Excepting traffic accidents, more people are killed and hurt from falls than from any other type of accident. Many people are hurt by touching live electric wires. Some are drowned. Some are hurt by stepping into holes in the street or stumbling over objects on the sidewalks. 

Many other accidents are caused by firearms, firecrackers, poisons, explosions, and breathing poisonous gases.

Accidents in the home. We are supposed to be safe when we are at home, but many people will be killed and injured in their homes this year. About 30,000 persons are killed in home accidents each year, and more than 4,000,000 others are injured. Each year, about 130,000 receive injuries in the home from which they never recover. What could so many people do to be hurt at home? Most of them fall down stairs, off ladders, over furniture, and in or out of bathtubs. Most of those injured by falls are old people.

About 6,000 persons, mostly children, are killed in the home each year by burns, scalds, and explosions. 

Many die from asphyxiation, suffocation, and poisoning. The kitchen is the most dangerous place in the

home. More than one-third of all home accidents happen there. School children can do much to make the homes safer by calling the attention of their parents to the new ways of preventing accidents.

Making work safe. People who work in factories would be in constant danger if plans were not made for their safety. Modern machines become engines of death if we are careless around them. About 

14,000 persons are killed in industrial accidents each year. More than 1,000,000 persons are injured.

These accidents are costing us about $5,000,000,000 per year, or about $40 for each of us. We pay this in the form of insurance and in the higher prices for the things we buy.

A few years ago the worker's safety was his own concern. If the building in which he worked was unsafe or if the machine with which he worked was dangerous, he had to take the chance of being injured or killed. About thirty years ago, states began to make the employers pay damages to injured workers. 

Safety devices were required on all dangerous machinery. The employers saw that it was better to prevent an injury than it was to pay for it after it happened. As a result of this movement for safety, accidents in industry are becoming fewer and fewer. In the four years from 1929 to 1933, the number of deaths in industrial accidents decreased from 20,000 to 14,500. 

What the government does to make work safe.

The government requires the buses and street cars in which we ride to be equipped with good brakes.

The drivers are required to pass certain physical tests. They must obey all safety regulations. The buildings in which we work must be constructed in accordance with certain safety standards. The electric, heating, and plumbing equipment must be safe. 

Fire-escapes and safety exits must be provided.

Whether we are at work, at a picture show, on the streets, or in the park, we see evidences that our governments are endeavoring to provide safety for us. State and city inspectors go about finding and eliminating danger spots.

Making schools safe. The school building itself may become a place of danger if it is poorly constructed or if it becomes very old and dilapidated. 

There are many accidents on the school ground resulting in broken arms and legs. Many accidents are caused by faulty equipment or by pupils' using the wrong equipment. More accidents happen to children of the sixth or seventh grade than happen to children of any other grade. More than 2,000,000 children ride to and from school each day on school buses. If the buses are unsafe or overcrowded, or if the drivers are careless, the lives of the children are endangered. Our school authorities are becoming more careful in their efforts to eliminate the hazards to school children. Terrible accidents, however, still occur. One of the greatest tragedies occurred on 

March 18, 1937, at New London, Texas, in which approximately 300 pupils and teachers were killed when an explosion wrecked the school building. 

Reducing school accidents. There is much that

can be done at school to reduce the number of accidents. The teacher should know and understand the ways of preventing accidents. The Parent-Teachers' 

Association should help to remove safety hazards.

Pupils could be organized into safety patrols to direct traffic around the school building, and to watch for and report dangers on the school grounds. Safety education should be a part of the school program, and the boys and girls taught how to avoid modern dangers. It is estimated that 7,500 school children are saved each year because of safety education.

Our part in providing safety. We cannot always depend upon others to protect us from dangers. 

Parents and teachers can tell us and show us what to do, and what not to do; our public officers can place every safeguard around us; yet if we are careless we may have an accident. Whether we are driving a car, climbing stairs, or playing ball, we should never take unnecessary chances. If we fall and break an arm, our parents not only worry about us but have additional medical expense. If we have an automobile accident, we may injure those in another car or someone on the street. Not only should we keep ourselves from having accidents, but we should help to keep others from getting hurt. We should play in such way as not to hurt our playmates. We should tell other people when they are in danger. 

We should help small children and old people across the streets. We should try to keep old people in our homes and elsewhere from falling. People who are alert and careful not only have fewer accidents

themselves, but they aid others in avoiding accidents. 

Fire, our friend or enemy. There is an old Greek myth that tells us about the visit of Prometheus in heaven. While there, he lighted a torch at the chariot of the Sun. He brought this fire back to earth and gave it to man. With fire, man became civilized and was soon lord over all other animals. 

When we stand and see the fire demon destroy our neighbor's house and all its contents, we know that fire is not always our friend. If we use fire in the proper way, it becomes a friend and servant. It will keep us warm, cook our food, and run our factories. 

If we handle fire carelessly, it becomes our greatest enemy. It burns our hands and our bodies, our homes and our factories, and, sometimes, our cities. 

How our forefathers fought fires. Man has always had to fight fires. You have heard how Nero played the fiddle while Rome burned. In ancient Rome, when a house caught fire, the owner would call in his slaves to fight it. In early American times, citizens were required to keep water pails and ladders near at hand to help their neighbors in case of fire. Later, certain people, known as volunteers, were made responsible for fighting the fires. In a few years some cities began to employ men to fight fires as they employed others to make arrests or to build streets. 

This was the beginning of modern fire departments. 

How we fight fires today. Each city is equipped today with modern fire-fighting machinery. The large pumping engines will release 1,000 gallons of water per minute under 100 pounds of pressure. The

ladder trucks are equipped with quick-raising, electrically operated ladders. A modern hose-wagon will carry 4,000 feet of hose. The rescue wagon is equipped with life nets, gas masks, pulmotors, and medicine for burns. Fire alarm boxes are placed throughout the city. These are all connected with a central office. Certain streets are used as fire lanes. The group provides protection from fire. 

It takes from one to three minutes for the fire truck to reach the fire. It is exciting to see the firemen lay the hose and throw the water on the fire. After the fire is out, a report must be made showing location of the fire, how it started, and the extent of the damage. If the fire is only a small one, a chemical extin-

guisher is used. Most states now have laws which require public buildings to be equipped with fire extinguishers.

Some big fires. Hundreds of small fires occur each day, and these small fires often grow so large they are hard to control. Some of them are recorded in history. Almost the whole city of Rome was destroyed by fire six times during its early history. In 1666, a great fire occurred in London. It left 200,000 homeless. About sixty years ago a large section of Chicago was burned. This fire was reported to have been started when a cow kicked over a lantern. On April 

18, 1906, one of the world's greatest fires occurred in San Francisco. This fire burned 250,000 buildings.

The losses were so great that many insurance companies were ruined. We have had many other fires in which there was great loss of life and property. 

What causes fires. Big fires are caused by little fires. Little fires can begin in the most unthought of places and be started in the most unexpected ways. 

Most of the accidental fires are started by the careless handling of matches. People in the United States strike 600,000 matches each minute of the day. Many of these matches continue to burn after they are thrown away. That is where the danger lies. We have no such thing as a "safety" match so long as it is afire. The next greatest cause of fires is found in defective chimneys or flues. Many fires are caused by worn-out stoves and pipes, gas, oil, cigarettes, and fireworks. All of these things create fire hazards. 

Open lights and fires are always dangerous. Defec-

tive electric wires start many fires. Some fires are caused by lightning and some by explosions. Oily rags packed closely in a box or closet will sometimes catch fire through what is called "spontaneous combustion." You can probably think of many fires that have been caused in other ways. The one big cause of most fires is carelessness. Few fires would ever get beyond control if we were not careless with the little fires we start. Remember, fires do not just happen; they are started. 

What fires cost. Almost every minute of the day somewhere in the United States, a fire alarm is ringing. We average more than one fire a minute night and day in this country. The United States loses more by fires than any other country in the world. 

We have a fire loss each year of more than four dollars for every man, woman, and child. In Holland, the loss is 11 cents apiece; in Switzerland, 15 cents; in France, 45 cents; and in England, it is 

72 cents. We have an actual cash loss in fires of about $500,000,000 each year. Ten thousand men, women, and children in this country lose their lives from fires each year.

Whether we have a fire or not, we all help pay for this fire loss in the form of insurance rates and in higher prices for goods that are insured. If you live in a town where there are many destructive fires, you will pay more for insurance than you will if you live where the fire loss is not so great. If we add the cost of insurance and fire departments to the loss caused by flames, we find that the average family of

five persons pays about $38 per year for fires. We pay this sum whether we have a fire or not. In other words, every time there is a fire in this country, it costs each of us a little. If someone should carelessly throw away a match, it might cost each one of us a small amount. A young man carelessly threw away a lighted cigarette in Jersey City, a few years ago, and it cost the American people $2,000,000. We should remember that where there is a destructive fire there is a loss.

Preventing fires. It is much easier to prevent a fire than it is to put out one after it has started. 

A few years ago, a campaign was started in this country to make the people realize the value of fire prevention. Today we have "fire prevention days" and "fire prevention weeks." Buildings must be constructed now in accordance with fire prevention rules. These rules provide for certain types of chimneys and flues, electric wiring, thickness of walls, and fire escapes. State and city inspectors look over buildings to see that the rules are followed. Most buildings are now equipped with fire-hose and fireextinguishers. You have seen fire-extinguishers on buses, trains, and in almost all public buildings. 

Many private buildings are equipped with automatic sprinklers. Fire inspectors are constantly seeking dangerous places where fires might start. They require business men to keep their premises free from fire hazards.

An important part of the work of a fire department is to prevent fires. The fire department does not

prevent the first fire from getting started, but it prevents it from spreading to other buildings. The quicker the firemen put out a fire, the less chance it has to spread.

The government makes a special effort to prevent forest fires. These fires are very destructive, since they spread rapidly. In many places, incinerators are furnished for campers. Caution signs are placed in various places reminding people of the danger of forest fires. Fire-lookouts are placed in our national parks and forest reserves. When smoke is seen from these lookouts, it is investigated by forest rangers.

Our part in preventing fires. Any person who strikes a match, stands before an open fire, burns trash, or smokes a cigarette can help prevent fires. 

A match, cigar, or cigarette should never be thrown away while burning. A person should form the habit of breaking every match before throwing it away.

Matches should not be left where rats or mice can get to them. Little children should not be allowed to play with matches. 

Open fires should never be built on windy days near houses, dry grass or leaves, or other inflammable materials. The fires should be put out before leaving camp.

We should be very careful with fire near gasoline and kerosene. These liquids throw out gas, and we should remember that oil fumes and gas will catch fire from the slightest spark. One should never strike a match in a room where there is an odor of gas.

What to do in case of fire. All of us should learn what to do in case of fire. We should know the location of fire-extinguishers and fire-escapes. We should know the quickest way to bring water to a fire. We should know how to call the fire department. If our clothes or the clothes of others should catch fire, we should know how to wrap ourselves or others in order to smother the fire. Clear thinking and calmness are needed to fight fires. We must realize that almost all fires are the result of carelessness. A person who is careless with fire is, indeed, a public enemy. 

Protection from flood waters. Uncontrolled flood waters are very destructive. Unlike fire, floods can- رالاو 

Louisville, Kentucky, during the Ohio River flood in 1937.

not be prevented; but they can be prevented from running wild and destroying everything in their path. We have two or three big floods each year somewhere in the United States. These floods occur along our rivers or along our coasts. Every river in the United States is a possible agency of destruction. Even our small creeks sometimes destroy much property and many lives.

Flood waters on the Mississippi and Ohio rivers have cost the American people millions of dollars. 

In 1927, the Mississippi river destroyed more than $300,000,000 worth of property. In 1937, the Mississippi and Ohio rivers became raging torrents of destruction. In Paducah, Kentucky, a town of 34,500 population, 32,700 suffered as a result of the flood. 

These people lost $27,600,000 in this flood. Homes were washed away or damaged to the extent of $17,500,00. Eight million dollars' worth of business property and merchandise was destroyed. Streets costing more than $1,000,000 were completely ruined, and there was a loss of more than $500,000 in school buildings. All of this happened in one city. Louisville, Kentucky, lost more than $100,000,000 in the same flood. For hundreds of miles along the Ohio 

River, houses, livestock, and crops were destroyed. 

Winds frequently blow water in from the oceans, gulfs, and lakes, causing floods along our coasts. In 

1900, the city of Galveston, Texas, was almost completely destroyed and more than 6,000 people drowned by one of these storms.

How we control the flood waters. We have tried

three plans to control our floods. In some places we have reforested the land from which water drains into our rivers. This helps to hold the flood water back. We have also built dams across some of our rivers. With a dam the water can be held back and let out gradually. In a recent flood on the Tennessee 

River, water was held back by the Norris Dam, and 

Courtesy Tennessee Valley Authority 

Results of uncontrolled flood waters. 

5,000 homes in Chattanooga were saved. As the water runs over a dam, it operates machinery which makes electricity for power. Since large dams cost a great deal, we find them on only a few of the rivers in this country.

Our third method of controlling floods is by dikes, levees, and sea-walls. Dikes and levees are banks of dirt thrown up to keep the flood waters from spreading. Sea-walls are dirt banks or walls of concrete or stone built to protect the land from ocean water. 

Fewer lives are lost than formerly in great floods. 

This is due to the fact that the United States Weather Bureau warns all people in dangerous places that the flood or storm is coming, and the government helps to move the people to safety.

What happens to the rain water that falls on our streets during a heavy rain? If this water were not controlled, it would often flow into our homes and business houses. The city builds storm sewers to carry away this water before it does any damage.

Storm sewers are large pipes with surface openings that are placed under the ground. 

There is little that we as individuals can do to control or prevent floods. This task is so big that it must be done by all of us through the government. 

1. Make a list showing the losses in your community resulting from accidents. 

2. Make a report on the different ways in which one might be injured in the home. 

3. Visit a factory, railroad shop, water or light plant, or gin and observe the different safety devices which are in use. 

4. Make a list of all the ways you can observe which show you what the government is trying to do to provide safety.

5. Find out how many children have been hurt by accidents on the school ground this year, and what caused the accidents. 

6. Write a paper, or make a talk to the class on the subject: "How we can make our school ground and schoolhouse safer." 

7. Debate: Resolved: That boys are more careless than girls. 

8. Give a description of the fire-fighting apparatus in a city of 100 years ago. Compare the firefighting apparatus of that time with a modern firefighting system. 

9. Tell exactly what you would do: a. If you awoke in the night and your house was afire b. If you were passing along the street and saw a house on fire c. If your clothes should catch fire d. If your playmate's clothes should catch fire 

10. Visit a fire hall and learn: a. How fire messages are received b. How the hose is laid c. How the firemen find the way to the fire d. How a person is rescued when overcome by smoke 

11. Make a list of all the fires that have occurred in your community the past year. What was the fire loss? Tell what caused the fires. 

12. Explain to the class a way to prevent fires in the home. 

13. Let each member of the class write or tell about the biggest fire he ever saw. 

14. Find out and compare the fire insurance rates in your community and another, and why the difference, if any. 

15. Find out where the rain water in your community goes. Make a report to the class.

CHAPTER IX MEETING THE PROBLEMS OF TRAFFIC IN MODERN LIFE 

Traffic in the United States in pioneer days. The first streets in the United States were built to accommodate only such traffic as that of pedestrians, pack horses, coaches, and wagons. This kind of traffic needed very little space in which to move easily and safely. The streets were, therefore, narrow and often crooked. The main streets were paved with cobblestones. The other streets were left to become muddy when it rained or dusty in dry weather. The travelers tied their horses to hitching racks placed along the sides of the streets. Traffic officers were not needed, as there were few people on the streets. 

The greatest danger to people on the streets was that of runaway horses. Most people could get out of the way of them before much damage was done. With the coming of the automobile, the easy-going and peaceful street-life in America was changed. Automobiles on narrow and crooked streets soon became instruments of danger.

What we pay for traffic accidents. The automobile killed its first person in New York City in 1899. 

During the next year five persons were killed there by automobiles. Now about four people per day are killed in traffic accidents in that city. 124

More than 27,000,000 automobiles are running on our streets and highways today. That is about one car for each five persons in the United States. Each one of these cars, if improperly handled, might cause a serious accident. In 1936, automobiles killed 38,500 people in the United States. This was 106 people

them will be permanently disabled. More Americans are losing their lives each month as a result of automobile accidents than were killed each month in the 

World War. 

From Sportsmanlike Driving Series, courtesy American Automobile Association 

The scene of an accident often involves new hazards. What precautions does this picture suggest for other drivers and pedestrians? 

Our automobile wrecks are costing the nation about $2,500,000,000 per year, $20 for each of us. 

This great loss can be reduced if people become convinced that it is too great and that care will prevent much of it. School children are often much smarter than adults in knowing how to protect themselves from automobiles. Fewer children under fourteen years of age are losing their lives in automobile accidents. This is due partly to safety education in our public schools. Children are learning that it does not pay to take chances. Safety education has saved the lives of more than 25,000 children during the past five years.

What kinds of automobile accidents do we have? 

Almost one-half of the people killed by automobiles

in this country are pedestrians. More than 20,000 men, women, and children walking or playing on the streets and highways will be killed by automobiles this year. Many will be killed because the drivers of the automobiles are careless. Many grown people will be killed because they do not observe city or town traffic rules, such as crossing a street only at a corner. Hundreds of children will be killed because they are playing in streets.

Another type of accident is that in which two cars run together. More than 4,000 people will die this year in this kind of accident. About 3,500 people will die while driving across a track in front of a street car or a train. About 2,000 will die because they run their cars into fixed objects. About 8,500 more will die because of various other types of automobile accidents.

Why we have automobile accidents. More accidents occur from fast driving than from any other cause. Failure to observe the rules of the road, such as signaling before turning, or driving on the wrong side of the street or highway, causes the second greatest number of accidents. Inattention while driving probably ranks third as a cause for accidents. 

Pedestrians who cross the streets in the wrong place or at the wrong time are responsible for many accidents. Drivers who double-park, cut-in, or fail to stop while passengers are getting on and off the street cars are responsible also for many accidents. 

People who are not accustomed to driving and the very young and very old drivers have many accidents

because they often become excited and confused. 

Driving while drunk, or intoxicated, is becoming one of the leading causes for automobile accidents. The following motto seen on signs along our highways contains good advice: "If you drive, don't drink. 

If you drink, don't drive."

Many times the cause for an accident is given as skidding, defective brakes or steering wheel, blinding lights, parked car, or road defects. We know that most automobile accidents are caused by carelessness and recklessness on the part of the drivers and the pedestrians.

Cures for congested traffic. If you look down the main streets of any city from four to seven o'clock in the evening, you will see almost a solid mass of The group attempts to meet the problems of congested traffic.

cars. If traffic should be held up for ten minutes at any busy street crossing, the streets would be packed with cars for several blocks in all directions. It is more dangerous to ride in an automobile at night than it is in day time. In 1933, there were 413,890 accidents between 6 A. M. and 6 P. M., but only 13,750 people were killed in these accidents. From 6 P. M. to 6 A. M. there were only 342,610 accidents, yet 

16,230 people were killed. In the four-hour period from 6 to 10 in the morning, 2,550 people were killed in traffic accidents. In the four-hour period from 5 to 9 in the evening, 9,720 people were killed.

Making driving easier and safer. It is not easy to drive on a street where traffic is congested. The city should try to keep too many cars from being on a particular street at the same time. Some cities solve this problem by routing certain traffic around the congested areas. For example, trucks are kept off certain streets which are used for passenger cars. "Through" highways are often built around a town instead of through the main streets. Big industries often let their workers out at different hours. This permits one group to leave before another group enters the streets. In cities where streets are too narrow for automobile traffic, cars may be driven in only one direction on certain streets. These are called "one-way streets" and are plainly marked so that drivers may know them. 

Traffic may be made easier and safer by improving the streets and highways. Smooth, paved streets are safer than rough ones. Streets are safer without

street car tracks. All streets should be made as wide as possible, and traffic lanes should be marked. All streets should be of uniform width. Narrow or winding streets are dangerous. Curves should be banked and steep hills cut down. Sharp turns and one-way bridges should be eliminated from all highways and country roads. 111 

Slow Driver

From Sportsmanlike Driving Series, courtesy American Automobile Association 

The slow driver, by holding up a line of cars, causes irritation and impatience, thus inducing others to take chances. What examples of poor judgment are shown here?

Proper parking arrangements make driving easier and safer. Some people are so thoughtless that in parking they will take up two spaces instead of one. 

Others will park at an imperfect angle and make it difficult for another to move his car from the curb. 

Parking is forbidden on many streets. On others it is permitted only for a limited time. Many cities are now using parking meters to control the time for parking. These are small, box-like slot-machines attached to posts on the curb. A small fee, usually a nickel, is charged for one hour's parking. 

Driving at a safe speed. A few years ago the speed

limit for automobiles in most towns was eight to twelve miles per hour. A speed of from 20 to 25 miles per hour is now permitted in most towns and cities. 

Speed limits are difficult to enforce. If the limit is set too low, people will ignore it; if too high, it encourages dangerous driving on the part of some drivers. 

Some cities forbid reckless driving regardless of speed. 

Most cities now use the "STOP STREET" and "THROUGH STREET" plan to slow up traffic.

Under this plan, drivers must stop before entering a through street. 

Traffic lights. A few years ago one saw a traffic policeman at nearly every busy corner. He told people when to go and when to stop. Nowadays we find traffic lights at busy street intersections. When the light is green, traffic moves forward; when the light is red, traffic stops. Some cities use an amber light between the green and red to warn the drivers to start or stop. In some cities a bell rings when the light changes. This warns drivers who are not looking. One should always watch for traffic signals and never cross the street while the light is red.

Left and right turns. Many accidents occur when people in heavy traffic attempt to turn either to the left or right. In many cities left turns are prohibited on certain corners. If a person intends to turn to the left, he should be as near the middle of the street as possible before attempting the turn. A hand signal should be given in making a turn on a busy street.

Regulation of drivers. Some of the cars on our highways have unsafe drivers. In order to eliminate the unsafe ones, states and cities now require drivers to be examined and licensed. Some cities require that the applicant know the traffic rules of the city. All who drive cars should be familiar with traffic rules. The group provides protection from traffic hazards. 

Some cities and states periodically examine automobiles to determine whether they are safe for driving. Such things as lights and brakes should be kept in first-class condition at all times. Many automobile firms distribute free booklets containing driving rules and safety instructions.

Investigating and reporting accidents. Every accident involving an automobile should be investigated by the traffic officers and a report made to the central traffic department. These investigations would bring out the details of the accident and show who was at fault. The information found by these investigations could be used as a help in preventing future accidents of the same kind.

Our responsibility. Every person who uses the streets or highways either as a car driver or as a pedestrian has certain obligations and duties to others. We must remember that the streets are public property. Other people have as much right to use them as we have. We should obey all the traffic regulations. If the street signs say, "DRIVE 

SLOW" we should drive that way. Road signs and markers are provided for our safety. Other people on the highway expect us to obey these signs. We should insist that all cars we ride in have good brakes and good lights. Many school children have been killed because the drivers did not or could not stop quickly enough. 

Boys and girls should not play in the street. Car drivers often fail to see children, especially if they run out from behind cars or other objects along the street. Children who ride bicycles on the street should be very careful. They should never make a turn if a car is near them. Nor should they hold on to the cars as they ride the bicycles. Bicycle riders also should obey the traffic rules. Everyone should always look in both directions before crossing a street.

Avoiding "unavoidable accidents". Many of the so-called unavoidable accidents can be avoided. The way to prevent them is to be on the alert to take care of a dangerous situation. By that is meant, do your This could have been avoided. part and the other person's part also, when he is careless, reckless, or incapable. Have a sufficient "margin of safety" to allow for others' mistakes.

This requires extreme watchfulness, so that one will be ready to act promptly when the wrong thing is done by the other driver or by the pedestrian. 

1. Talk to some older person in your family or community about a trip he or she made in the days before the automobile. Retell this conversation to the class.

2. Go to the business district of your town or city during the busy time of the day and make a list of the traffic rules you see disregarded. 

3. Make a list of the traffic rules that govern traffic in your community. Which of these are state laws and which are municipal? 

4. Plan an examination you would give a driver to test his knowledge of his car and the safety of his driving. 

5. Notice three or four areas in your community where parking is a serious problem. What would you suggest to eliminate this difficulty? 

6. Make a list of the different kinds of traffic light systems you have seen in use and determine the merits of each. 

7. Suppose you were in an accident where someone else was hurt but you escaped injury. What would you do? 

8. Talk to a traffic officer in your community and learn of his qualifications for his work and of some of his difficulties.

CHAPTER X PROTECTING OURSELVES FROM LAWBREAKERS 

The importance of public protection. The maintenance of law and order is the most important function of government. If our government should suddenly cease to protect us from the evil actions of other people and from the destructive forces of nature, nothing we possess would be safe. Some individuals would rob and mistreat other individuals. 

Groups would fight other groups and the strong would oppress the weak. We could not own an automobile, money, or even a watch, without having a constant fear that some stronger person would take it from us. Our churches, lodges, and other organizations would help to maintain law and order, but these would not be enough to furnish needed protection. 

The need for rules and laws. In order to live, learn, work, or play together people must have rules. These rules indicate what the citizens of a community, a state, or a nation consider right. They also indicate what they consider to be wrong. If a person does what the law indicates is wrong, he is considered a delinquent or a lawbreaker. Each person is expected to obey the law. When a person refuses to follow the rules of the group, he becomes dangerous to those who are following them. For example, the rules 136

require that a person cross the street only when the traffic light is green; if someone should attempt to cross when the light is red, an accident might occur in which those who were trying to follow the rule might be injured. Imagine what would happen at a busy street corner if no one obeyed the traffic laws. 

What would happen in a football game if no one followed the rules or if all should refuse to obey the referee? What would happen if one team were willing but the other refused to be governed by the rules of the game? Now, our federal, state, and local governments have all made rules governing the art of living together. Some people live their lives with no more sense of fair play than the disobedient boy in a football game. They do not want to follow the rules. In order to keep the lawbreakers from interfering with our living together, we must either change their attitude toward the rules or else remove them from the group.

The changing need for protection. In the early days of our country, the people did not need so much protection from the public as we need today. 

Everyone in the community knew almost everyone else, and few people visited from one community to another. In modern times people are strangers to many of their neighbors nearby. In many instances people living in an apartment house do not know who lives next door. The first Americans had little occasion to steal because land was cheap and food plentiful. Few people had property that others wanted. 

There was very little money in the country to tempt

robbers. Since people had few contacts with other people, there was little need for personal protection. 

Today, there is much desirable property of all kinds. 

Money is found in cash registers in every store and filling station in the country. Many persons move about from place to place and are constantly among strangers in whom they have no interest, and for whom they have no love. They care not what happens to their fellow-men. For these reasons, we need much more protection from the public than our grandfathers needed.

The nature of crime and criminals. In early days when one person injured another, no one felt obligated to do anything about it except the one injured, or his family, or possibly his friends. Then each person imposed such punishment as he could on the one who injured him. When, later on, certain unfair acts were considered harmful to the group, as well as to the individual, laws were passed declaring them crimes. Anyone who violated these laws was called a criminal. It is sometimes difficult to tell exactly when a person becomes a criminal. Many acts are forbidden which are not in themselves crimes, but which, if done, might lead to a crime. For example, failure to slow down at a street crossing might not be a crime. But if, as the result of a failure to slow down, a collision should occur and someone be killed, that would be a crime. For this reason, we are forbidden to do certain things even though the acts within themselves might not be criminal. 

Changing ideas of crimes. Our ideas of crime

change with the times. Five hundred years ago it was a crime for a person in England who owned no property to keep a dog. There was a time when it was a crime to print a book. Not many years ago it was not considered a crime to hold lotteries or gamble. These are usually forbidden now. Our lawmakers are constantly making new laws and repealing old ones. Thus, as man's ideas change about right and wrong, new laws are made to meet changed opinions and conditions. 

The beginning of lawbreaking. Did you ever know one who did not respect the rights of others: one who neglected duty, violated law, and had no regard for the lives and property of other people? Such a person is a criminal.

Most of our criminals started on their road to crime while they were young. Indeed, almost onehalf of those in our prisons and reformatories today are men and boys under twenty-four years of age. 

What caused these young people to lead lives of crime?

A child who lives in a home without proper standards, or one in which there is much quarreling, very likely will not learn to respect the rights of others. 

It is easy for a child in such an environment to form bad habits, such as drinking intoxicating liquor or loafing around cheap amusement places. After a child has formed one bad habit it is easy to form another. Parents who live in the slums of our cities are usually busy making a living. They do not have time to play and be with their children. As a result

the children find their own associates and amusements.

Many a young person has become a criminal because of bad associates. Idleness often leads young people into lives of crime. People who are busy and employed with worthwhile work seldom become lawbreakers. When young people leave home, fail to

Courtesy Federal Bureau of Investigation, United States Department of Justice find work, and find themselves in need of food and clothing, they often turn to stealing and robbing.

They are soon wanted by the police and learn too late that crime does not pay.

Amount of crime in the United States. It is difficult to determine the exact amount of crime in this

country. About the only definite information we have is about those who have actually been sent to reformatories and prisons. There are in our prisons today about 200,000 men and women. This is about one person out of every 630. These figures do not include the number in our jails. Each year, about 500,000 people in this country are sent to jails of various kinds. About as many more are fined or placed on probation. These people are injuring all of us either directly or indirectly, and they are a constant danger to our lives and property. 

What we pay for crime. The cost of crime is so great and includes so many different things that it is almost impossible to determine how much crime costs the nation. In the United States we spend on police departments and prisons about $1,000,000,000 per year. We lose through forgeries, worthless checks, and other frauds, about $1,300,000,000 per year. Property losses through burglaries, robberies, and thefts amount to about $1,200,000,000 per year. 

These figures do not include the vague and unknown losses caused by waste of crime; for example, losses caused by the failure of our criminals to do productive work, or losses caused by the liquor and drug traffic. If we should add all these losses together, they would probably reach the staggering sum of $12,000,000,000 per year. This is almost $100 for every man, woman, and child in the United States. 

We pay this loss in high insurance rates, in taxes, in court costs, and in replacing stolen property.

Even with all this loss in money values, we might wonder if the other costs of crime are not still greater. Think of the sorrow, shame, and misery our criminals have caused. Homes have been destroyed because the fathers have been killed or sent to prison. 

Widows have had all their property stolen from them. Many have lost their best friends through crimes. Thousands of men, women, and children are heartbroken today because of crime.

How criminals are detected and caught. Many people think that all criminals are ignorant and stupid. They are not. A test of about 200,000 criminals shows that as a group the criminals are only a little less intelligent than those who are not criminals. 

Some of our worst lawbreakers are very shrewd. 

Our government officers must be wise and intelligent to detect and arrest them. 

We have many officers in our national, state, and local governments who spend their time studying the habits and customs of criminals. They try to learn with whom the notorious criminals associate, and where they are likely to hide after committing a crime.

The Federal Bureau of Investigation. The Federal 

Bureau of Investigation at Washington has been very successful in finding and arresting some of the most daring and dangerous criminals. This bureau is trying to secure an identification record of every criminal in the United States. It keeps also a record of criminals in other countries.

The best way of identifying a person is by studying his fingerprints. No two persons have the same fingerprints. If a criminal should leave only a small part of the prints of one finger at the scene of the crime, it would be enough to identify him, provided his fingerprints are on file. At present the Federal 

Bureau of Investigation has on file more than 5,000,000 sets of fingerprints. It receives about 

2,200 new sets each day. These prints are classified and arranged in such way that any one of the Courtesy Federal Bureau of Investigation, United States Department of Justice Making examination of crime evidence in the technical laboratory. 

5,000,000 can be located within five minutes. Pictures and descriptions of wanted criminals are sent from Washington to all police departments throughout the country. The city, state, and federal police are always searching for these enemies of society.

State and local officers. Most lawbreakers are discovered and arrested by state and local officers. 

There are so many of these officers that one is likely to be near when a crime is committed. When anyone discovers that a crime has been committed, he should at once notify the police or sheriff, who will make an investigation of the place where the crime occurred. Every mark of evidence will be sought. 

A cigarette stub, a drop of blood, a hair, or a fingerprint will be carefully taken and studied. A small piece of wood taken from the ladder used in the Lindbergh kidnaping case was found to come from the same tree as the lumber used in a garage in 

New York City. Ballistic experts can tell from what gun a bullet is fired. If our officers can find one little clue, they are usually able to unravel the most baffling crime.

Capturing criminals. It has not been so many years since criminals tried to escape from the scenes of their crimes by riding fast horses. It would take them hours to go from one state to another. Today, by using high-powered automobiles and good roads, a criminal can commit a crime early one night and by the next morning be 500 miles away. This makes it necessary for local officers to work fast and try to catch the lawbreaker before he leaves the community. 

The radio is a great help in the capturing of criminals. Policemen use automobiles and motorcycles equipped with radios. If a store is robbed, for example, someone calls the police station. A call is broadcast to the officers who are cruising around.

A description of the robber and his car is given to all officers in the city. Even those out on the highways and in other cities may hear the broadcast of the robbery. Immediately all begin to look for the wanted lawbreaker. Descriptions and pictures of some criminals are placed in public places, and newspapers usually give accounts of the crimes that have been committed. If criminals escape the local officers, the police officials of other states will be notified to watch for them. Thus, our enemies of society soon learn that they are pursued by the officers of the law wherever they may go.

Changing ideas of punishment. What do we do with our lawbreakers after we catch them? The first step is to try them in a court to find out whether they violated the law. The court also determines what punishment they should have if found guilty. 

In the past, most of the convicted criminals were treated with a spirit of revenge. They were given severe penalties for slight offenses. People thought only of punishing the criminal, not of reforming him. Today, we realize that the criminal is a misfit in society. Part of this is due to ignorance and bad association. We have, therefore, changed our aim and method of punishment, and seek to change the criminal's habits. We place the convicted criminals in a favorable environment and teach them how to make an honest living. We know now that certainty of punishment will keep more people from committing crimes than will severity of punishment.

How lawbreakers are punished. People who would injure us or steal our property must not be permitted to be free to do as they please. This is the reason we have jails and penitentiaries. But all people who commit crimes are not placed in prison. Sometimes the judge finds the offender guilty but promises that he will not be punished, provided he will not violate the law again. This is called the suspended sentence. 

If the offense is only a minor one, the offender will be fined. Fines are greater punishment for poor people than for the rich. First offenders are often placed on probation. When a lawbreaker is placed on probation, he is given into the care of someone who promises to look after him and to report regularly to the judge about his conduct. 

Children nine years of age or over are subject to punishment in most states, but they are tried in special courts known as "juvenile courts." Formerly, the young and the old criminals were tried in the same courts and placed together in the same prisons. 

Instead of becoming better, the young often became worse because of their association with hardened criminals. The boys and girls are now sent to reformatories or placed on probation. A reformatory is somewhat like a school. Of course, the boys and girls are watched closely to keep them from running away. They must stay in the reformatory for such time as the judge commits them. 

You can see that the chief purpose of punishing young criminals is to break them of their bad habits early in life. How much better for all it would be if

the boys and girls could keep from forming bad habits, and would never need to be punished!

Public protectors of life and property. The federal government and every city, county, and state employ a number of men and women whose duty it is to protect us from those who would threaten our lives or property. When we deposit money at the bank, we know that the state and the federal government will protect it for us. We may leave our homes for a week without fearing that someone will break in and steal our clothes and house furnishings. Even at night while we are sleeping, the night watchman and police are protecting our homes and our stores. 

The smallest towns have someone to protect lives and property. Large cities like New York and

Chicago employ thousands of policemen. The presence of these protectors does not stop crime altogether, but it does lessen it. 

What policemen do for us. Few people in a community render a more valuable service than do the policemen. They not only keep people from injuring and robbing others, but they are always ready to help in many different ways. They keep order at public gatherings. They care for lost children and give information to strangers. They render first aid to people who are injured and lead processions and parades through the streets. They assist at fires, report danger spots in the streets, direct traffic, and testify in court cases. In a recent study of the 

Los Angeles police system, it was found that the ordinary policeman had 152 different duties to perform.

The most important work of the policeman is to detect and arrest law violators. To do this job well requires a strong, courageous, and intelligent person. 

An applicant for a position on a police force now must pass a difficult examination which tests his physical and mental ability. After he is selected, he is required to attend a special' training school for policemen. Here he learns the best way of handling criminals and of dealing with people in general. 

This is one reason why our policemen are courteous and qualified to do their work. 

Over 100 cities in the United States now employ policewomen. The chief work of these officers is to protect and care for young girls who may be in danger.

People who live in the rural areas need almost as much police protection as do those who live in the towns and cities. Automobiles and good roads make it easier for the criminal to rob country stores and homes, and then escape to the cities. We also need police to operate between towns to help catch lawviolators who try to escape from one town to another. 

To meet this need we have a county and a state police force in most states.

County police protection. The head of the county police force is called a sheriff. He usually has his office in the county courthouse. One man would be unable to furnish protection to all the people of a county. For this reason, the sheriff has men called deputies to help him. These men go about over the county looking for law violators. The county officers

are often assisted by constables and marshals who operate in different sections of the county. 

The state police force. Many of our states have a police force that operates over the whole state. In nineteen states the state body of police is known as the highway patrol. These officers enforce the highway laws. The oldest and one of the best known state police forces is that of the Texas Rangers. 

This organization was formed ten years before Texas was admitted to the Union. Many states are forming state identification bureaus in connection with the State Department of Public Safety. Finger prints and other means of identifying criminals are collected by cities and states in a way similar to that used by the national government. 

The federal police force. The Federal Bureau of 

Investigation at Washington has about 600 policemen or "G-men", as they are usually called. These officers look for persons who commit such crimes as kidnaping, killing federal officers, and robbing banks.

Whenever these men begin a search for a criminal, they never quit until they find him. 

Thousands of persons are reported "missing" each month in the United States. Many of them are found dead and are buried as unknowns. Some are found alive but do not remember who they are. The 

Federal Bureau of Investigation is trying to make this impossible by encouraging all citizens to submit their fingerprints so they may be filed for future reference. If every person's fingerprints were on

record in Washington, there would be no such thing as an "unidentified person" in this country. 

1. Report to the class the things you have observed which protect us from lawbreakers. 

2. Make a list of the rules or laws which you have seen violated. Why do you think these laws were violated? 

3. Find out what is being done for juvenile lawbreakers in your community. 

4. Make a floor talk about the property which has been stolen and destroyed by criminals in your community during the past year. How much was this property worth? 

5. Make a list of the ways criminals are punished in your state. Tell why you think these ways are good or bad. 

6. Talk with a policeman. Find out how long he works, how he finds lawbreakers, and other services he renders. 

7. Attend county court some day. Report to class what you observe.

CHAPTER XI PROVIDING SECURITY FOR ALL 

The causes of insecurity. If you were to plan a trip into the heart of Africa, you would think of many uncertainties which you might encounter. You might be uncertain as to the weather conditions. 

You might not know whether you would meet savages, wild animals, and other dangers. The first thing you would do would be to study the causes for these hazards and the chance of meeting them, and then make plans, either to avoid them or to make them less dangerous.

In making the trip through life, a person does not know exactly what hazards he may meet. We know that most persons will meet conditions that may make uncertain a comfortable and happy existence. 

Some will have only the barest necessities of life, and others will have to depend upon relatives and upon relief organizations for food, clothing, and shelter. Some will be in this condition through their own fault. These are the ones who have been intemperate, wasteful, or lazy. Some of the hazards of life are not the result of individual action, but are caused by forces beyond individual control. 

The chief causes for insecurity are old age, unemployment, sickness, and accidents. Children often become insecure if crippled or if they have no parents to care for them. 151

The meaning of security. Having security means to be free from fear, anxiety, or care. It means being sure or certain of something. Today, we want to be secure in our health, our lives, our jobs, our property, our old age.

People have always tried to plan their savings in order to have something for the future. Even ants, squirrels, and other creatures plan for the winter by building nests and storing away food. 

In man's search for security, he has cleared and improved the land, built homes, and organized and supported banks and insurance companies of various kinds. Also, he has educated and equipped himself and, through perseverance, industry, and economy, is endeavoring to carry out a program which will protect him and his family against sickness and want. 

Old age as a cause for insecurity. There are many things about the future that we do not know. We do know, however, that if we live long enough we will become old. If a boy who is twelve years old lives fifty-eight years more, he will be an old man of seventy years. The number of people who live to an old age is greater now than it was sixty years ago. 

At that time, three persons out of every one hundred were 65 years of age or older; today six persons out of every one hundred have reached the age of 65. 

We now have about 7,500,000 men and women over 65 years of age in the United States. By 1970, it is estimated that we shall have 15,000,000, or twice as many persons, 65 years of age or over. We are glad to know that so many of our people are living to a

ripe old age, but it makes us sad to know that about 1,000,000 of those who are 65 or over are being cared for by public charity. Why are so many of our old people unable to care for themselves?

The percentage of people working in factories and industries is higher today than it was several years ago. Most factory work is of such nature that young men can give better service than old men. For this reason, in many industries, men over 40 years of age are not employed. When almost everyone lived on a farm old people could usually find some type of work. About one out of twelve farmers has passed his sixty-fifth birthday. Only about one out of seventy-five cotton mill workers is 65 years of age or over. In the past, young men would go into business for themselves and, when they became old, they had the income from the business to provide a living for them. Now, many of our young workers find jobs with large corporations, chain stores, or filling stations. When they grow old, all they have is a small bank account which they have managed to save from their wages. Since wages have been low for almost everyone, not many workers have even small bank accounts. Although many people work and save part of their money, they may later lose all their savings through bad investments or during a depression. For these reasons, many old people find themselves out of work and out of money, and they must be cared for by others. 

Unemployment as a cause for insecurity. Every boy knows that if he wants a ball, a pair of skates,
