or a bicycle, he must pay the merchant for them. 

If he has no money and no one will give him any, he must get a job and work for the money with which to buy them. If he has no money and cannot earn it, he must do without the things he wants. If a father has a family and wants to supply it with food and clothing, he must work to make the money to buy these necessities. If he cannot find work, the family must go without food and clothes or these things must be furnished by others. If a person does not have enough money, or cannot earn enough with which to buy the necessities of life, he does not have security. During the "good" years of 1922 to 

1929, one person out of each twelve in this country was unemployed. During the period 1929 to 1933, approximately one person out of each four was without work. This meant that one-fourth of our people could not buy even the necessities of life. In cities, four-fifths of all the families that were on the relief rolls during the depression years were there because they could not find work. Not only does unemployment bring insecurity to those who are unemployed but, because unemployment tends to lower standards of living and morality, all of us are less secure when there are many unemployed persons in our community.

Insecurity resulting from accidents. When a person loses his eyes, his arms, or his legs in an accident, he lessens his chance for having security. A person who is permanently injured cannot do as many kinds of work as one who has had no injury.

Hundreds of people are injured each day in the United States. Many of these have to stay in bed the remainder of their lives. Others may be able to walk around but cannot work. Some of them are breadwinners whose families are depending upon them for their living. Others are members of families who are too poor to care for them. If an employee is injured while working in a factory, he can usually collect workmen's compensation insurance. 

Many accidents happen to workers at home and on the farm who usually have no compensation insurance. They must pay doctor and hospital bills, and if they are unable to return to work, they must lose their jobs besides.

Insecurity resulting from sickness. Just as accidents happen unexpectedly, sickness usually comes when we are least expecting it and when we are least prepared for it. There is no good time for a person to be sick. At any time in the year, about one person out of thirty-five is too sick to work. Not only does a sick person lose his wages while he is sick, but usually he must pay a doctor's bill. Many people are so poor that they cannot afford to pay for proper medical care. These people do not have the proper security against unemployment as a result of disease.

Lack of security for children. Children, as well as grown people, are often unsafe from the dangers of poverty. Most children depend upon the earnings of their father for their food, clothing, shelter, and doctors' bills. If the father should die, or become

ill, or lose his job, the children would suffer. There were 700,000 children on the relief rolls in 1934 because they had no one to make a living for them. 

More than forty per cent of all people on relief during the depression were children. We know that children who must stay out of school to work for a living are not likely to make the best citizens when they are grown. It takes time, attention, and money to properly rear children. They must have clothes, food, school supplies, and countless other things to make life worth while. If the child has no parents or guardians to furnish these, he is a dependent; that is, he must depend upon others outside of his family for these things. We shall see later how the government is planning to care for dependent children. 

Security in the past. Our grandfathers did not worry much about what they would do when they became old. Most of them owned their homes, farms, or places of business. They saved for misfortunes and uncertainties of the future, and usually left a small inheritance for their families. In case the father and mother had not saved enough to live on until their deaths, they lived with their children. 

Sometimes a son or a daughter would live at the home place and take care of the parents. There were a few old people whose children could not or would not take care of them. These lived in poorhouses, supported by the city, county, or state. 

Usually they were very undesirable places in which to live. When people began to work in factories, they no longer had farms or businesses upon which they

could depend for a living in old age. They had to make other plans to give them security for old age. 

Some labor unions made plans to care for their aged members. Money was raised by collecting monthly dues from all the members. Schoolteachers organized and provided group funds for teachers who were retired because of old age. In 1920, Congress made provision to care for the aged federal employees. Many private business firms started old-age retirement plans for their employees. These plans proved helpful, but, as we have seen, few old people were benefited by them. 

Employment and security in the past. Until recent years, people did not worry much about being unable to find work. If work became scarce in a community, some of the people would move into other communities, find a job, start a small farm, ranch, or business. 

There were always many new opportunities for work. Anyone who really wanted to work could find a job. Later, more and more people learned to do specialized work in factories. That is, they learned to do only one thing but learned to do it well. Now, if someone should invent a machine that would do their particular work, or if the employer should dismiss the workers, they would find it difficult to secure another job because they could do only one thing well. The modern worker's job may depend on whether someone perfects a new invention, or it may depend on the decision of the employers to reorganize the company. It may depend on some other company's making a better product and reducing

the output of the company where the worker is employed. For example, if a man is working for a company that is making automobiles, and some other company makes a better one, the people will buy the better automobile and the first company will have less work to do. In the past, a man's job depended largely on his willingness to work; now, it depends on many factors over which he has no control, and workers are less secure in their jobs now than they were several years ago. 

Security from sickness and accidents. Communities have always been afflicted with disease and sickness. Our forefathers did not have the medical care we have today, but much sickness was avoided because the people at that time did not live so close together. They spent much of their time out-ofdoors, which helped to keep them in good health. 

If a person did become sick, his work was of such nature that he did not worry so much as one would today. He did not have to worry about doctor and hospital bills because people seldom had doctors; the relatives and neighbors took care of the sick. 

The neighbors and the church looked after widows and little children. People had few accidents in those days. They were not bothered with modern hazards, such as the automobile. A person injured in an accident was cared for much as were the sick people. A feeling of insecurity from sickness and accidents is largely the result of conditions of modern times.

Our personal efforts to find security. Sickness to-

day usually works a double hardship. In the first place, a sick person loses his wages. In the second place, he usually has to pay for medical care. People who have very small incomes have more sickness than those with larger incomes. This is because they do not have proper food, clothing, and shelter. This situation is being met by various kinds of security, such as protection against loss from sickness and accidents. Some people who make low salaries are forming health groups to secure coöperative rates for hospital service. About 250,000 persons in New York

City are members of the Associated Hospital Service. If a member should become ill, the association would pay his hospital and doctor bills. This service costs each member about three cents a day. There are about 60 similar associations in the United States. Many people carry regular health and accident insurance to care for them when they are sick or injured.

There is little a person can do to make sure of his job except to work hard and to render good service to his employer. Young people can make their future jobs safer by preparing themselves to do better work.

We protect ourselves from property losses largely through insurance. This protection is given by large companies to which we make annual payments called premiums. If one has a fire or other loss, he is paid the amount for which the property was insured. In 

1936, the people in the United States paid in premiums for fire insurance over $461,160,000, and received

THE AMERICAN PEOPLE- THE TREND OF THEIR EMPLOYMENT 

1929 1932 1933 1934 1935

for fire losses $179,244,000. Premiums paid for insurance on motor vehicles amounted to more than $162,000,000, while the amount paid for losses was about $60,000,000. There are other kinds of insurance, such as tornado and theft insurance.

With insurance we try to protect our dependents and ourselves in old age. Sixty-three million, or about one-half of our people, carry some kind of life insurance. A recent report of 321 of the largest life insurance companies shows that they have paid their policy holders $2,785,000,000. This report shows also that these companies had $115,064,000,000 worth of insurance in force, which is an average of $936 for each man, woman, and child in this country. 

Government plans to help us find security. In his message to Congress on June 8, 1934, President 

Roosevelt said: 66 'Among our objectives I place the security of the men, women, and children of the nation first. This security for the individual and for the family concerns itself primarily with three factors. People want decent homes to live in; they want to locate them where they can engage in productive work; and they want some safeguard against misfortunes which cannot be wholly eliminated in this man-made world of ours."

The President appointed a committee to find out what our government could do to help us avoid these misfortunes. After several months' study this committee recommended a plan which Congress adopted in 1935. This plan is known as "The Social Security

Act of 1935", and includes old-age assistance, oldage benefits, unemployment compensation, aid to the needy blind, security for children, extension of public-health services, and vocational rehabilitation. 

1. The old-age assistance plan. The first of the old-age plans concerns persons over 65 years of age who have no children or relatives to care for them, and who are too old to work. They must have food, clothing, and shelter in order to live. According to the Federal Social Security Act of 1935, each state is invited to provide a plan to give immediate aid to all aged individuals on the basis of need. 

The federal government offers to share with the state in making monthly payments to those who are eligible and to share in paying the administrative expenses of the plan. To be eligible for aid one must be 65 years of age, be in need, and have lived within the state a certain number of years. 

The state decides how much each needy old person should receive each month. The federal government will pay one-half of the amount the state allots, provided its part is not more than $15 per month. State inspectors find out who needs aid, and determine how much money each person may receive. 

2. The old-age benefit plan. The second plan to help old people is the old-age benefit plan. This provides for a savings account to which an employed person contributes a small percentage of his wages to be kept by the federal government for him until he is 65 years of age and quits work. The employer also contributes an equal amount, based on the wages

of the employee. The state government has nothing to do with the old-age benefit plan. Under certain regulations, the benefits will be paid in monthly checks by the federal government to those who are eligible. This plan is to secure those who are working now against the time when they become old and unable to work.

Under the old-age benefit plan each eligible person, upon retirement from work, will receive a monthly payment from the federal government. The amount of the monthly check will depend upon how much the person earns in wages between the time he secures his account number and the time he is 65 years old. If he should earn only $3,000 during that period, the benefit would be $15 each month. If he should earn more, he would receive more, but no one may receive more than $85 nor less than $10 per month. No monthly payments will be made before 

1942. About 29,000,000 workers have registered with the Social Security Board and are now building up a fund to care for themselves when they become old. 

Let us see how this plan will work, based upon the Act of 1935. Payments are made in proportion to the amount earned. Suppose that in 1945 you are twenty-one, and you start working in a store for $100 per month. Your employer will deduct 2 per cent of your wages in 1945, add to it an equal amount and send the money to the United States Treasury. The next three years he will take 22 per cent of your salary each month, and after that, 3 per cent. Each time he will add an equal amount and send it all to

the government, the custodian of the fund. In 1989, you will be 65 years old. You then can quit work and receive a monthly check for $53.25 as long as you live. If you make more, you will be paid more. If you should die before you are 65, your family will receive 32 per cent of all the wages upon which you have paid contributions.

The old-age benefit plan does not apply to persons who are engaged in farm work, domestic services in a private home, work for city, state, and national governments, work for nonprofit-making organizations, such as the church and Y. M. C. A., and work on certain ships. 

3. The unemployment compensation plan. Since people are more concerned about losing their jobs than they are about growing old, our government has tried to work out a plan to care for those who lose their jobs. There are few employees in this country who have not suffered because of unemployment. Not only the workers themselves have suffered, but their wives and children as well. 

The federal government, through the Social Security Act, encourages the states to provide plans for paying workers when they lose their jobs. The federal government will pay the state the cost of the administration of its plan, but the state must provide that all benefits will be paid through public employment offices.

Our federal and state governments operate more than 1,500 employment offices where those who are unemployed may register for jobs. Efforts are made

to find work for these people. On February 1, 1938, 6,054,616 unemployed persons were registered.

Under the plan, each employer of eight or more persons on at least twenty days in each year (each day representing a different week), sends to the Secretary of the Treasury at Washington a certain per cent of his payroll. This payroll contribution amounts to 1 per cent the first year, and increases 1 per cent each year until it reaches 3 per cent in 1938, and is collected from employers in all of the states in the Union. If a state should adopt a plan to care for its unemployed, the federal government will give the state 90 per cent of the payroll tax collected from that state. The state will use this money to compensate workers who lose their jobs. It is estimated that about 18,000,000 workers will be affected by this plan. Farmers, government employees, domestic servants in private homes, nonprofit organizations, employees on certain ships, and a few others are not included in the plan. 

4. Providing aid for the needy blind. The states are giving the blind special training to prepare them for work which they can do. Even with this special training, the blind do not have so many opportunities as others. Under the Social Security Act of 1935, the federal government asked the states to make plans to give aid to the needy blind and agreed to pay a certain per cent of the administrative expense. 

If a state will provide assistance to the blind, the federal government will pay one-half the benefit up to $15 a month. For example, if the state decides to

give to each blind person $30 a month, the federal government's part will be $15. 

5. Providing security for dependent children.

There are many unfortunate children in this country who have no parents or relatives to care for them. 

Our governments and churches have established homes for many but all who need help are not in children's homes. Many widowed mothers cannot properly provide for their children, yet they do not want to send them away to children's homes. If these mothers could receive a few dollars each month from some source, they could take care of their children in their own homes and send them to school. 

The federal government, through the Social Security Board, has asked each state to provide a plan for finding and giving aid to its needy children. If the state's plan is approved by the Social Security Board, the federal government will pay one-third of the amount provided by the state plan up to $18 a month for the first child, and one-third of the amount under the state's plan up to $12 a month for each additional child in the family. It is hoped. that with this type of security no child will have to suffer for lack of food, clothing, and care. 

6. Additional provisions. The Social Security Act provides also for the extension of public-health services. Money appropriated for aid to state and local health services is distributed to the states on the basis of population, special health problems, and the financial needs of the respective states or localities. The money may be used for extending and

maintaining adequate public health programs and for investigating diseases and their prevention.

EMPLOYEE'S GNATURE 

Another provision of the Act extends and strengthens the programs of vocational rehabilitation for the physically disabled. The money which the federal government appropriated for this fund is available for certain projects through the Office of Education in the Department of the Interior. 

1. Find out how many old people in your community are not working because of their age. Tell how some of these people are being supported. 

2. Talk with some men who would like to work but have no jobs. Find out why they have no jobs. Tell the class how these people might be helped. 

3. Talk with some high school boys and girls who would like to work but can find no jobs. Why were they unable to secure work?

4. Find out if there is anyone in your community who is not secure because of an accident or sickness. 

Ask him what he thinks should be done to eliminate insecurity in cases like his. 

5. Have your father explain to you how much life and property insurance your family has. How much does this insurance cost him? 

6. Talk with some insurance man in your community and find out how much and what kind of insurance the people in your community have. Find out what this insurance is costing. 

7. Ask some employee who has a Social Security account number for old-age benefits to explain how he will benefit from the plan. Ask him to explain how he received his number. 

8. Make a list of all the people you know who have been assigned account numbers for old-age benefits. 

9. Talk with some employer of eight or more employees who is paying for unemployment insurance. Ask him to explain the plan to you. Tell the class about the plan. 

10. Consider and discuss the possibilities and problems of security in the future. Will our problems of security increase or decrease?

CHAPTER XII MEETING THE PROBLEMS OF AGRICULTURE 

Farming in the past. Farming is one of the oldest occupations of mankind.' Since the products of the soil supply food, shelter, and clothing-or the necessities of life-farming is the most important of industries. By far the greater portion of the people who settled America depended on farming for a living. The ambition to acquire more and better land for farming purposes acted as a driving force, impelling them to push on into the West. In Washington's time fully eighty per cent of the people were farmers. The others were engaged in commerce, fishing, hunting, or manufacturing, and a few in the professions.

Farming in those days was quite different from what it is today. For instance, our forefathers' main purpose in farming was to produce farm products for the use of their own families. Each farmer tried to produce enough food products for his family and his livestock. He produced his own wool, cotton, or flax to make the clothing for the family. If, after using all he needed he had a surplus, he would sell it and buy the few necessary things that he could not produce himself. The farmer of that day was almost self-sufficient. 170

Machinery and education change farming. With the invention of machinery, many people quit farming to enter factories. This caused those who remained on the farm to have a bigger market for their surplus foodstuffs and raw materials for 

Courtesy International Harvester Co. 

Machinery on the farm. clothing. Each farmer then began to produce more on his farm than his family needed. By learning better farming methods; by using better farming machinery, such as the harvester, invented by Cyrus McCormick; by building agricultural colleges and experiment farms; by farm papers; and by the great work of men like Luther Burbank in developing better plants, the farmer was able to increase the yield from his acres. He quit trying to raise products

entirely for his own use. He lost his self-sufficiency and came to depend on the market for his livelihood. 

In many instances he specialized by planting only one crop, such as cotton or wheat. His days of independence were over.

Farming during the World War. In order to understand the condition of farming today, we should study the effect the World War had upon the farmer. 

The international strife in Europe created a great demand for our foodstuffs. During the war much of the European land was left uncultivated, because all the able-bodied men were either fighting or working in factories manufacturing war supplies. The fighting forces needed bread and meat and clothing. 

Prices of farm products kept rising. After the United 

States entered the war, wheat rose to $2.60 per bushel and cotton sold for thirty-eight cents per pound. The 

American farmers' boom time had arrived. Three hundred bushels of wheat would buy an automobile. 

The army bought the farmer's mules, paying as high as $800 a pair. The farmer could buy a tractor that would never get tired like an animal. High prices caused the farmers to make big mistakes in judgment. They thought the high prices of war time would last; hence they began to buy and put into cultivation more and more land; they used their profits and borrowed money to buy more land. As a result the price of land increased. Good wheat land sold for as much as $400 per acre. The idea of producing crops in larger quantities with the hope of selling the entire production at the high price level

stimulated the farmers to greater and more extensive farming activities. 

Changes in farming after the World War. After the war the European nations resumed the cultivation of their own fields. To rebuild and improve their own circumstances, they passed tariff laws that prevented our farm products from entering their countries. Other large areas throughout the world began producing crops which competed with ours; 

Brazil, Egypt, and India produced more cotton; Russia, Argentina, Canada, and Australia produced more grain; the tropical British colonies and China and Japan produced more tobacco. Because labor costs were lower in most foreign countries, our farmers found it difficult to compete with them.

Changes in the habits of diet of the people in the United States in recent years have had an effect on farming. Our people do not eat so much bread and other starchy foods as formerly. This has lowered our use of wheat but, on the other hand, our increased use of fruits and vegetables has called for production of such foods in greater quantities. 

Effects of the depression on the farmer. About 

30,000,000 people, or one family out of four, now live on farms. The depression for the farmer started in 1920. Prices of farm products began to decrease. The farmers' share of the national income diminished. In 1919, the farmer received 18.5 per cent of the national income; in 1925, he received only 11.1 per cent, and in 1932, he obtained less than 7 per cent of the total national income. Think what this meant.

25 per cent of our population receiving only 7 per cent of the total income made in the United States! 

Let us see what effect this diminishing income had upon the farmers. Thousands of them became bankrupt and lost their farms because they could not make the necessary payments to their creditors. 

Thousands of rural banks failed. When these banks failed, farmers lost their savings, and many schools in the rural communities lost their financial support and had to close. The farmers lost their buying power and thousands abandoned their farms. This meant that the city manufacturer could no longer sell his products to the farmers in such great quantities. The farmers' inability to buy the goods produced in the city was one of the big reasons for the general depression that started in 1929. In 1932, when the depression was at its worst, our farmers were in debt almost $10,000,000,000. At that time they were receiving only 6 cents per pound for cotton and 38 cents per bushel for wheat. The future held little hope for the farmers as long as these conditions continued to exist. 

Is there an overproduction of farm products?

Many people believe that the reason the farmer is in financial distress is because he produces more than is needed. Some people tell us that he has raised too much cotton, wheat, oats, hogs, cattle, and milk, along with other farm products; that because he has produced more than we use there is a surplus; and because there is a surplus of everything he raises, the prices are low.

Another large group of people believe the farmer has not produced too much. They point out the fact that most of our people do not have incomes large enough to enable them to buy all they need. This group thinks that the farmer would not be growing

NEG. 18350-8 BUREAU OF AGRICULTURAL ECONOMICS Courtesy U. S. Department of Agriculture crops he could not sell if our people were able to buy more of the necessities of life. They offer as proof the fact that during the depression, when farmers in Minnesota were burning their wheat in furnaces because they could not sell it at a profit, people were almost starving in New York. The 

United States Department of Agriculture has prepared a chart showing what should be produced in order for everyone to have all the food he needs.

Expressed in percentages the items and the needed increases are as follows: 

Dairy cattle 76% 

Beef cattle 43% Hogs 22% 

Vegetables 204% 

Citrus fruits 51%

This chart is pointed to by those who believe that we do not have an overproduction of farm products, but that there is underconsumption. If underconsumption is our problem instead of overproduction, then the solution is to increase if possible the incomes of those who are now earning too little for their necessities. This would place greater buying power in their hands and they would thus buy more merchandise and consume more products. 

Farm lands become poorer. Another big problem confronting the American farmer today is that of soil erosion. By soil erosion we mean the washing away of the topsoil by winds and floods or heavy rains. 

Much of the topsoil of the vast farming belt that runs from Canada to Texas is slowly being carried away in great dust storms. In former years this country was covered with grass which held the soil in place. 

When the land was cultivated and the grass killed, there was no vegetation on the ground throughout the year to prevent the winds from blowing away the soil.

Another way in which the soil is being exhausted is by following a one-crop system. By this is meant planting one crop, such as cotton or corn, on the same land year after year. This practice has a tendency to exhaust certain needed plant foods found in the

soil. It has been found that if we rotate our crops, that is, if we plant a field in corn one year, in cotton the next, and in peas the third year, or in some similar rotation, the soil will keep its fertility. In fact, it has been discovered that legume crops, such as clover, peas, or soy beans, will add nitrogen, a necessary plant food, to the soil. 

The problem of tenant farming. A tenant farmer is one who rents a farm and works it for the owner. 

The owner receives a part of the crop raised and the renter receives the remainder. If the owner furnishes the seed, teams, and tools to make the crop, he usually gets half of what the tenant makes. If the tenant furnishes his own mules, tools, and seed, he usually keeps a larger portion, depending upon the kinds of crops raised.

Tenant farming is bad for the land because the average tenant does not stay on one farm over two years. He is interested in immediate profits and does not take care of the soil. He usually does not feel any pride of ownership, and as a result, often neglects the farm he is working. 

Tenant farming has been increasing rapidly during the past fifty years. Especially is this true in the cotton-producing South, where over 60 per cent of the farmers are tenants. 

What our government is doing to help the farmer. 

Solving the problem of the farmer is not an easy task. It is of such a nature that the farmer cannot accomplish much working alone. Let us see how the government and the farmer, working together, are

trying to solve some of the farmers' problems. 

In 1933, Congress passed the Agricultural Adjustment Act, sometimes called the A.A.A. In it four methods were to be used to aid the farmer. First, the government was to purchase the farmers' surplus products and distribute them among the needy. Second, it was to control, or reduce, the quantity by removing 40,000,000 acres from production. Third, the government planned to rent or purchase the poorest land and withdraw it from cultivation. And fourth, the government wanted to restore our foreign markets by trade treaties with foreign countries. 

The A.A.A. was in effect until 1936, when it was declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. 

Since the A.A.A. was declared unconstitutional, the government has continued to aid the farmer under the Soil Conservation Law passed by Congress in 1935. Under this law, the government pays a farmer for diverting a certain portion of his land from soil-depleting crops to soil-building, or soilconserving crops, such as peas or soy beans. In this way millions of acres of land are being improved for the use of future generations.

Another way in which the government is helping the farmer is by organizing Farm Loan Banks where the farmer may borrow money at low rates of interest. This plan seeks to prevent the loss of farmers' homes and is called the Farm Credit Administration (F.C.A.).

Other ways of helping the farmer. The Soil Conservation Service aids the farmer by making charts

of the various types of land in the United States. 

These charts show us what crops are best for certain lands, seasons, and climates. The departments of agriculture and the state agricultural colleges maintain experimental stations to study soil conservation, breeds of livestock and poultry, seed breeding, and The group provides valuable information concerning the weather. 

Observatories are usually located on the tops of tall buildings. control of plant diseases and insects. Their reports are furnished free to farmers through their extension service. Nearly all counties have government farm agents to render assistance.

The federal government maintains Civilian Conservation Corps camps all over the United States. 

The men in these camps aid the farmer by helping

him build terraces and drainage structures. The government has also built many huge dams throughout the United States. These dams hold immense quantities of water which may be used in irrigating fields located near the dam. Many acres of arid and semiarid land have been made very productive in this way. 

The government has undertaken also the creation of subsistence farms. These farms, usually located near cities, are cut into small areas of from three to fifteen acres. Needy families from the cities are encouraged to live on these farms, and by intensive cultivation, to grow most of the food necessary for their consumption. The head of the family is expected to find work in the near-by city to take care of the money needs of the family. There were 6,600 families in 1935 living on 62 subsistence farm projects in the United States. 

The state and national governments together sponsor the Future Farmers of America, an organization for farm boys who are in school. The membership includes over 100,000 boys in the United States, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico. Courses in agriculture are offered to teach the boys how to become good farmers, how to improve the advantages of country life, and how to be good citizens.

Farm income since 1932. Through the aid of the 

United States government in its soil conservation program, and through more scientific farming methods, the farmer's income has been increasing since 1932. The gross farm income for 1932 was about $5,333,000,000. In 1937, the income of the farmer

had increased to over $9,000,000,000. This was slightly over $300 for each person living on the farm, giving an increased buying power which has aided materially in helping all businesses and occupations. 

1. Make a report to the class on A Trip through the Country. Describe the condition of the farms, fences, and general appearance of the homes of the farmers. 

2. Make a list showing what products are grown in your community. 

3. Make an oral report to the class on what Cyrus

McCormick and Luther Burbank did for humanity. 

4. Discuss the problem of whether we have an overproduction or an underconsumption of farm products. 

5. Give a talk on what you think would be the best way of preventing soil erosion. 

6. Make an oral report to the class on The Advantages of Living in the Country. 

7. Make a chart showing all the things the government is doing for the farmer. 

8. If there is a C.C.C. camp near by, visit it and find out what the camp is doing to help the farmer. 

9. Make a list of the machines the farmer of today uses that were not available twenty-five years ago. 

10. Compare life on the farm today with that of twenty-five years ago.

CHAPTER XIII CONSERVING OUR NATURAL RESOURCES 

Our dependence upon natural resources. A group of boys went out into the woods for a week's camping trip. On the first day it was necessary to gather wood, carry water, clean and level the camp ground, and repair a fence around the camp to keep out roving animals. The boys all went to bed early the first night in order to be ready for a daylight start next morning. During the night one of them went outside the enclosure to investigate a strange noise. 

When he returned he forgot to shut the gate. He stopped for a drink of water but failed to close completely the faucet to the barrel. Then in order to find the path he lighted a match, which he carelessly tossed away while it was still burning. The boys were awakened after midnight by the noise of animals to find that hogs had come in through the gate and had eaten some of their supplies. They found that their drinking water had leaked out, and their fire-wood was burned. The carelessness of one boy had wasted their food, fuel, and water, and had spoiled the entire camping trip.

Nature has furnished us with an abundance of land upon which we can grow the materials that we need. It has given us plenty of water for every purpose for which water is used. Whether we live in 182

the country, town, or city, we must depend upon land and its forests, minerals, and water for our very existence. Without them people would perish from the earth. With less of them we should have fewer resources with which to enjoy life. Yet, we have some negligent and careless people who leave the gates open so that floods and winds may come and destroy our lands. A few are wasting our mineral wealth so that much of it will be gone in a short time. 

We see the water rushing to the sea, carrying with it the soil, and sometimes our houses and other property. Like the group of boys at camp, we shall all suffer because of the negligence and carelessness of a few, unless we prevent them from wasting our natural resources.

The nature of natural resources. Natural resources are the things upon this earth which man uses but which he did not and cannot make or create. Our most important natural resources are land, air, water, forests, the seasons, wild game, fish, and minerals. Man spends much of his time in changing the natural resources into more useful things. For example, he changes crude oil into gasoline for his automobile. With favorable seasons he produces grain, cotton, fruits, and vegetables from the soil and water. He warms the cool atmosphere in winter and cools the warm air of the summer to make his buildings comfortable. In his efforts to find comfort, conveniences, and happiness by changing natural resources, he often forgets that there is only a limited amount of these resources for his use. If we are per-

manently to enjoy what nature has given us, we must plan the careful use of our natural resources just as we plan the careful use of the money which we deposit in the bank. 

Planning the best use of our natural resources. 

One hundred years ago the people in the United States had more land, more forests, and more minerals than they could use. Just as a child with more candy than he can eat is wasteful, our people, beginning with the early settlers, have been wasteful of our natural resources. They burned the forests to clear the land for farming. They permitted the soil to wash away without making an effort to save it. 

When one farm became impoverished, they moved on westward to a new farm and started wasting more soil. In the early days, the government owned all the land beyond the frontiers. It was given to homesteaders or sold at a very low price to anyone who would buy it. Some of our best land sold for a few cents per acre. The railway companies were given millions of acres to encourage the building of railroads. Under these conditions, it was only natural to waste our natural resources. 

Many years ago President Theodore Roosevelt warned the American people that they should make plans to save the natural resources of the nation. 

He pointed out that forests should be cut only as needed, and when one tree was cut, another should be planted to take its place. He encouraged Congress to pass laws which would protect our lands, our minerals, our forests, and our waterways. Since it is

necessary for people to work in groups to conserve natural resources, we must look to the national and state governments for conservation plans. 

The uses of land. If you look about, you will see land being used for many different purposes. Our houses, streets, railroads, and parks are built on land. Indeed, approximately 7 per cent of our land surface is covered by cities, railroads, parks, and waste lands. About 38 per cent of our land is planted in crops each year. About 37 per cent is pasture land and 15 per cent is woodland. The remaining 

3 per cent is in farmsteads, lanes, and idle crop lands. This land varies in value from several thousand dollars per square foot in the centers of the large cities to practically nothing in the deserts. 

Most of our crop land is planted in wheat, corn, oats, and cotton. Much of the land in Texas, California, and Florida is used for raising fruits and vegetables. Many families in the smaller towns and cities plant gardens in which they raise fruits and vegetables. In spite of all the land in this country, there are millions of people who do not have enough vegetables, fruits, and milk for healthful living. We have not learned how to make the best use of our land.

Changing uses of land. As the people in other countries buy less of our wheat, cotton, and other products, the land on which we formerly raised these crops is used for other purposes. People use much more of some products and much less of others than they used a few years ago. You will see more fruits

and vegetables in the stores now than your fathers and mothers saw when they were children. The amount of land for recreational purposes is rapidly increasing. Nearly 350,000 acres are now used for city parks. We have nearly 75,000,000 acres of land in this country so poor that those who live on it can make only a bare living. The government has been considering the question of moving the people from poor lands to more productive acres. This poor land could then revert to forests, pasture lands, and wildlife retreats.

Land impoverishment from careless use. Originally, we had in this country about 1,903,000,000 acres of land. The number of acres has not decreased, but our land is not so productive today as it was in the past. There are two reasons for this. In the first place, the growing crops impoverish the soil by taking out certain valuable chemical elements. 

These can be replaced by the use of fertilizers, but it is not always done. In the second place, much of the surface soil is lost by erosion. The remedy is terracing, but this is frequently neglected. Because of these two destructive processes, we are raising in many instances less per acre today than our fathers raised thirty years ago.

Loss through soil erosion. Our National Resources 

Committee estimates that more than 35,000,000 acres have been completely destroyed by soil erosion, an acreage equal to 219,000 farms of 160 acres each. 

Nearly all the topsoil from another 125,000,000 acres has been washed away, leaving the land almost

worthless.

This means an additional acre lost for each man, woman, and child in the United States. 

Our annual loss from soil erosion is approximately $400,000,000, or $3 per person. 

Courtesy Tennessee Valley Authority 

Soil erosion prevention. 

The reasons for soil erosion. Much of our soil is being washed away. One reason for this is that many of the trees in our forests have been cut, and much of our grass lands have been plowed and put into cultivation. When the trees, underbrush, and grass are removed, there is nothing to keep the soil from washing away. Another reason is that the farmers have plowed up and down the hills in such a way

that the water can run down the furrows and carry away the soil.

The results of soil erosion. If, after heavy rains, water is permitted to run rapidly off the land, unchecked by forests or grass, very destructive floods sometimes result. The soil which the water carries down from the highlands settles in canals and reservoirs and fills them so that they soon lose their usefulness. Eighty-five per cent of the storage basin of the lake made by the dam at Austin, Texas, filled up with mud in 20 years. Of course, the big loss from soil erosion caused by water is the loss of the soil itself.

Loss of soil through winds. When most of the moisture is taken out of soil, it becomes light and dusty, and can easily be picked up and carried away by the wind. When the soil is extremely dry, it breaks up into very small dust particles. Even a breeze can pick up dust. When tons of these small dust particles are carried into the air there is a "dust storm". In some of the midwestern and southwestern states, particularly in parts of Kansas, Colorado, Oklahoma, and Texas, the dust storms at times become so dense that one cannot see more than a few yards. Since this dust is very light, it often drifts for hundreds of miles in the upper air currents. In 

May, 1934, dust was carried from the midwestern states eastward to the Atlantic Ocean. It was so thick that it blotted out the sun in some of the cities on the Atlantic coast 1,500 miles from where it was

An approaching dust storm in the Middle West, Spring, 1935. picked up by the wind. Larger and larger areas are being covered each year by dust from these storms. 

Reclaiming and saving the soil. Each landowner can do much to save his soil by following certain simple rules of farming. He can plow his land in such a direction that the furrows will not run up and down the slope. He can build terraces to hold back the soil that washes. He can keep grass growing on other land that washes or blows away easily. He can build check dams in the small gullies to keep them from becoming larger. These dams will also help to fill the gullies by holding back the soil that is washed down from above. 

Saving the soil by group action. Because soil washes and blows from one farm to another, from one countryside to another, and from one state to

another, the problem of soil erosion is too extensive for any one person to solve. The saving of the soil is of interest to all of us, and not merely to those who live on the land. For this reason the federal and state governments are helping to save the soil. 

County farm agents advise the farmers as to the best ways to conserve the soil. The Soil Erosion Service of the federal government coöperates with farmers in building terraces. The members of the Civilian 

Conservation Corps do much of this kind of work. 

The federal and state agricultural departments encourage the farmers to plant soil-protecting plants. 

People who live on land where nearly all of the soil is blown or washed away are assisted in moving to other farms. If worn-out land is left uncultivated, it will become covered with grass and trees and will eventually rebuild a good topsoil. 

Since 1902, the federal government has spent many millions of dollars in building dams and reservoirs. 

These are used to furnish water for the lands on which the rainfall is insufficient. Many of the fruits and vegetables which we eat are produced on this land. Without the help of the government, it would be impossible for us to have many of the foods which we now enjoy.

Water has always Nature has given The nation's water supply. been one of man's best friends. us so much of it that we hardly appreciate its real value. The sun acts as a great water pump to lift the water from the oceans. It is then distributed over our land in the form of rain and snow. Through

small streams, gullies, creeks, rivers, and subsoil waters, it finds its way back to the sea. While it is making this never-ending circle, man uses it in many ways. He uses it for health, for recreation, and for transportation of ships, boats, and barges. The plants and trees use much of it to dissolve their foods from the soil. As water tumbles over our dams and falls, it operates the machinery of mills and factories generating electricity. Water, as well as other natural resources, must be saved and used wisely to be of the greatest benefit.

Uncontrolled water a danger. If improperly handled, water can be one of man's greatest enemies. 

We have learned that it robs us of our soil if it is not protected from its stealthy grasp, and that it will wash away bridges, roads, crops, livestock, and even our houses, if we permit it to be uncontrolled. If we do not provide proper sanitary methods, diseases may be spread through impure drinking water and unsanitary bathing pools. We must plan constantly to control the rainwaters so that they will benefit rather than injure us.

Soil erosion may be prevented by proper plowing and terracing as well as by adequate drainage canals and ditches. The canals have usually been constructed by a group of people working together in drainage districts. Thousands of miles of levees have been built along the banks of rivers to protect land, property, and people in the river valleys. Dams and spillways have been built to hold in reserve a surplus water supply. States have passed laws to forbid peo-

ple from throwing waste and refuse into our streams. 

This protects those who live along the river from sickness and disease. Practically all cities and towns provide safe drinking water by using various 

Courtesy Standard Oil Company of California

Looking across the Grand Canyon. means of purification. "Old Man River" is our friend if controlled, but our enemy if left uncontrolled.

Making better use of the water supply. We are now planning the use of our water resources so that they will serve us better in four ways: (1) to make waters carry more commerce; (2) to use more water in irrigation projects; (3) to provide more recrea-

tional facilities by making reservoirs and lakes; and (4) to make water generate more electric energy. 

The extension of commerce. Where nature has provided a waterway large enough to use, materials can be shipped at less expense than on a roadway made by man. For this reason many of our rivers have been made navigable. The federal government, through the Inland Waterways Corporation, supervises a shipping or barge line on some of the largest rivers of the country, and on many canals constructed by the government. The intra-coastal canal project, now partly completed, will extend from New York to Corpus Christi. This will provide for nearly all of the Atlantic and Gulf coasts a protected waterway which does not require deepwater ports. 

Saving water for irrigation purposes. Rain does not always fall at the place or at the time it is needed. 

By building dams, it is possible to store up water during the rainy season for irrigation purposes when there is no rainfall. By storing water in the reservoirs behind the dams, it is possible to distribute it through irrigation ditches to growing crops when they need it. In 1938, about 15,000,000 acres of land were irrigated. If the water were available, about 

30,000,000 acres more could be profitably irrigated. 

Since the navigable streams are controlled by the federal government and since most irrigation projects are on these streams, the federal government constructs and supervises most of the important dams. The Roosevelt Dam on the Salt River in Arizona, the Elephant Butte Dam in New Mexico, and

the Shoshone Dam in Wyoming provide a large amount of water for use in these areas. The giant 

Boulder Dam across the Colorado River at Black

Canyon is over 700 feet high and cost approximately $165,000,000. Lake Mead, formed by the backwater from this dam, is said to be the largest artificial lake in the world. 

Using water for recreational purposes. The lakes and reservoirs formed by dams are used also for 

Using water for recreation. recreational purposes. Summer camps and cabins line the shores where people go for vacations and outings. Fishermen go to these lakes to enjoy their favorite outdoor sport. Hunters find much enjoyment there in hunting ducks and other waterfowl.

Converting water power into electric energy. 

Power to run machines and factories, and to provide heat for buildings is derived from our natural resources. Most of this power and heat are obtained from coal, gas, oil, and water. Since it is difficult to use the power directly from these sources, in order to operate such things as sewing machines, fans, and hair clippers, and to heat flat irons, curling irons, and radio tubes, it is transformed into electric energy. 

This is done by burning the coal, oil, or gas in an engine which runs a generator to create electricity. 

If a turbine wheel is placed so that water falling over a ledge or a dam may strike it with sufficient force, the turbine will operate a generator and produce electricity exactly as does the engine which burns coal, gas, or oil. Whenever we produce electricity by burning coal, oil, or gas we destroy a natural resource which we can never replace. But when we produce it by falling water we do not destroy anything, since the water flows on to the sea and is drawn up by the sun, distributed as rain, and flows down to the sea again. For this reason, the use of available water power in the production of electricity is one of the ways of conserving natural resources.

Only about one-fifth of the electric energy now used in this country is produced by water power. 

We have harnessed less than one-fifth of the water power in the United States. Private companies have developed most of our water power plants. In 1933, Congress established the Tennessee Valley Authority

(T. V. A.). One of the chief purposes of this agency is to harness the waters of the Tennessee River and its tributaries. Several dams were built and generating plants were soon in operation. Many people in the Tennessee Valley are now receiving cheap electricity from the T. V. A. The Grand Coulee plant in 

Washington and the Boulder Dam plant in Colorado 

Courtesy Tennessee Valley Authority 

Wilson Dam at Muscle Shoals, Alabama. will furnish much electricity. Many states are coöperating with the federal government in an effort to make running water do more work for us. 

Making the best uses of mineral resources. If for one year, some unseen force should prevent us from

using our mineral resources and all the things derived from them, our living conditions would resemble those of barbarians. We could not use automobiles because the metal in them comes from mines. 

The gasoline which furnishes the power to operate them is taken from oil wells. The heat which keeps us warm is derived primarily from coal and gas. Our whole transportation system depends upon minerals for operation. Not only our power, but much of our machinery, is made from minerals. Many of the articles in the schoolroom and in our homes originally came from mines, and the power to manufacture them is derived from minerals. We have become so accustomed to these everyday things of life that we seldom realize how narrow and primitive our life would be without them.

The mineral supply is not inexhaustible. We know that there is a limit to the quantity of minerals in the earth. What will our people do when the mineral supplies are gone? Whenever we burn a ton of coal, it is gone. There is no way to make a new ton of coal to take its place. If we waste a barrel of oil, we can never make another to take its place. Think of the amount of gasoline that will be burned today in all of the automobiles in the United States! Fortunately, nature was kind to us and in the beginning gave us an abundance of different minerals. We must learn how to use them wisely so that those who live after us can enjoy their advantages.

Using and saving the coal supplies. Coal has long been and still is one of our greatest sources of heat

and power. The old records of Ancient Greece and 

Rome tell us about "stones that burn". Much of our progress has been due to the materials furnished by coal fields. Coal has been so plentiful in this country that we have given little thought to careless methods of production or to wasteful habits in the use of coal. 

Thousands of companies have been mining coal. In their eagerness to compete with each other, they have used only the richest veins and wasted the others. 

Under this method of mining, when the best of the coal has been taken, the mine is closed. A closed mine is soon ruined by water. There were 4,802 mines closed between 1923 and 1932, and hundreds of millions of tons of coal were lost in them. 

The chief value of coal lies in the energy it will produce when it is burned. Coal burned in an improved machine will produce more energy than the same amount of coal burned in an out-of-date machine. It required four pounds of coal to produce one horsepower of energy in 1920. Today, due to improved machinery, one pound of coal will produce that much energy. We mined enough coal in 1934 to produce 252,106,000,000 horsepower of energy. This was about half of the total power produced in this country. This seems an enormous amount, but when we think about pulling all of the trains, running all of the factories, and operating all of the machines in this country, we realize that it takes a great amount of power and energy.

If we continue taking coal from the ground at the present rate, many of the best mines will be

exhausted in a few years. It is estimated that the great coal mines of West Virginia will last only 85 years. Nearly 22 per cent of the coal has already been taken from the mines of West Virginia and Virginia. The coal reserve in this country now is thought to be about 4,100,000,000 tons. We are losing about 150,000,000 tons a year through careless methods of mining. A coal mine caught fire in Hocking 

Valley, Ohio, in 1884. It is still burning. It has consumed more than $50,000,000 worth of coal. The government and private companies are searching for better plans for the mining and the using of coal. 

Using and saving oil supplies. As our forefathers moved into the West they often dug wells only to find salt water. Instead of finding water, some of those who dug wells in western Pennsylvania and `Ohio found an offensive smelling black substance they called "rock oil". They were disgusted because this apparently worthless material was a detriment. 

They soon learned, however, that this bad smelling oil would burn. In 1859, at Titusville, Pennsylvania, the first well was drilled for oil. Now, the oil business is one of the largest industries in this country. 

Using petroleum and its products. Although crude oil has been available less than seventy-five years, many uses for it have been discovered. Where electricity is not available, oil is the greatest source of light. The heat for many of our buildings comes from oil. Diesel engines, streamlined trains, and ocean liners secure their power from crude oil. Every wheel that turns is lubricated with an oil product.

Many of our common household articles and insecticides are made partly from oil. The 27,000,000 automobiles in use in the United States are lubricated by oil, and driven by gasoline, a product of oil. We wonder how our forefathers lived without so important a product!

Taking the oil from the ground. Oil is now being produced in twenty-three states. It is being brought to the surface through pipes in about 350,000 wells. 

Some of these wells are only a few hundred feet deep, વા 

An East Texas oil field. while others reach more than two miles below the surface of the earth. Gas also is almost always found in these underground oil pools. When the well drill breaks through into the underground reservoirs, gas and oil rush through the well pipe to the surface.

Sometimes the oil is thrown several hundred feet into the air. Such a well is known as a gusher. About 

1,000,000,000 barrels of oil are being produced each year. Most of this oil is consumed immediately, but a large quantity is stored in huge tanks. These tanks are not always located at the source of supply, but are often found at central points of distribution. 

Making gasoline from crude oil. Crude oil, or oil as it comes from the ground, is not suitable for use in automobiles. It must first pass through a refinery where the gasoline, lubricating oil, and other products are extracted from it by a heating process. An improved and more effective method is the cracking process, by which the crude oil, under a pressure of 300 lbs. per square inch, is heated to a temperature of 950° Fahrenheit. Under this method about 80 per cent of the crude oil, depending upon quality, becomes gasoline in the refining process. There are about 600 refineries in the United States with a daily capacity of more than 6,000,000,000 barrels. Twentyseven million automobiles consumed 17,234,000,000 gallons of gasoline in 1934. This is about 900 gallons of gasoline for each car. The great consumption of gasoline explains why there are more than 300,000 filling stations in this country. 

How long will our crude oil last? We do not know exactly when all the crude oil will be consumed. We do not know definitely how much there is left under the ground. We do know that the more than 2,000 oil fields in the United States have an oil reserve of about 13,300,000,000 barrels. New wells are pro-

ducing about 600,000,000 barrels each year. At the rate we now are using oil, the known supply will last us only about fifteen years. 

The conservation of oil. One of the greatest losses in oil production is caused by wasting the gas which helps in taking the oil from the ground. Another great waste in oil has been caused by drilling too many oil wells, and the producing of more oil than was needed. To do away with this cause of waste, some states have passed proration laws. A proration law is one which regulates the quantity of oil each well is allowed to produce each day. For example, a man might own a well which could produce 10,000 barrels a day. Under the proration law he might be allowed only 100 barrels a day. This means that he must regulate the valves at the well so that only 100 barrels will flow from it in one day. If the well owner produces more oil than the law allows, this extra oil is known as hot oil. The federal government forbids the shipping of hot oil from one state to another. 

The uses of natural gas. In many places in the earth gas only is found by drilling; and in other fields gas and oil are found together. Gas, being lighter than oil, goes to the highest point in the reservoir. One of the chief uses of this gas is the assistance it renders in bringing oil to the surface, so that pumps are unnecessary. The gas under the ground exerts a great pressure on the oil, and drives it out of the pools into the wells, and up through the wells to the surface.

Gas is a very convenient fuel for heating and cook-

ing purposes. Gas pipe lines extend all over the country and supply towns and cities hundreds of miles away from the gas field. These pipes carry the gas from the wells to the homes of the users. 

The waste of gas. If one should pass near an oil field at night, he would see great flames leaping high into the air. These flames are called flares. They are caused by the burning gas which the owners of oil wells are permitting to escape. In many oil pools this gas is used for repressure, by returning it through a key well (one for the field or group) to the inside of the earth, where it pushes more oil to the surface, but in other oil fields it is allowed to escape into the air. In the Panhandle gas field in 

Texas more than 1,000,000,000 cubic feet of gas is escaping as waste into the air every day. Each year there is enough gas wasted in this one field to equal the heat energy contained in 62,634,000 barrels of oil. 

If that many barrels of oil were placed side by side, they would make a solid belt around the earth. Similar waste is occurring in other gas and oil fields. At the present rate of wastage our supply of natural gas probably will not last many years. The legislatures in some states are making laws to reduce the great waste of natural gas. Research is going on continually in the laboratories of scientists, refineries, and universities in an endeavor to reduce waste and increase the output of the refined products. 

Making the best uses of forests. The forests are necessary to us because they furnish timber. About 

65 per cent of the timber that is cut is used for

lumber. About eight per cent is used for wood pulp. 

The books you read and study and the daily newspapers are made from this wood pulp. About thirteen per cent of the timber is used for fuel. The remainder is made into various wood products. 

About two per cent of the forests is lost each year because of fires, and about six per cent is destroyed by insects, disease, and drought.

Most people think of forests as being valuable only for timber, but they serve us in many other ways. One of the great benefits derived from forests is the prevention of soil erosion. They also help to hold back water from big rains and melted snows and thus prevent floods. Birds in great variety and most of the wild animals, some of them fur bearing, are found in wooded areas. Recreational opportunities are increased if timber land is available. More than 35,000,000 people enjoy our national forests each year. Woodlands serve as windbreaks and as an aid in the prevention of wind erosion. 

The conservation of forests. When the Pilgrim 

Fathers landed in this country, about one-half of what is now the United States was covered with forests. Since that time saws and axes have been busy felling trees. Today, timber is being cut down about four times as rapidly as it is being replanted. 

In order to save some of the timber lands for future generations, the federal government has established about 150 forest reserves containing about 162,000,000 acres of land. Timber can be cut on these lands only under the supervision of govern-

ment agents. It is always cut in such a way as to avoid waste. For each tree that is used another is planted to take its place. All trees on these forest reserves are guarded against fires, pests, and tree diseases. More than 4,000 people are continuously employed in protecting the national forests for us. 

The states, as well as the federal government, are attempting to save the forests. Nearly every state in the union has laws to protect its forests. More than 

15,000,000 acres have been set aside by the states as forest reserves. It is a mark of good citizenship for every person to help the government protect our forests. We can be of the greatest aid by preventing forest fires.

Making the best uses of fish and wild game resources. When our grandfathers were boys, they spent much of their time in hunting in our fields and forests, and fishing in our streams. At that time the streams were filled with fish, and wild game could be found almost anywhere. Small game, such as rabbits and squirrels, and wild birds, such as plover and quail, were found in abundance. Bear, deer, and other big game were found in many places, and the western prairies were covered with great herds of buffalo. The birds and animals could not protect themselves from the guns, traps, and hunting dogs of man. So much of the bird and game life has been killed that in many places little or none remains. 

Present efforts for conservation. Not until a few years ago did some of our citizens begin to realize that we were about to destroy all fish and wild game.

Courtesy International Newsreel Photo 

The "Singing Tower" located at Mountain Lakes, Fla., presented to the

American people by Edward W. Bok of Philadelphia.

They saw people selfishly killing birds and wild game without limit, merely for sport and pleasure. They saw that as people cleared more land and built more homes over the country the wild animals would have Land used for grazing purposes is not often subject to wind erosion. few places to go. These good friends of birds and animals began to form groups and make plans to protect and develop our wild game. As a result of their activities, we now have several million acres of land set aside by the federal and state governments as sanctuaries where birds and wild animals may live and grow, unharmed by hunters. The federal government prohibits people from killing migratory birds except for a short time each year. All states have fish and game laws which prohibit people from needlessly destroying these friends of man. Every

person who hunts and fishes ought to know and obey these laws.

1. Make a list of the things you use daily that are classified as natural resources. 

2. Find out the price per acre of different sections of land near your community. What are the chief forces that control these prices? 

3. Compare the farming in a large country like the 

United States with the methods used in some small foreign country, such as Holland or France. Why are these methods so different? 

4. Talk to a farmer in your community who has made some effort to prevent soil erosion, either through his own ideas or with the aid of the government. Report your conversation to the class. 

5. Visit the source of water supply for your community as well as the filtration plant. How is the water purified? 

6. Make a study of the various means of irrigation practiced throughout the country. 

7. Prepare an oral talk, supplemented by pictures if available, about one of the large federal dams that have been built recently or are under construction. 

8. Make a list of things operated by electricity in your home and community that you have observed. 

9. If you have ever visited an oil or gas field, an oil refinery, or a coal mine, tell the class what you saw. 

10. What are some of the important game laws which affect your community? Fishing laws? 

11. Talk with a local printer and learn where he secures his various kinds of paper, of what they are made, the cost, and how transported. 

12. Have the class name as many migratory birds as it can.

CHAPTER XIV MEETING THE PROBLEMS OF INDUSTRY 

Industry in the home. If you had lived about one hundred and seventy-five years ago, you would have been one of a family group that produced nearly everything used by the family. Your home would have been a veritable factory. Your mother and sisters would have made all of your clothes by hand, spinning the thread on a spinning wheel, and weaving it into cloth with a hand loom. They would have made your moccasins and shoes out of deer hides brought in by your father. Your caps would have been made of coon skins. Your father and brothers would have made the furniture out of wood cut from the forest. You would have saved tallow to make candles. Your family would have needed to buy only iron products, such as kettles, or guns, and perhaps some tea, or lead, and powder. If you had lived in or near a town, your father would have traded the surplus cloth, shoes, and candles for something that could not be made at home.

We learn to use machinery. About the time of the 

American Revolution, machines that changed all of our ways of making a living were invented in England and elsewhere. Working quietly in small shops, inventive minds were able to bring about more farreaching changes in world history than all the gen- 209

erals and statesmen combined. James Watt invented the steam engine and gave us a source of tremendous power with which to run our factories. Hargreaves, Arkwright, and Cartwright gave us inventions that made possible the production of cloth in great quantities. Eli Whitney, an American schoolteacher, invented the cotton gin in 1791. Steam for propelling boats and trains was applied by Fulton, Stephenson, and others. The Industrial Revolution was under way.

Changes brought about by the use of machinery. 

The invention and use of machinery brought about great changes in the daily life of the people. Let us Machines require control.

study a few of these changes in order to understand some of the problems of industry today. 

The beginning of the factory system. One of the first changes brought about by the invention of power machinery was to transfer the work of manufacturing from the home to the factories. Naturally, no one wanted to live in a house cluttered up with machinery. On the other hand, since the rooms in the home were not large enough to accommodate machinery, larger houses, called factories, were built. 

Increased power of production. The invention of machines greatly increased man's productive power. 

By tending a machine, a man was able to produce hundreds of yards of cloth in a day where formerly the output had been very small. Clattering and clanging machinery, driven by steam or water power, enabled workmen to produce factory-made products with which slow hand-made goods could not compete in quantity. This increased production lowered the manufacturing cost of goods, and, therefore, the selling price.

Greater purchasing power. When products became cheaper people could afford to buy more. Since factory-made products became cheaper and more desirable, old hand-industries gradually disappeared. 

People began to specialize in one type of work or occupation. Some raised cotton for the factories to manufacture into cloth; others raised food for the workers in the factory and factory towns; some taught school or practiced medicine; while still others moved near to the factory and secured em-

ployment in operating machines. The days of homeproduction were over. 

The growth of cities. Since the greater part of transportation in the early days was by boat, the towns of that time usually grew up near a good harbor or a navigable river. With the introduction of machinery, factories were established in these towns because existing transportation routes furnished means of securing raw materials as well as an outlet for manufactured goods. This centralizing of industry and transportation led to a transfer of population. People moved into factory towns to secure employment and to establish businesses. A study of the United States census reports shows clearly how the Industrial Revolution caused the growth of cities. Detroit, Michigan, and Chicago,

Illinois, were mere frontier settlements one hundred years ago. Now, millions of people live in these cities as a result of factories and trade. The growth of large cities has created many social problems for us, such as those concerning housing, crime, health, education, recreation, transportation, and water supply. In fact, many of the social problems that confront us today are found largely in the cities. 

The concentration of wealth. Such things as land, cattle, grain, wool, and cotton, formerly composed the greater part of man's possessions; and the early factory was usually a small business undertaken by one man or perhaps by a partnership composed of two or three persons. When these small partnerships grew into corporations business began to increase

and wealth to multiply. The large corporations, through the use of machinery in factories, have in many cases produced wealth for their stockholders. 

How are corporations organized, and how are they responsible for the production of wealth? Suppose a person owning a shoe shop wishes to organize a corporation to manufacture shoes; he would apply to his state government for a charter of incorporation. 

This charter would specify the rules and regulations governing his corporation. After receiving his charter, he could sell stock in his company to anyone who would buy it. If the company should be incorporated for $50,000 he could sell stock certificates up to that amount. After raising the necessary money he would build a factory and begin to manufacture shoes. 

Everyone who had bought stock in the company would share in the profits in proportion to the amount of stock he owned. As a rule, a part of the profits are reserved to take care of poor business periods and in order to enlarge the factory or widen its field of work. We have several corporations in the 

United States today, each of which controls over $1,000,000,000 worth of property. These corporations buy and sell goods all over the world. 

Our dependence upon one another. We have only to look around us today to see how dependent we are on other people. Consider your own home, your clothes, and your food: the stove that cooks your meals was probably manufactured in Ohio; your bed in 

Michigan; your refrigerator in New York; your shoes in St. Louis; and your scarf in Czechoslovakia. For

breakfast, perhaps, you had cereal from Michigan made from North Dakota wheat, coffee from Brazil, fruit from Texas, California, or Florida, bacon from 

Chicago, Kansas City, Omaha, or Ft. Worth, and eggs from some farm near your home, while your milk came from still another source. We are all dependent upon someone else for most of the necessities of life. If the community in which you live should be completely separated from the outside world for a few weeks, many people would probably die of starvation. During the Civil War the South, through a blockade, was deprived of foreign trade; and as a result, the people had to do without many necessities of life, such as tea, coffee, quinine, glass, and iron products. The South suffered greatly because of specialization in both agriculture and industry.

Present day industrial problems. There are many problems of industry confronting us today that have not been solved to the satisfaction of everyone and to the best interests of society. One of the greatest problems confronting the nation is that of unemployment, or providing work for every able-bodied adult. 

About ninety people out of each one hundred in 1790 lived on the land and provided the food, clothing, and shelter necessary for the population. The ten others provided health service, banking, transportation, trade, and commerce, and the cultural services, such as education and music. Today, through the use of machinery, probably twenty persons out of each one hundred can supply the basic necessities. This

leaves eighty per cent to supply all other services. 

A few men operating machines can make 8,000 automobile frames per day. An automatic machine for making electric light bulbs has a capacity of over 150,000 bulbs every twenty-four hours. It is said. that each of these machines does the work of 994 men. 

Automatic machines are taking the place of many workers in nearly all industries. Plants that formerly employed thousands of men can now be run by a few hundreds. What is the meaning of this?

It probably means that the service occupations such as recreation, retail trade, law, medicine, nursing, teaching, and other public services, must expand in order to absorb the workers deprived of employment by automatic machinery.

There are some people who would try to solve the problem by destroying our machines. Others think that would be trying to turn back the wheels of progress. Some think that a better way would be to reduce the working hours, so that more people would have employment. Workers would then have more time for recreation and more people would be required in the service occupations to supply their needs. Unemployment is a question that cannot be solved in a year's time. It is a challenge to the best minds of America. We realize how great a challenge it is when we consider the millions of people still unemployed in the United States in 1939. 

Distribution of income. The National Resources 

Committee made a study of consumer income in the United States for the year 1935-1936. The report

of this study, made in September, 1938, showed the average family income to be $1622. The poorest third of our population received only $471 per family, the middle third $1076 per family, and the highest third $3000 per family. The richest one-half per cent received about as much of the national income as the lowest one-third. Farming and wage-earning families received an average of $1284 per year.
