The salaried business group received about $4200 and the professional group about $6700 per year. Incomes are lower in the South than in the North. The national income of approximately $59,000,000,000 in 1935-36 increased to $64,000,000,000 in 1937-38. 

Many people think that the ultimate goal of society is the distribution of wealth in a way that will enable everyone to have an adequate amount of good food, in order to insure strength and health; to build a home equipped with modern facilities, such as bath rooms, running water, electric lights, standard heating facilities, and proper ventilation; and to buy enough clothing to insure warmth and a feeling of dignity and well-being. They think also that everyone should have a moderate amount left to provide for the buying of books, magazines, and other educational aids, and such recreational activities as tennis, golf, swimming, camping, hunting, fishing, travel, and motion picture shows. How much healthier, happier, and better satisfied our people would be with the realization of this ideal! 

Government aid to industry. The question of how far our government should go in passing laws to help

industry is a perplexing one. One of the ways in which the government has aided the manufacturing industry is by passing tariff laws. A tariff law is a tax placed on an article coming into the United States from a foreign country. This tax secures revenue for An industrial plant employs many people. the government, and it protects the American manufacturers and factory workers against the low-priced goods made by foreigners who have a lower standard of living than our workers. However, this tax or tariff usually causes an increase in the prices of the articles thus protected. 

The government, acting through the Reconstruction Finance Corporation (R. F. C.), kept many industries from becoming bankrupt during the period of the depression by lending them money. 

From February, 1932, to July, 1933, the government

loaned to American industries over $1,900,000,000. 

Business improvement has allowed industry to repay most of these loans.

There are many ways in which our government is aiding industry. Patent laws protect the inventor so that others may not deprive him of the benefits of his work. The Bureau of Standards maintains experimental laboratories whose bulletins and reports are made available to industry. It also sets up standard specifications for many products. The 

Department of Labor helps industry by furnishing statistics on the volume of employment and its changes. The Bureau of Foreign and Domestic Commerce and the Consular Service help industries to find markets in foreign countries. The Bureau of 

Mines is trying to discover better and more economical methods of mining. The government is constantly striving to settle disputes between capital and labor. 

Government control of industry. How far the government should go in controlling industry today is an important question. Some people think our government should not impose any restrictions on industry, while others would go so far as to have industry under complete control of the government. 

Our government has followed the policy of establishing only such control over industry as seemed necessary for the best interests of society as a whole. 

It has established the right to determine railroad and truck-line rates in order to protect the small shipper from unfair discrimination in rates. It has set up laws controlling the liquor traffic.

Congress has recently enacted laws concerning the hours, wages, and working conditions of labor. These laws recognize the right of the labor unions to bargain collectively in these matters. They also provide means of settling disputes between workers and their employers through mediation and arbitration boards. The National Labor Relations Board tries to prevent unlawful or unfair practices which interfere with the right of group or collective bargaining. 

The Pure Food and Drug Act was designed to guarantee pure, unadulterated foods, and pure, trustworthy medicine. Because of lack of funds and legal authority, the law is only partially enforced. 

The government has established control over the selling of securities, such as stocks and bonds. This control prevents the selling of worthless securities to unsuspecting people. There are many other ways in which the government has set up controls over various industries designed for the protection of the best interests of all the people. 

The rapid development of transportation and communication in recent years has made world-wide the activities of many industries. This creates new problems of government in its relation to industry, and requires coöperative action among the nations. 

Labor organizes to protect its interests. Before the days of the large corporations the owners and laborers usually worked side by side in the small shops. They knew each others' problems and a common bond of interest united them. Labor troubles or causes for labor disputes were scarce.

In the large modern factory there are three distinct groups responsible for the prosperity of the factory: the stockholders or owners, many of whom live in other sections of the country and have never seen the factory; the management, responsible to the owners for the efficient operation of the plant; and the workers, who do the actual labor of producing the goods. The management, in order to make a profit for the owners, operates the plant as economically as possible. This means that labor and materials are secured at the lowest possible costs. 

Workers, in order to protect their interests, have organized into unions. A labor union has two main purposes; first, to secure more money for the commodity its members have to sell, that is, their labor; and second, to secure better working conditions, such as shorter hours, better ventilated factories, and the elimination of machine hazards, such as open belts, unguarded machinery, and other dangers. One method used by labor in securing what it wants is to strike. When the members of a labor union in a factory go on strike, it means the workers refuse to work until the management of the factory meets their demands. They also try to discourage anyone else from taking their places and this often leads to violence and destruction of life and property. A big strike that lasts several weeks always causes losses in production that amount to many millions of dollars. In that way a strike is injurious to the general public. One of the unsolved problems today is to

find some way to adjust the disputes between capital and labor that will be fair to all. 

1. If there is a factory close by, visit it, and notice the labor-saving machinery and safety devices. Make a report to the class on your trip. 

2. Make a list of the articles you use each day for which you are dependent upon someone else. 

3. Make a list of the articles in daily use in your home which are made by some member of your family. Compare with No. 2 above. 

4. Prepare a paper, listing all the ways in which our government has controlled business. 

5. Prepare an oral discussion to be given in class on How to Distribute the National Income More Equally. 

6. Debate: Resolved: That our Federal Govern ment should not establish any control over private business. 

7. What is being done in your community to solve the unemployment problem? 

8. Prepare a chart showing some of the unsolved problems we have today in industry. 

9. Debate: Resolved: The specialization of man's effort is more profitable to him than diversified effort. 

10. Investigate the most recent strike in your section of the country. What were the causes? How long did it last? What were the results?

CHAPTER XV MEETING THE PROBLEMS OF COMMUNICATION AND TRANSPORTATION COMMUNICATION 

Communication in olden times. The first important advance in communication was man's development of language. He did this by using signs, gestures, and guttural sounds; and at first his vocabulary contained only a few hundred words. Language has continued to grow throughout the ages. New words are being adopted all the time. After man learned to communicate by the spoken word he discovered the necessity of writing. The invention of the alphabet allowed man to record his thoughts and actions not only for his own sake but for the benefit of future generations. Language, or communication by means of the written word, has been the greatest of civilizing forces. In early days messages were carried by runners or signaled by drum beats. The African tribes still use drums as one means of communication. We are going to see how man has made discoveries and invented instruments that quicken his means of communicating with others. 

Speeding up the use of the written word. Early writing was done by hand; and, in some backward countries of Asia today, scholars do a profitable business by writing letters for people who cannot write. 222

Paper making and the art of printing were first developed by the Chinese, a people considered nonprogressive by some today. They made paper by pounding fibrous plants to a pulp, which was then pressed into thin sheets. The Egyptians used the papyrus plant in making their paper. Today, our modern paper factories use wood pulp for paper making. The wood is reduced to a pulp by grinding, and it is then mixed with water and run between great rollers, from which it comes out in continuous rolls of paper. The manufacture of paper is consuming more and more of our timber resources. Canada supplies us with a large part of the wood we use in making paper. Recently, a method of making white paper from pine was discovered, and several large paper mills have been established near pine forests in the South. 

Gutenberg and his printing press. John Gutenberg, a German, is given credit for inventing, in the year 1450, the first movable type printing press.

Before that date books and papers had been printed from wooden blocks, each engraved by hand. By using Gutenberg's press a strong man could make fifty or sixty imprints in an hour, which was a great advance for that time. Books and papers printed in those days were very expensive, and for that reason only the wealthy could afford them. 

Inventors in the field of printing have tried to accomplish three things with their inventions: faster printing, cheaper printing, and more printing. As books, magazines, and newspapers have been made

cheaper by inventions and discoveries, their use and number have increased. Let us see what changes have occurred in the art of printing since the time of Gutenberg. 

Improving our printing machines. In 1836, David 

Bruce, Jr., of New York, patented the first typecasting machine, which made type out of metal. Type making was formerly done by hand. In 1847, Richard 

Hoe invented the first rotary press. In this kind of press the types are set on a curved surface, or cylinder, and the paper is rolled over it. In 1886, Ottmar 

Mergenthaler invented the first linotype machine. 

Before this time all type had been set by hand, a very slow and expensive process. Henry Barth of Cincinnati invented a machine in 1888 that turned out 200 types a minute. Today, as a result of the inventions in the field of printing, magazines and newspapers can be produced so rapidly and so cheaply that everyone can buy them. One great Philadelphia magazine plant produces on its 120 presses each month more than 12,000,000 magazines. Each of these magazines consists of between 100 to 200 pages and twenty mail-cars are required to carry the daily output.

The development of a postal service. With the development of language and the printed word, it was found necessary to provide means of carrying letters, magazines, and newspapers. The various postal systems are the result. Before our postal system was organized, letters were carried by private individuals. Of course, the rates were high and the

service undependable. The receiver had to pay the postage, which was usually ten to twelve times as much as we pay today. Adhesive postage stamps, which transferred the cost from the receiver to the A representative of the group brings our mail. sender of the letter, were first used in 1847. Local free delivery had been started in the cities of the North during the Civil War and by 1896 it had become a nation-wide service, both in the city and in the country. Today, mail is carried by the principal air lines, and a letter can now travel from New York to China by regular air line in one-fourth of the time it required a few years ago. A special air mail

stamp is provided for letters sent by plane. The postal system is one of the greatest services the government maintains for our benefit. 

The telegraph is a quick means of communication. 

The story of the telegraph reads like a fairy tale. 

Samuel F. B. Morse, the inventor, was an artist. In 

1834, he became interested in the work of inventing the telegraph, neglected his work of painting, and lacking funds as a result, he often went hungry.

In 1837, he patented his first electro-magnetic telegraph, but since he was so poor, he could not develop his invention. Although he asked the Congress of the United States to appropriate $30,000 to construct an experimental line from Washington to Baltimore,

Congress considered his invention a "scientific toy" and refused his request. In 1843, his bill was again introduced in Congress and after a bitter debate it was passed by a small majority. One senator who opposed the bill said it would be better to use the money to build a railroad to the moon. This illustrates the non-progressive attitude that some people have taken toward many of the inventions we now enjoy. With this $30,000 Morse and his friends began the construction of the line from Washington to Baltimore. Unfortunately, they wasted about twothirds of the money in trying to construct an underground line. The insulation on the underground wires proved defective. Ezra Cornell, who later founded Cornell University, rendered much assistance to Morse. He suggested stringing the wires on poles, with bottle necks for insulators. The line was

completed and ready for the first test on May 24, 1844. The first message Morse sent over the wire was: "What hath God wrought!" Morse eventually made a fortune from his patent rights.

Cyrus W. Field conceived the idea of laying telegraph wires, a cable, under the Atlantic Ocean, thus connecting the Old World with the New. After many failures and disheartening experiences, finally he succeeded in 1866 in laying a cable from America to 

Europe. Had this cable been completed before 1815, the battle of New Orleans would never have been fought. The United States and England had signed a peace treaty two weeks before the battle, but the news had not arrived at New Orleans.

The telephone carries our voice long distances. 

In 1876, Alexander Graham Bell perfected the telephone, an invention that would send our voices over a wire. Bell was a teacher of deaf children in 

Boston when he became interested in electricity. 

He experimented several years before he built a telephone that would transmit the voice. A dead man's ear, secured from a doctor, was used at one stage in his experiments to learn how sound vibrations are transmitted. His telephone proved to be the most popular of all the exhibits at the Philadelphia Centennial Exposition.

The next invention necessary for the widespread use of the telephone was a central station to connect the lines; and when this was accomplished the use of the telephone grew rapidly. Now, in many places, automatic exchanges are used to give us our numbers.

Today, the telephone is regarded as a necessity. 

The radio brings us news and entertainment. If you call your friend over the telephone, you are talking over a wire; but when you "tune in" on your favorite radio program, you are not connected by wire with the broadcasting station, yet you hear someone talking or singing as if he were in the room with you. When and where and by whom was this wonderful invention created? The man given credit for inventing the radio was Guglielmo Marconi, a twenty-two year old Italian. His first machine in 

1896 would send signals only a short distance, but A representative of the radio station broadcasts a program.

he found that by elevating the sending wire, he could increase the range. By 1897, he was able to send telegraphic signals ten miles. By 1901, he, with others, had so improved his instrument that he was able to send messages across the Atlantic Ocean.

Navigation companies and the navies of the world quickly adopted his invention and, as a result, many lives have been saved from shipwreck. Now the use of the radio, or wireless telegraph, is universal. At the time of his death in 1937, Marconi was working on experiments with the view of making television practical.

Lee De Forest, young American radio engineer, invented the vacuum grid tube. This invention allowed the receiving set to magnify sound signals several million times, which eventually led to the transmission of speech over the radio. 

Today, we sit in our living room and listen to the 

President address us from Washington, or we hear a symphony orchestra playing in St. Louis, or perhaps a comedian in Los Angeles. The whole world of news and entertainment is at our finger tips.

In recent years machines have been constructed to send pictures, called telephotos, by wire. As a result of this invention, we can now pick up our morning paper and see pictures of events that happened the day before in some far-away land.

TRANSPORTATION 

How our forefathers traveled in olden times. 

Man's first means of travel was by crawling or walk-

ing. A great step forward was made when he trained horses and oxen, and perhaps camels, to help him move about. From a hollow tree he made a canoe which enabled him to travel upon the water. When the first settlers came to America, they had learned to use sails on their boats and had learned the use of the wheel for land transportation. The Indian's 

Ellison Photograph 

How our grandparents traveled. only beast of burden had been the dog. The white man brought his horses and oxen. He settled at first along the rivers so that he could transport his goods by boat. Later, on foot and horseback, he followed Indian trails into the West, and, finally, the trails were widened and became wagon roads. 

Private companies began the construction of turnpikes, and charged "toll" of all travelers who used

them. The first turnpike was from Philadelphia to 

Lancaster, Pennsylvania, a distance of sixty-six miles. Travel then was much more costly than now. 

Steam as an aid to transportation. To an American goes the honor of first using the expansive power of steam in transportation. Robert Fulton had an ambition to become an artist. While studying art in Paris, he became interested in trying to use steam in water transportation. He built a steamboat on the Seine 

River, but the engine was so heavy that it sank the boat. After returning to America, he built a steamboat in New York in 1807, and named it the Clermont. 

The boat made the first trip up the Hudson River from New York to Albany in 32 hours. That was rapid transportation then, but slow when compared with the speed of our ocean liners today, which travel from New York to Liverpool in a little more than four days. 

Steam was applied to land transportation in

America about 1831. The first railroad lines were built very crudely. Fifteen miles per hour was fast for the early trains. The brakes were operated by hand and since they were not very effective, there were many wrecks. A young man, George Westinghouse, invented the effective airbrake shortly after the Civil War and immediately the frequency of railroad accidents was lessened.

After the Civil War the growth of railroads was rapid. The national government gave transportation companies large grants of land for building railways. 

By 1900, the country was connected by a great net-

work of railroads. Today, our 500 railroad companies have 250,000 miles of track. There is about $26,000,000,000 invested in our railway systems. 

It is indeed a large business. 

BULU SOFTULAN MAILWAY SUPER CAROLINA 

KAS, BOAR 

Courtesy Southern Railway System 

The first American railway train, operated between Charleston, S. C. and Augusta, Ga.

The railroads are confronted with some serious problems. The competition of the automobiles, truck lines, bus lines, and airplanes is reducing the volume of railroad business. Some lines cannot operate at a profit because of this competition. Railroads are trying to meet the competition by offering better service and in many cities a door-to-door freight delivery is furnished. Lighter, faster, and cleaner trains are taking the place of the slow and dirty trains of yesterday. Air conditioning is becoming a part of the standard passenger equipment. More efficient financial management is solving the problem of bankrupt railway companies. It is hoped that their problems may be solved satisfactorily for there

is a definite need for railroads in our system of transportation.

Electricity an aid in transportation problems. One of the greatest passenger transportation problems we have is found in the large cities. In New York City alone, over 1,000,000 people must travel from their homes to their work each day. Transporting such a large number of people is an immense problem. 

Courtesy of Baltimore and Ohio Railway

A modern train-The Royal Blue. 

New York has met the problem by building its car lines on three levels. The subways are placed under

the ground; the surface cars operate on the ground level; the elevated tracks are built above the ground. 

Without the invention of the electric car these types of transportation would have been difficult, if not impossible.

Horse-drawn cars supplied the first public transportation in the cities. Experiments by Doctor Werner Siemens in Germany, George Green, Stephen D. 

Field, and Thomas A. Edison in the United States, soon gave us the modern trolley car. Some railroads have part of their lines electrified through mountains where electricity can be produced cheaply by water power. The use of electricity in transportation has solved many traffic problems. 

The use of the gasoline engine by individuals. Our grandfathers' usual way of traveling was by horseback or in the family carriage or wagon. This means of travel was slow and the average distance traveled from home was not very great. The first mechanical carriage built in America was in 1825 and was run by steam. This method of travel did not prove very successful because of bad roads. The invention of the first gasoline automobile is credited to two Germans, Daimler and Benz, in 1872. Charles E. 

Duryea built the first successful gasoline automobile in America in 1891. The United States, with its great natural resources, soon took the lead in making automobiles, and today American-built machines are used in all parts of the world. In 1900, there were about 8,000 automobiles in the United States. In 

1938, the number of motor cars had grown to more

than 26,000,000, or about one automobile for each five persons in the United States. This tremendous increase in motor transportation has created a large number of problems, many of which are still unsolved. The question of safety is one of our greatest problems. This problem has been discussed at length in Chapter IX. 

The automobile creates a need for good roads. 

With the increased use of the automobile there came a demand for better roads. Both the United States government and the state governments are helping to solve this problem. Different kinds of taxes, including licenses, gasoline, and oil taxes, are being used to raise money for highway construction. Over $1,300,000,000 was collected from these sources in the United States in 1937. By 1938 we had over 

3,000,000 miles of highway, a sufficient amount to encircle the earth at the equator more than 120 times. 

The roadbuilding program is an important one, on which the expenditure of tax money is greater than anything else except education. This work should have the attention and the service of the best equipped officials the state can procure.

Air travel as a means of transportation. On December 17, 1903, at Kitty Hawk, North Carolina, occurred an event that meant much to mankind. On that day

Wilbur Wright successfully operated the first airplane. He and his brother, Orville, who built the plane, formerly had been bicycle makers in Dayton, Ohio. Their first successful plane was an improvement on the machines previously built by Maxim and

Langley. Since that date, the use of the airplane has increased steadily. Charles A. Lindbergh made his world's record solo flight across the Atlantic in 1927. 

In 1936, the China Clipper flew from San Francisco to Manila, a distance of 8,100 miles.

Today, airlines connect all the larger cities. The 

United States government aids the airlines in several 

Air travel at night. ways. It pays a high rate for air mail transportation. 

It provides federal routes with beacon lights to enable flyers to steer the right course. It also furnishes radio beam equipment for "blind" flying and is working on experiments to perfect equipment which will enable flyers to land "blind". The United

States Bureau of Air Commerce provides radio communication for all flying planes. This bureau furnishes also reports on weather conditions and emergency landing fields.

Much has been done in recent years to reduce the hazards of flying in our large transport planes. The airplane has a good safety record when compared with the private automobile, but it is not so good as that of the railroads. 

Applying steam and gasoline to water transportation. Immediately after Fulton built the Clermont, there was a rapid growth in the use of steamboats. 

But the period between the Civil War and the World 

War saw a great decline in American shipping.

The war taught us the value of owning our own merchant marine and the government began to encourage the building of ships. In 1914, we had only 17 ships in foreign trade, while today we have about 500. Our dock facilities for foreign trade are worth about $1,000,000,000. These docks employ over 200,000 men.

American shipping is confronted with some difficult problems. Perhaps the greatest is the competition of foreign ships. Foreign shipowners do not pay their sailors such high wages as American shipowners are required by law to pay. Also, it costs about twice as much to build a steamship in the United States as in foreign countries. These two facts make it difficult for American ships to go out "on the seven seas" and compete with other countries for the shipping business of the world.

The world is smaller. It seems strange to say that the world is smaller, but it is true in a real sense because of man's conquering time and space. The rapid developments in transportation and communication have resulted in our living "nearer" to our neighbors in Europe and Asia than our forefathers lived to their neighbors in the next state. 

1. Make a survey of all the modern means of communication you can find in your community. Report to class. 

2. Visit a telephone office and report to the class how connections are made there. 

3. Make a talk on the lives of Samuel F. B. Morse, Alexander Graham Bell, Cyrus W. Field, and Guglielmo Marconi. 

4. Make a list of the ways in which your daily life is influenced by modern means of communication. 

5. Make an oral report on the postal service in your community. 

6. If you have taken a trip recently, report to the class about the different kinds of transportation you used and observed. 

7. Compare transportation at the time of the 

Revolution with that of today. 

8. If you have ridden in an airplane tell the class all about it. 

9. Make a report on the lives of Henry Ford, Robert Fulton, the Wright brothers, Samuel Langley, Charles A. Lindbergh, and James Watt. 

10. Make a list of some of the things you use, the distribution of which depends upon modern transportation. 

11. Make a list of our transportation and communication problems and discuss possibilities for solving them.

CHAPTER XVI MEETING OUR PROBLEMS AS CONSUMERS 

We are all consumers. It does not matter whether we are young or old, rich or poor, clever or stupid, we are all consumers. We consume food when we eat. We consume gasoline and tires when we ride in automobiles. We consume clothing, pencils, chalk, and anything else that we use. 

What we consume. All people do not consume the same things or the same quantities of goods. 

Some consume bacon and eggs for breakfast; others, orange juice and toast; and some consume only milk and dry bread. Have you ever thought about the number and kinds of articles you consume? One can go into the stores and find hundreds of articles from farm and factory which people use and consume. Millions of people are producing the things that are consumed; while thousands more spend all their time trying to find new and better products for our consumption. 

Who are the producers? Individually we would not have much food, clothing, or other things, to consume unless others produced them for us. If we could use only those things which we could raise or make, our supply of consumable goods would be very limited. We must depend upon others for most of the things we use. Those who raise and 239

make the things we consume are called producers. 

All people who work are producers. Some people produce more of one article than they need for their own use. They sell the surplus to others who do not have as much of this article as they desire. This exchange of goods is called trade. Since it would be too inconvenient for producers to trade goods for goods, we have developed a medium of exchange called money. A person who has more goods than he needs, trades the surplus for money and in turn exchanges the money for other products. 

The problems of the consumer change. In the early days the majority of the American people lived on farms and produced at home most of the things they consumed. Most of the food, the clothing, and even the furniture, was produced on each farm. This meant that the family did not have so great a variety of goods to select from as we have. 

Only necessities and a very few luxuries were consumed by the people. Little money was needed because people bought few things. Peddlers went about over the country selling a few of the common household articles. A few crossroad stores served the people in the community. Since only a few goods were sold in these stores, the buyers had only a small list from which they could make their selections. The buyers and sellers were usually good friends and trusted each other in their business dealings.

Today, the consumer buys practically everything he uses. He prefers to buy where there is a large

variety of goods from which he can make his selections. Goods are attractively made and displayed, and widely advertised; and the consumer is made to feel a need or desire for them, but money is required with which to buy them. Most children spend more money and buy a greater variety of goods now 

A consumer makes a choice. than their grandfathers did a few years ago. Our stores have become larger, and hundreds of different articles are sold in them. The buyers and sellers today are often strangers. We visit more places, buy more goods, spend more money, and meet more problems as consumers than did our forefathers one hundred years ago.

The standard of living. If we look about us we see some people who seem to have plenty of everything. They drive new automobiles; they wear good clothes; they have a variety of foods on the table at each meal; and they spend a large sum of money for luxuries and the non-essentials of living. 

We say that these people maintain a high standard of living. On the other hand, we often see people who have no automobiles; they wear shabby clothes; they have scarcely enough food to keep them alive; and they spend little or no money for luxuries. We say that these people maintain a low standard of living. Usually, the standard of living is governed by the amount of income the family receives. This is not always true, however, as some people who are good managers can buy more food and clothes than others with the same amount of money. 

Wealthy people spend a smaller percentage of their income for food than do those with small incomes. Indeed, we can almost measure the standard of living which a family maintains by the part of its income which is spent for food. Families with an income over $25,000 usually spend only about 3 per cent of it for food. Families with incomes of less than $1,000 per year usually spend more than 50 per cent of it for food. Wealthy families spend about 33 per cent of their incomes for luxuries, while poor families spend less than 2 per cent. 

Improvements in mining, manufacturing, and agriculture have made it possible for all to live on a higher plane of life. The home of an average

worker has more comforts now than did the homes of the early European kings. Those kings had no telephones, electric lights, radios, or automobiles. 

They had plenty of money, but they could not buy the conveniences which are found today in the average home. At the present time about two-thirds of the homes in America are equipped with bathtubs; one-fourth of the homes have vacuum cleaners; and one-fifth of them are equipped with electric fans. More than 1,000,000 electric refrigerators and more than 22,000,000 radios are now in use in the 27,000,000 American homes.

More of the modern conveniences are found in the homes in towns and cities. The chief reason for this is that water, gas, and electrical conveniences can be furnished more easily and more cheaply to people who live in groups. At the present time, however, many of the modern conveniences are being carried to farm homes. Many rural people now enjoy the advantages of running water in the houses as do their friends in the city. Four-fifths of the families on the farms have automobiles to take them from place to place. Most farm people live on or near good roads. Farm conveniences will increase very rapidly with the bringing of electricity to the rural areas. 

Electricity, with its almost magic usefulness in lighting the home, running the electric refrigerator, operating the radio, running the vacuum cleaner, the flat iron and other equipment, is taken as a matter of course by most city dwellers. Farmers, as

well as their city friends, need its magic because it will end much of the drudgery of farm life. Farmers can use this new hired hand, "Mr. Kilowatt", to milk the cows, cool the milk, churn the milk, saw the wood, pump the water, wash the clothes, grind the feed, and do many other chores around the farm. 

Only about eleven per cent of the farms in the United States had electricity in 1934. Congress has since passed a law making available to farmers a loan fund of $500,000,000 to be used over a period of ten years for rural electrification purposes. 

Groups of farmers may organize a coöperative, borrow from this fund, build rural electric lines, and repay the government over a period of twenty years. 

Cities and private corporations also can borrow from this fund for the purpose of building rural electric lines. It is hoped that cheap electricity will be available soon in all rural communities. 

Buying as a problem of the consumer. Very few people have enough money with which to buy all the things they need. This means that they must make choices between the many things they are not able to buy and the few articles which they are able to buy. The wise family will make a budget. By a budget is meant a careful plan for spending the income in order to derive the greatest benefit from it. A budget plan usually covers a certain period of time; for example, a week, a month, or a year.

In making the budget the family will decide how much of the income is to be spent for food, clothing, rent, and for the other household expenses. A

family of ordinary means usually will set aside about 30 per cent of the income for food, 15 per cent for clothes, 30 per cent for rent and upkeep of the house, and the remaining 25 per cent for recreation, church, and other things. A great variety of articles available requires careful buying. 

The American people eat less of some types of food than they did a few years ago. Thirty years ago each person consumed about 220 pounds of flour and about 78 pounds of meat each year. Today, each person consumes less than 175 pounds of flour and less than 50 pounds of meat per year. On the other hand, there has been a great increase in the consumption of fruits and vegetables. Formerly, fresh fruits and vegetables were seldom sold out of season.

Now, due to modern methods of refrigeration and rapid transportation, we are able to buy fresh fruits and vegetables during the entire year. 

Present guides to buying. When a boy buys a knife, a pair of shoes, or a cap, how does he know that he is obtaining good materials? We do not know how to examine most of the things we buy in order to find out whether they are made from good materials. The old Romans had a rule which said, Caveat emptor. This term meant, "Let the buyer beware." If the buyer did not get his money's worth, it was considered his own fault. Buying in accordance with this rule has caused the consumer to lose money in making his purchases. 

How can the consumer know that he is getting the kind of goods he thinks he is buying? One or more of the following guides may be used by consumers in making purchases: 

1. Inspection. This guide is a fairly safe one in buying such things as fruits, vegetables, and handkerchiefs, but few of us could tell by inspection whether an electric fan or a can of corn would be worth the purchase price. 

2. Experience in previous use. If we have previously used one article, we know by experience whether another article like it is worth the price we are asked to pay for it. For example, if one has eaten a certain brand of candy bar, one will know the quality of another just like it. 

3. Guarantees. Many products which we buy are guaranteed to last a certain period of time, or to

. 

give satisfactory service. Some guarantees seem to be worth very little, but they often help to give the buyer needed confidence in the article he is considering. 

4. Salespeople. Some buyers rely on the honesty and judgment of salespeople in determining the value of an article. Honest clerks, however, do not always know the real value of goods. 

5. Reliable stores. Some stores have a reputation for selling only the best of goods. Consumers buy with confidence in these stores. 

6. Testimonials. Many products are sold on the testimony of previous users. This is especially true in regard to medicines. However, testimonials are usually a very poor guide in making purchases, for the products that appeal to one buyer might not appeal to another. 

7. Price. Many people utilize price as a guide in making purchases. Frequently, the cost is considered an indication of quality. 

8. Testing agencies. Buyers sometimes test or have others test the goods that they buy. Batteries, flashlights, and eggs are often tested before they are bought. 

9. Specifications. A description of the article to be bought often serves as a guide. Machinery, furniture, and other articles described in catalogues are bought in this way from mail order houses. 

10. Grades. Many goods are graded as first, second, or third class, or as A, B, or C grade. Milk, for example, is graded as A, B, or C.

11. Labels. Many people buy goods altogether by label. Foods, such as tomato catsup, canned peas, or pineapple are often purchased because they are recognized as standard merchandise by the manufacturer's labels. 

12. Advertising. Buyers are often influenced to purchase a certain article because of advertising.

Although any or all of the above named guides may be followed, the consumer cannot be certain of getting his money's worth. Which one of the 75 shaving creams is best, or which one of the 164 fountain pens will write most efficiently and last the longest? Which of the many makes of automobiles is the best bargain? How do we know? Do we really know the answers to these questions or do we buy blindly?

Advertising as a friend or enemy of the consumer. 

We see the work of advertisers everywhere. If we read the newspapers and magazines, go to the movies, drive along the highways, or listen to our radios, we are told by the advertisers that we should buy certain articles. American business men are spending more than $2,000,000,000 a year to advertise their products. About three-fifths of this amount is spent for newspaper and magazine advertising. 

A good advertising agent studies the desires of the consumer. He knows, for example, that we all wish to be healthy, and that we desire to be well-liked by our friends and associates. The advertisements point out how the products advertised have in them the qualities most desirable, and thus they create

in us the wish to buy them. We see pictures of foods that make us hungry, and pictures of cold drinks that make us thirsty.

Advertising has some advantages for the consumer. It stimulates business and informs the people about the many available articles from which to make a choice. The housewife reads the advertise- Services of others are consumed by individuals. ments and budgets her money to meet her needs before she goes shopping. This saves both time and money. The proper kind of advertising is very helpful to the consumer. Much advertising, however, is detrimental to the consumer's interest. This is especially true when advertising influences people to buy things they should not have, or to buy one thing when they think they are buying another.

According to the advertisers, some cigarettes will improve appetites, certain breakfast foods will serve as brain food, and certain patent medicines will cure almost any disease. We know that most of these claims are extravagant, because the products cannot produce the results claimed in the advertisements. 

The advertisers know that on many articles, the American buyers look first for style, next for price, and last for quality. The producers know that by changing the styles, they can make more sales. 

Women buyers especially are interested in style, and, for this reason, styles in women's clothes change often. Even automobiles are sold on the basis of style. An automobile manufacturer who did not change the style of his automobile soon would have few buyers for his product. 

Ways of paying for what we buy. One of the problems involved in the purchase of an article is to determine the plan we wish to use in paying for the purchase. Some people always take their money with them when they go to the store and pay cash for what they buy. Others take no money with them, but write checks in payment for the goods they buy. 

This is also called a cash payment because the merchant exchanges the check for money. Other people buy a bill of goods and tell the clerk to "charge it”. 

Thus, the buyer will pay the store owner at the end of the week, month, or some other agreed period of time. Some people buy certain articles on the instalment plan. This means that the buyer pays a part of the cost when the article is bought, and the

remainder in regular weekly or monthly instalments.

A large percentage of the things we buy are paid for in cash. Merchants who sell for cash can usually sell for a somewhat lower price since they are not required to keep books, make collections, or lose anything from bad accounts. In a study made in 

New York City, it was found that prices in cash-andcarry stores averaged about 17 per cent less than they did in stores that carried "charge accounts" and delivered the goods to the consumers. 

The majority of people often do not have enough ready money to pay cash for the more expensive articles which they buy, such as electric refrigerators, sewing machines, radios, and automobiles.

Rather than wait until they can pay cash for these articles, many consumers buy them on the instalment plan. It is estimated that the American consumers are now spending about $7,000,000,000 each year on instalment purchases. Most of this sum is spent for automobiles, furniture, and electric supplies. When a purchase is made on the instalment plan there is usually an extra, or "carrying charge", made for the privilege of being able to use the article while payments are being made. The "carrying charge" is made to pay for interest, the extra cost of keeping books, making collections, and paying insurance on the articles purchased. The extra expense for instalment buying is often greater than the buyers realize.

Protecting the American buyers. The national, state, and city governments are rendering great

services to the American consumers. We have already learned how the national government is protecting us from the sale of impure drugs and foods. 

In addition to this service, the Federal Trade Commission gives information to the consumers about certain brands of goods that are sold to the people. 

This commission also studies prices in order to find out whether or not various companies in this country are making too much profit or being unfair to their competitors. The commission informs consumers in case certain companies are using misleading advertising.

Since the federal government itself is the largest single consumer in this country, it has set up an agency known as the Bureau of Standards, to supply the information that a consumer should have. The 

Bureau is used only by other governmental agencies, since it does not have the power or money to furnish information to individual consumers. The Bureau of Standards operates many testing machines to determine the real worth of hundreds of different products which the government uses. This Bureau can accurately determine the best tooth paste, vacuum cleaners, shoes, automobiles, and many other articles which are sold to consumers.

The state and city governments have passed various laws to protect the consumers. All states have laws regulating the use of weights and measures. 

State and city inspectors test scales and gasoline pumps to see whether or not the consumers are getting full weight or measure in their purchases. Other

inspectors test gasoline to determine whether or not it meets the standards set up by the state. The government requires also that the contents of a container be shown on the label.

Private organizations help the American buyers. 

Many private organizations furnish valuable information to the consumers in this country about the goods that are sold. The Fire Underwriters Laboratories test electrical equipment to see that it is safe for its users. The Better Business Bureau in the larger cities warns buyers about defective goods and unfair practices. The American Medical Association furnishes the people with valuable information about drugs and foods. The American Home 

Economics Association gives helpful facts to the housekeepers and home makers. Various consumer groups have been organized to help members buy more wisely. The federal government started its 

Agricultural Extension Service in 1914 to give farm groups information of value concerning food products. Groups of consumers in the cities are being aided in a similar way by the Consumers Project of the United States Department of Labor. Under this plan, Consumer Councils are established in various cities and towns. The Council then furnishes the members with whatever information they may need in order to buy wisely many types of desired merchandise. Much of this information is furnished through the Consumers Guide, monthly magazine published by the Consumers Council of the United States Department of Agriculture. Other consumer

organizations are the Consumers Research organized in 1929, and the Consumers Union which began its work in 1935. These organizations print bulletins which advise their members concerning the merits of various brands of merchandise. 

Today, many consumers are buying their goods through organizations known as Consumers Coöperatives. A Consumer Coöperative is a group of people organized to make purchases as a group rather than as individuals. The first Consumer Coöperative was started at Rochdale, England, in 1844 by 28 poor English weavers. Only in recent years, however, have the American people given much thought to the Consumer Coöperative movement. There are about 6,500 Consumer Coöperatives in this country, serving about 2,000,000 American families. These families spent more than $500,000,000 through their coöperatives in 1936.

Income and price as problems of the consumer. 

Very few children and very few adults buy all the things they would like to buy. Most of us can think of many articles we should like to have. If we had more money, or if prices were lower, we could buy more goods. Therefore, the quantity, or class, of goods we buy depends largely upon two things: first, the size of our income, and second, the prices of the things we want to buy. 

The total annual income of all the people in the United States in 1938 was about $64,000,000,000, an average of about $500 for each man, woman, and child. This does not mean that every person in the

United States is receiving $475 each year, for some are receiving more than this amount, and some are receiving less. Those with small incomes can spend very little except for the necessities of life. As long as a large per cent of the people have too little income or buying power, we will have many products for sale which they need, but are unable to buy. 

Every school boy knows that the amount of goods he can buy depends partly on the price he is charged for them. When prices are low, he can buy more with his money, but when prices are high, he can buy less. Why are prices higher at one time than they are at another?

There are many things which influence the prices we must pay for the things we buy. We shall name some of the most important ones. 

1. The amount of money available for use in the 

United States affects commodity prices. People spend more money when it is plentiful than when it is scarce. When people begin to spend a great amount of money, prices go up. This is because money becomes cheap when there is a great amount of it, and it takes more cheap money than dear money to buy the same goods. When money is plentiful and prices are high we say we have inflation. 

2. The supply of goods and the demand for them affect the price. When there is a scarcity of goods the owners will often charge more for them because they know that those who want them cannot buy them at other places. If there were only one hundred automobiles in this country, one hundred wealthy

people would pay very high prices for them in order to secure them. People formerly thought that the prices of commodities depended altogether on supply and demand, (the supply, or quantity, of the goods, UNITED 

Courtesy Standard Oil Company of California

The airplane consumes gasoline. and the demand, or market, for them). It was argued that when there was a large supply of products on the market, the owners would offer them at reduced prices in order to dispose of them. We know now that many other things influence prices even when there is a surplus or a shortage of commodities on the market. 

3. Prices are sometimes determined by those who control our large business organizations. If one company or one group of companies controls the manufacture or sale of a certain article, those who

control the company or companies could decide what the price would be, and all consumers would be forced to pay this price or do without the article. 24104 Special truck for hauling gasoline to be consumed in automobiles. 

Thus, if one company controlled the manufacture of aluminum, or automobiles, or cash registers, or soap, this company could tell us what we would have to pay in order to secure its product. Likewise, if one company owned all of the telegraph lines or gas lines, the consumer would have to pay the price asked or not use the telegraph or gas service. When one company controls the production or distribution of an article, we say that it has a monopoly on that article. 

4. The government sometimes regulates the prices which the consumers must pay for goods. This is especially true of goods sold by companies that have monopolies. The government now regulates the rates the consumers must pay for services furnished by railroads, bus companies, insurance companies, power and gas companies, and other utilities.

CHAPTER XVII STUDYING THE SERVICES OF MONEY AND BANKING 

Money, a medium of exchange. Two of the things most necessary and of the greatest convenience which the buyer uses in making his purchases are money and bank checks. If we did not have money, it would be necessary to trade articles and goods or services for the things we want. For example, if there were no such thing as money and we wanted a new pair of shoes, we would go to a merchant and trade some other article to him for the shoes. This ancient custom, called barter, was in use before we had money. What articles could we trade to the automobile salesman for a new car? It would be inconvenient if we had to carry goods around with us to trade for other goods which we needed and wanted. Imagine taking goods on a trip to trade for gasoline, and suppose the filling station owner did not want what we had to offer in exchange. We can see that money, as a medium of exchange, is necessary in present-day business. Money also sets the standards of value of the various commodities and services. 

Gold, silver, and coin. Where does our money come from? In the United States, all the money is made, and its value declared, by the United States 260

Government. Congress determines how much and what kinds of money shall be coined, or printed. 

Congress has decided that we have three kinds of hard money alloys-silver, nickel, and copper. Our hard money is coined at mints in Philadelphia, San Francisco, and Denver. The silver is coined into dollars, half-dollars, quarters, and dimes. Nickel is made into nickels, and copper is coined into pennies. Until 1934, the government coined gold pieces. 

The government has called in all the gold coins, melted them into gold bars, and stored the bars in large government vaults in the city of Washington, and at Fort Knox, Kentucky.

Paper money. In addition to the hard money, the federal government also makes paper money. Most people find it convenient to use paper money rather than hard money; for this reason, the government makes paper money available in much greater quantity than hard money.

Paper money is as good, or dependable, as the government upon whose authority it is issued. All of our paper money is made by the Bureau of Printing and Engraving at Washington, and is guaranteed by the government. It is based upon gold or silver in the vaults of the National Treasury or upon securities in the Federal Reserve Banks.

Three different kinds of paper money are now made: Federal Reserve notes, gold certificates, and silver certificates. Paper money wears out when we handle it just as do our clothes, shoes, or other articles made of perishable materials. But did you ever hear of anyone's throwing away a five-dollar bill

because it was worn out? We do not throw away the old worn-out bills; we exchange them for usable bills at our local bank and the bank exchanges them with the Federal Reserve Bank of its district. They are finally sent to the National Treasury at Washington where they are either renovated or destroyed. 

Thousands of bills are worn out each day in this country. A new one-dollar bill will last only a little more than a year; bills of greater value last longer because they are not handled so much. When the government destroys a current bill it always makes a new one to take its place. The Bureau of Engraving and Printing is now making about $20,000,000 in paper money each day. It is printed from special plates on linen paper, reënforced with silk thread. 

This gives the money durability, and makes counterfeiting difficult.

The Federal Reserve Banks. All national banks, and most state banks, operate under the Federal Reserve Banking Act, passed in 1913, and amended in 1935. The act is directed by the Federal Reserve 

Board, consisting of seven members appointed by the President of the United States. Under this law the country is divided into twelve districts, each with its Federal Reserve Bank. Each district Federal Reserve Bank is owned, and partly controlled, by its member banks. These Federal Reserve Banks deal with banks only. They have been given extensive powers, with restrictions, which enable them in many ways to give prompt and efficient banking service to the member banks of their districts.

Banking is essential to commerce. People usually carry very little money in their pocketbooks and purses. If a person should suddenly come into possession of a large sum of money, he would think at once of depositing it in a bank. A bank is a place where people deposit their surplus money in safekeeping until such time as they may need it. There are thousands of banks throughout our country, serving the people of the cities, towns, and rural districts. A bank renders many services. 

Merchants and business men could not carry on their daily affairs without banks. Anyone who buys or sells goods of any kind has almost daily need of a bank. Each merchant carries or sends to the bank

for deposit the money which he has taken in during the day. The bank clerk receives the money and makes an entry of the date and amount in the depositor's pass book, which is a receipt. The depositor may withdraw his money from the bank as needed by filling out and signing an order called a "check". 

The check is made for a certain amount, to be paid to a person, firm, or organization. When the merchant or depositor wishes to pay an account he gives a check for the amount. The person or firm to whom the check is given can then go or send to the bank for the actual money; or, as is usually the case, he may deposit the check at his own bank and receive credit for it just as if it were actual money, which it represents. The bank carries on a great deal of its business with the aid of checks. Of course, the banker always keeps enough money on hand to pay any depositor who might want his money. 

Banks lend money. Where does the banker get his pay for doing all this work for people who use his bank? He lends to others part of the money that people deposit with him. Those who borrow the money pay the banker interest on it. If the average amount to the credit of the depositor is large enough to yield a profit from lending the funds, no charge is made for safely keeping the customers' money, for paying checks drawn upon the account, and for various other services. If, on the other hand, the customer's average balance is so small that when lent, the interest received will not pay the bank for the expense of handling the account, a small fee is charged the customer.

Banks sometimes lend their credit. For example, a person might borrow $100 from the bank; the bank would credit the borrower with $100 on its books and permit him to draw checks to the value of $100; the borrower would pay interest on the $100 although he might not have received a cent in real money. We see that banks render a double service: they keep safe the money of the man who, for the time being, has more than he needs; and, by lending, they aid the man who finds a temporary need for more than he has on hand. 49-00

Fill all blank spaces by drawing a line through the unused space. Avoid flourishes in your signature. A simple, legible signature is the hardest to forge. Confidence in banks. Why is it that a person, without fear of losing his money, will walk into a bank and deposit his savings with a banker whom he has never seen before? Because, under our present banking system, it is almost impossible for a de-

positor to lose his money. In the first place, all national banks, and most state banks, operate now under the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation

Act, which guarantees as much as $5,000 of the money of a depositor in a bank. In the second place, the state and national governments have passed laws under which our banks must organize and operate. 

Men are sent out by the government several times a year to examine the banks to see whether they are meeting the required banking regulations.

Another department maintained by banks is the Department of Savings, where customers may deposit any amount, large or small, and after a certain time receive interest on their money. In 1936, there were more than twenty billion dollars in the savings accounts of the various banks and trust companies of the country. Many banks have also a 

Trust Department, which takes care of estates, and inheritances, and administers the wills of deceased persons. For a small fee the banks provide safetydeposit boxes for their customers. The customer thus has a private fire-proof and burglar-proof box for the safe keeping of his bonds, stock certificates, deeds, or other valuable papers. For the convenience of depositors who are unable to go to the bank before closing, night deposit vaults have been installed by many banks.

Most banks are progressive and public spirited and do much good for their communities. Their officers are usually able and willing and, when called upon, give sound financial advice to their customers.

There is no safer place for one to put his money than in a good bank.

PART THREE INDIVIDUALS WORKING THROUGH THE AGENCY OF GOVERNMENT

Franklin D. Roosevelt. 

The Thirty-second President of the United States.

CHAPTER XVIII ELECTING OUR OFFICERS 

We have studied in the preceding chapters about individuals within groups; how groups work, live, learn, and play together. We have considered also many interests in our everyday lives as we meet the problems of living together. We have learned that there must be control by the individual, by the group, and by the nation. Our liberties are guaranteed to us so long as they do not conflict with the liberties of others, but we must know the established rights of individuals, of groups, and of the nation. 

It is necessary to direct our attention to the study of government in order to learn how we elect our officers, how laws are made and enforced, and the place of the courts in government. 

Government by party. We have learned that before governments can operate they must have officers. Political parties are the chief agencies by which officers are elected. They, therefore, contribute to the operation of our government. A political party is a voluntary association of voters holding similar views upon public questions. The voters organize a party for the purpose of influencing the public to support its principles and to elect its candidates to office. A party advocates principles which it thinks will advance the public interests and gen- 271

eral welfare if they are made the basis of governmental policy. This can be done only if the candidates of the party are elected to office. 

A system in which there are two parties of nearly equal strength is best for a democracy. This makes it possible to have informative and stimulating debates on the issues and for each party to check upon the other. It causes each party to put forth its best men for public offices, and to render its best service when it is in power in order to be able to claim the support of the people at the next election. 

Throughout our history there have been two major parties, with minor parties at times attempting to displace the major parties. Since the Civil 

War, the major parties have been the Democratic and Republican parties. The origin of the Democratic Party may be credited to Thomas Jefferson of Virginia, while the principles of the Republican Party may be traced to Alexander Hamilton of 

New York. In general, the Democratic Party has championed states' rights, and the Republican Party nationalism, or a strong central government. However, parties modify their programs as do individuals because of the changes that are constantly occurring in our American life. 

How political parties serve. 1. Parties nominate candidates for offices. Nominating candidates for office is a great service to voters because it prevents the voters from having to choose officers from a large list of unknown people. A Democrat, Republican, or member of any other party has a right to

expect that a nominee of his party will stand for the principles of his party. Each can vote for his party's nominees with the feeling that he is voting for the right man, one who will uphold his party's principles. 

2. The parties conduct campaigns. Political campaigns are for the purpose of educating the voters on the issues before the people concerning their government. They increase the interest of the voters in public affairs and promote better government. Books and pamphlets are printed and circulated; meeting places for public speaking, and radio addresses are arranged; and the voters are encouraged to go to the polls on election day to vote for the men of their choice. 

3. Issues favored, others opposed. The written platform of a party declares its principles and its position on the issues of the campaign. It is a difficult matter to get a great many men and women in a convention to agree on a set of principles which satisfy the entire party and maintain its interest and unity. The formulation (making) of the platform demands the attention of the wisest members in the party. 

4. Parties express public opinion. In a democracy, there must be a means to secure the expression of public opinion and to formulate this opinion into principles and policies. After these principles and policies are formulated, laws are proposed to carry them into effect. In this way, public opinion exercises an influence upon government.

5. Parties supervise the activities of the government. One party acts as a check upon the others. 

Often the chief business of the opposition parties is to criticise the policies of the party which has charge of the government. Criticism of error often brings about improvement. It makes a party careful in its selection of candidates for office. 

6. Parties secure coöperation. The members of a party will often yield their individual views to a general party program, which makes it easier for legislatures to pass laws and to coöperate with executives.

Party machinery. A party must be well organized in order to perform these services. Parties are organized very much like the government itself. They have leaders, committees, and conventions.

Parties nominate their candidates through conventions and primary elections. Each party holds a national convention which nominates the candidates for President and Vice-President and formulates its platform with the various planks, for the national campaign. These conventions establish a national campaign committee for each party. It is composed of two persons from each state, one man and one woman, and the chairman of each committee is the head of his party.

There are state conventions and state committees for similar purposes for each party in each state. 

Each party's state convention adopts its own platform, which usually is in harmony with the party's national platform. Under the supervision of the

national committees of each party are the state executive committees, the congressional or district committees, and the county committees. This completes the national organization of each party. 

Primary elections are held in all except four of the states for the nomination of United States senators and representatives, and state and county officers. By this method, a candidate must receive a majority of all the votes cast for the candidates running for the same office in order to be nominated. For example, if A, B, C, and D were running for the nomination for governor, A would have to receive more than half (a majority) of all the votes cast for himself and B, C, and D to be nominated. In case there is no nomination made in the first primary, a second primary, sometimes called a "run-off", is held.

After the nomination is made, the regular election follows. In states where the two-party system exists, there is a contest between the nominees of each party for the same office. This is a party battle. Consequently, there is first the contest between the candidates of the same party for the nomination to an office, and then there is a contest between candidates of different parties for election to the office. Nomination merely gives the right to become a candidate for an office while an election gives the right to hold the office. 

Qualifications for voters. 1. In granting its citizens the right to vote, a state is under certain restrictions or limitations of the Constitution of the United States, which says that no one can be denied the

right to vote on account of sex, race, color, or previous condition of servitude. 

2. The state, however, may fix the qualifications of voters, provided it does not violate any of these restrictions. All the states require voters to be at least twenty-one years of age; and a certain period of residence in the state and the county where they vote, is required. No state permits insane persons or convicted criminals to vote. Some states require educational and even property qualifications. Voters must also be registered; and nearly all of the southern states require the payment of a poll tax. 

3. Voting is not a right, like freedom of speech or freedom of religion. A voter is really an officer of the state and he should perform his duty in a serious manner. He should prepare himself to vote intelligently because he helps to direct the affairs of the state when he casts his ballot. 

Registration of voters. A list of all the voters and their addresses is made in order to prevent persons from voting in one locality or precinct and then voting again in another. Officers and registration places are selected by local officials to make the lists of legal voters. A time is fixed during which each voter must appear at a registration place and give his name, age, address, and such other information as may be required. If any error or fraud is found in voting, the person involved is sought out and the mistake corrected or the guilty person punished. 

Ballots. Ballots are printed and distributed at public expense. Most of the states use what is known

as the Australian Ballot. This type of ballot is printed on one large sheet of paper, and has the names of all candidates of each party in separate columns in order that it may be easy to vote a straight party ticket.

OFFICIAL BALLOT SPECIAL ELECTION FOR CONGRESSMAN, 7th Congressional District: 

Donald C. Watson of Union County 

John W. Gaines of Union County 

Walter Lance of Austin County 

Henry Clay Murray of Lee County

Virginia Cole of Austin County 

Pat M. Thomas of Union County

At a general election, ballots are much larger to afford space for the names of many candidates for the various offices. 

The Australian Ballot provides for secret voting, which is essential to a democracy. Unless the voter is independent and can vote his true convictions, the ballot box becomes a joke or a farce. A voter who

is frightened or controlled by money or pressure of any kind is deprived of the high privilege of helping to control our civic affairs. 

Voting. When a person goes to vote, he takes with him his poll tax receipt (if he has had to pay a poll tax) or registration certificate which he received when he registered. This is checked against the record books of the election officers; and, if it is found to be correct, he is then given a ballot with a number on it. He next goes into an election booth to mark his ballot; if he cannot read, or is unable to mark it, election officers assist him. When he has finished, he folds the sheet so as to keep it secret and drops it into the ballot box. 

Election officers. The law prescribes the number of election judges and clerks necessary to hold the election at each voting precinct. Usually both major parties are represented and watchers may be appointed. Workers for candidates or issues must be at a certain distance on the outside of the buildings in which the voting is being done. 

The election officers have charge of the ballot box and the ballots, and supervise the voting. When the voting has ended, they open the ballot box and count the votes. They report the results of the voting to the county election chairman. These reports are called the election "returns".

1. What is a political party? 

2. List all the political parties you know about and indicate some of the things each stands for.

3. Investigate the differences between the principles of the Democratic and the Republican parties. 

4. Discuss reasons for voting for the man and voting for the party. 

5. Attend an election and write a paper on your observations. 

6. Hold a primary and an election within your class. 

7. Find and read newspaper clippings about elections and political parties.
