10. List the qualifications for a voter in your state. 

11. What important local elections have been held in your district this year? 

12. How can a voter learn of the ability and qualifications of candidates for office? 

13. Discuss how the public attitude affects an election, 14. How are state officers now elected? What changes in the system of electing officers do you recommend?

CHAPTER XIX MAKING OUR LAWS 

Why we have laws. Whenever a number of people get together in any kind of organization, it is necessary that rules be made and observed. In your games and school activities you have seen that it is necessary to have rules. It would be impossible to play baseball or football without rules to govern the play. There are rules for sports, clubs, and school and home activities. Every group must have rules so that each person will know his rights and duties. 

If everyone observes the rules, there will be no trouble and everyone will receive fair and just treatment. If, however, someone violates the rules, there may be trouble. The rules, then, furnish the basis for settling the difficulties. They tell what the punishment shall be for the violation.

Rules should be made before any trouble arises. 

They are made by all to be observed by all. No one is given special favors or advantages by them, but all are treated alike. Rules help to keep peaceful relations among people. They promote honesty, cooperation, fair play, and justice. Everyone admires good sportsmanship. Those who play the game according to the rules are the ideal sportsmen, students, and citizens.

Our government also is based on rules. The rules 280

of the government are called laws. A good American citizen will obey the laws at all times. Just as a group makes rules for all of its members, so the government makes laws for all of its citizens. 

The government itself is under a set of rules called a constitution. A constitution is proposed by representatives of the people and afterward adopted by the people. A constitution provides rules of conduct for all of the people of a state or a nation in their political relations. It is the law of all the people and is superior to all other laws. Whenever there is a conflict between a new law and the constitution, the constitution must govern. 

The kinds of laws in the United States. In the 

United States there are four kinds of laws: (1) constitutional law, (2) statutory law, (3) common law, and (4) equity. Constitutional law is the supreme law of the people. It establishes the form of government and fixes and limits its powers. It describes the structure of the government and declares what the government can and cannot do. It grants certain rights to the government, it fixes the rights of the people, and it sets up certain rights that the government cannot take from the people. 

Laws made by a legislative body are known as statutory laws. The United States Congress and the legislatures of each of the forty-eight states make statutory laws. Hundreds of such laws are made during each session of our legislatures. 

Common law is not made by a legislative body, but is a group of customs which have developed

and which people recognize. In England long ago, every community had certain rules of conduct and as these communities were brought together into a nation, their rules were combined into a common law for all the people. After long years of being accepted and enforced, they came to have the effect of law. Judges applied the common law in the courts.

Common law was adopted and used in all of the early colonies. Later it was continued in the states after independence was won from England. In this way we came to use the common law. It is interesting to know that only English-speaking people use it. Since common law is based on custom, it has changed as new customs have developed and old ones have been dropped.

Equity is a body of maxims or principles which developed from decisions of the secretary or chancellor of the English king. Whenever a dispute arose, people could go to the king's secretary for a decision. He would settle the dispute in the fairest manner possible, provided the laws were inadequate. 

Thus, common law and equity worked together to make a system of laws to provide justice to all men in the ordinary affairs of life. Like common law, equity was brought to America by the colonists and was retained after the revolution as a part of our law.

Statutory law, if made in harmony with the constitution, prevails over common law and equity.

THE FOUNDATION OF AMERICAN GOVERNMENT 

Signing the Constitution. 

Ewing Galloway

Classification of duties of government. The framers of our Constitution wanted to separate the several powers of government. Therefore, the government of the United States is divided into three great branches: (1) the legislative, or lawmaking branch, which in our national government is the Congress; (2) the executive, or law-enforcing branch, which is the President and all of his assistants; (3) the judicial, or law-interpreting branch, which is the system of courts.

Legislative bodies in the United States. Since it is impossible for all the people to meet and make laws, they elect representatives to make laws for them. Each governmental unit has a legislative body. In the United States, we have three units—the city, the state, and the nation-each of which has its own legislative body.

Cities and towns that have received charters from the state, giving them the right of self-government, have lawmaking bodies usually known as city councils or commissions. The members of these bodies are elected by the voters of the cities and towns. 

They make the laws, usually called ordinances, which apply to their respective cities. These ordinances must not be in conflict with a state law or with the state constitution. If an ordinance declares one thing and the state law another, the state law will be enforced rather than the city ordinance.

Each of the states of the union has a lawmaking body, usually called the state legislature. All state legislatures, except one, are bicameral; that is, they

are composed of two houses or groups. Nebraska has a unicameral, or one-house, legislature with 43 members. The two houses of the legislatures are. generally called the Senate and the House of Representatives. The Senate is the upper house and is smaller in size than the House of Representatives. 

The Senate usually consists of from twenty-five to fifty members, while the House of Representatives has from a hundred to a hundred and fifty members. 

The state legislatures divide the state into senatorial and representative districts, and the voters in each of the respective districts elect senators and representatives to represent them in the legislature. 

The lawmaking body for the nation is called the Congress of the United States. The Senate is the upper house and contains ninety-six senators, two from each state in the union. All states, therefore, regardless of their size, are equally represented in the Senate. The House of Representatives, with 435 members, is the lower house, but is a much larger group than the Senate. The members are elected by the voters from congressional districts, into which each state is divided. Each congressional district contains about 381,000 people. 

The Constitution of the United States declares the powers of Congress and the kinds of laws it may or may not pass. The passing of all other laws must be left to the state legislatures. Congress makes laws that concern the entire nation, while a state legislature makes laws affecting its state only. Congress legislates on such things as commerce, general

-- 
John N. Garner. 

Vice-President of the United States presiding over the Senate.

taxes, the army and navy, public lands, crime in interstate matters, and general welfare. The state legislatures pass laws on both local and general matters, such as crime, education, property, business, and those things closely related to the problems of our everyday life.

How laws are made. A proposed law is called a bill. Let us follow the process of making a law in the 

Congress of the United States. 

1. Introduction of a bill. A bill may be intro duced by a member in either one of the houses of the legislature, and must be read on three different days. 

The title of the bill is read so that all of the members may know the nature of it. This is called its first reading. Then, the presiding officer of the house refers the bill to a committee. 

2. Committees. Each house has regular standing committees so that the work can be done more rapidly and effectively. These committees are selected by a special committee chosen for this purpose. (In most state legislatures, the committees are appointed by the presiding officer of each house.) 

They carefully study the bills referred to them before making their recommendations that they do or do not pass. Sometimes a committee will amend a bill or it may refuse to report it out of the committee. 

3. Second reading. When a committee reports on a bill it is read the second time, after which the members of the house may discuss it and amend it. 

It is then voted on and, if the bill passes, it will go to its third reading.

4. Third reading. At the third reading, on another day, the members of the house again consider the bill and decide whether or not to pass it finally. 

It is voted on again, and if passed by a majority vote, it is signed by the presiding officer and sent to the other house for consideration. The constitutional rule requiring the reading of a bill on three separate days may be suspended by a two-thirds majority of either house. 

5. Consideration by the other house. After passing one house, a bill is sent to the other house, where it must go through the same steps it did in passing the first house. If it passes the second house, it is signed by the presiding officer, and sent to the President of the United States for his consideration. 

6. Action of the President. If the President should sign the bill, it would become a law. However, the President may not favor the bill, in which case he can veto it, or allow it to become a law without his signature. If the bill is vetoed, it is returned to the house in which it was first introduced and again considered. If both houses then pass the bill by a two-thirds majority, it becomes a law without the signature of the President.

The passage of a bill through the state legislatures is about the same as that described above. In the sixth step, instead of the President, the governor of the state considers the bill. In Nebraska, where there is only one house in the legislature, the bill naturally does not have to be considered by a second house.

Lawmaking by the people. In a democracy, laws are supposed to be the expression of the will of the people. Sometimes legislative bodies, composed of representatives of the people, do not make satisfactory laws. Some states have adopted a method by which the people themselves may make laws. Two methods which have been practiced in several states and cities are called initiative and referendum.

Initiative means that the voters propose the laws. 

This is done by having about fifteen per cent of the voters to sign a petition, which is a written request for a law. Then the petition may be presented to the legislature for its consideration, or it may be voted on by the people at an election. If it should receive a majority vote, it becomes a law. 

Referendum is a means of allowing the people to vote on a law. The measure is prepared by the legislature and then referred to the people to vote on at an election. The voters may accept or refuse the measure.

The initiative and the referendum may be used successfully to a certain extent, but they cannot take the place of the legislature as the lawmaking body. 

In order to have good laws, therefore, it is necessary that every citizen study the issues and vote intelligently for members of the legislative body. 

1. List several reasons for having laws.

CHAPTER XX ENFORCING OUR LAWS 

In the last chapter we learned how laws are made, and that the bodies which make laws are called the legislative branch of the government. The laws would be useless unless officers were made responsible for their enforcement. This power is given to the executive branch of government; and we shall now study the way in which it is used in the city, county, state, and nation.

City executives. There are several kinds of city governments. If a city has a council for its lawmaking body, it usually has a mayor for its executive. The mayor, like the council, is elected by the people of the city. He, then, usually appoints his assistants to help him enforce the laws made by the city council.

Since the early part of this century, many cities have adopted the commission plan of government, in the place of the mayor and council plan. Under the commission plan the voters of the city usually select five commissioners to operate the government. 

Each commissioner is in charge of one part of the city's business, and is known as the City Street Commissioner, Fire Commissioner, Police Commissioner, and so on. The commissioners elect one of their 291

number as mayor, who serves as the presiding officer.

Regardless of the kind of legislative body a city may have, it may decide to employ a city manager as the executive of the city. The city manager is usually selected by the council or commission, to whom he is responsible. He is placed in charge of all of the affairs of the city, and may select his assistants to aid him in doing this work. 

The executive work of the city covers many different fields, such as police and fire protection, maintenance of streets, parks, and playgrounds, management of water and light plants, control of traffic, and so on.

County and township executives. Most of the southern states are divided into counties. In New 

England and the middle and western states, there are townships. These counties and townships are units of the state government and are under the control of the state. They have no lawmaking bodies but have executive officials who administer the laws of the state within their boundaries. The chief administrative body of the county or township is the board of commissioners or supervisors. This board looks after such matters as taxation, schools, bond issues, the building of roads, and caring for the poor.

The county or township also has other executive officers. The head of the police force of the county is the sheriff. His assistants are called deputy sheriffs. There is usually a tax collector and asses-

Enforcing Our Laws 

293 sor, a county clerk to keep records, a treasurer, a county attorney, and a school superintendent. In most cases these officials are elected by the voters. 

In some states the county commissioners employ a county manager, similar to a city manager. He looks after the affairs of the county under the supervision of the commission.

State executives. The governor of each state is its chief executive. There are other executive officers the lieutenant-governor, secretary of state, attorney-general, treasurer, and superintendent of education; but they, like the governor, are usually elected by the people and are responsible only to them. Therefore, in many states the executive authority is not so strong as it would be if the governor were given more power and more responsibility in the management of the state's affairs. The term of office, salary, and qualifications of each of the executive officers are declared in the constitution. 

In some of the states the governor has the power to appoint boards and commissions to perform certain executive duties, such as may be needed in the operation of state schools, highways, railroads, health, and so on; and these boards are usually responsible to the governor. Before the appointments become final they must be confirmed by the Senate. 

National executive. The President of the United 

States is the head of the executive department of the national government. His term of office is four years, and he is elected indirectly by the people of the United States.

The Constitution provides that the President shall be elected by an electoral college. The electoral college is made up of a group of citizens from each state, the number being equal to the number of sena- 

The dome of the National Capitol. 

tors and representatives from the state. The people vote for the candidates for the electoral college but indirectly they know they are voting for the President and Vice-President, for the electors always vote for the party nominees.

The first step in the election of the President is his party nomination. The major political parties, the Democratic and the Republican, and others, hold conventions to which every state in the union sends delegates who nominate candidates for President and Vice-President.

Enforcing Our Laws 

295 During the summer and fall, the candidates address the public on the radio and travel about the country meeting people and making speeches. The campaign lasts until the presidential election, which takes place on the first Tuesday after the first Monday in November, every four years-1940, 1944, and so on.

After the November election, those who have been chosen as "electors" meet at their respective state capitols and cast their votes for President and Vice- 

President. Their votes are then sealed and sent to

Washington, where they are opened and counted by the Vice-President, in the presence of both houses of Congress.

The candidate who has a majority of the electoral votes is declared elected. If none has a majority, the House of Representatives elects the President from among the three candidates having the highest votes; and if no one has a majority for Vice- 

President, the Senate elects the Vice-President from the two highest on the list.

The President is inaugurated on January 20. The 

Chief Justice of the Supreme Court administers the oath of office, which is as follows:

I do solemnly swear (or affirm) that I will faithfully execute the office of President of the United States, and will, to the best of my ability, preserve, protect, and defend the Constitution of the United States.

In case of the death, resignation, or removal of the President, the Vice-President becomes President. If he, in turn, should die, resign, or be re-

moved from office, the members of the cabinet succeed to the presidency in the order of the dates on which their departments were established, the first being the Secretary of State.

The President is a very powerful executive. He appoints officials, with the advice and consent of the Senate; he is responsible for the enforcement of laws; he is the spokesman of the United States in all foreign relations; he recommends legislation to 

Congress; and he is Commander-in-Chief of the army and navy.

It would be impossible for the President personally to direct all of the departments of the government for which he is responsible. The Constitution gives him the authority to appoint, with the advice and consent of the Senate, men and women to serve as heads of the various governmental departments. 

They advise and confer with the President on government business and they are known as his Cabinet. 

The members are usually prominent influential friends of the President and belong to the same party.

Each Cabinet member is the head of a department of government. There are ten of these at present: the Departments of State, Treasury, War, Navy, Post Office, Justice, Interior, Agriculture, Commerce, and Labor. The President may ask Congress to create other departments if the need arises.* *See Chapter XXVI for a discussion of the various departments.

Enforcing Our Laws 

297 Many administrative duties are performed by a third group-independent boards and commissions, such as the Interstate Commerce Commission, to regulate commerce between the states, and the Federal Reserve Board to control the national banks. Such boards and commissions are responsible to the President, but they do not belong to any of the executive departments of government. 

The national executive divisions of our government employ thousands of persons in law enforcement.

CHAPTER XXI INTERPRETING OUR LAWS 

The judicial function. We have seen how laws are made, and how laws are enforced. The third function of government is the interpretation of laws. 

This is called the judicial function. It is natural that people will differ in their opinions and that disputes will arise. It is the duty of the judicial branch, or the courts, to settle these differences and disputes. The courts interpret the laws and make clear what they mean. They try persons who have violated the laws. The courts, when appealed to, endeavor to protect our rights. They tell us what our rights are, and, through the executive branch of our government, see that they are protected. The courts protect society, our liberty, our property, and the rights of the individual; they are the safety of our government. Let us now look at the systems of courts in the United States. 

City courts. The city courts try cases involving city laws or ordinances. Large cities have several city courts. These courts try local police cases, traffic violations, and the like. 

State courts. 1. The systems of state courts vary in the different states. In most states the justice of the peace court is the lowest ranking court. There is usually a justice of the peace court in each 299

precinct, or subdivision of the county. This court tries only cases involving petty crimes and small amounts of money. Some of its decisions may then be appealed to a higher court. In others its decisions are final. 

2. Next in importance and authority is the county, district, or in some states, the circuit court. In these courts most of the important cases, both civil and criminal, have their beginning. A court where the first trial is held is a court of original jurisdiction (authority). If the case is appealed to a higher court, the higher court has appellate jurisdiction. The county (or district or circuit) court has both original and appellate jurisdiction. Cases may be started there or they may be appealed from the city and justice of the peace courts. Cases may be appealed from the county to the district or next higher court. 

3. Next in authority are the courts of appeal. As the name suggests, they have appellate jurisdiction. 

They hear cases which are brought up to them from the lower courts, although in some cases they have original jurisdiction. Some states have an appellate criminal court and an appellate civil court. There is usually a state supreme court which is the highest state court. The decisions of the highest state courts of appeal are final unless they involve questions about the Constitution of the United States, or its laws, or treaties.

In most states the judges of the courts are elected by the people. There are also county, district, and state's attorneys who are elected by the people. These

attorneys represent the state in civil suits and prosecute the criminals or law violators.

United States courts. The United States govern ment also has a system of courts. The United States courts, or federal courts, try cases involving the United States Constitution, laws, and treaties. They also handle cases in which the parties concerned live in different states, or in which a citizen of the United States is suing a citizen of a foreign country, or in which one of the states is a party. Such cases as these could not be tried by a state court; therefore, they are handled by the federal courts. 

The three kinds of regular federal courts are as follows: (1) district courts, (2) circuit courts of appeal, (3) the Supreme Court. The district courts are the lowest of the federal courts. district courts in the United States. has at least one district court. 

There are 83 

Every state

Nearly all federal cases are first tried in the district courts. A case may be appealed from the district court to a circuit court of appeals. There are ten circuit courts of appeal. They have only appellate jurisdiction, and in most cases their decisions are final.

Under certain conditions, however, a case may be appealed to the United States Supreme Court.

The United States Supreme Court is the highest court. It is probably the greatest judicial tribunal (court) in the world. It is composed of nine judges who are appointed by the President with the approval of the Senate. One of these nine judges is

Supreme Court Building, Washington, D. C.

the chief justice and the eight others are called associate justices. They do not have a set term but hold office during good behavior. Judges may retire on full pay when they are seventy years old if they have served at least ten years as federal judges. 

The Supreme Court has both original jurisdiction and appellate jurisdiction, depending upon the matters involved. The Supreme Court has the power to decide whether or not an act of the President, Congress, or a state is constitutional. If the act is contrary to the provisions of the Constitution of the United States, the Supreme Court will declare it unconstitutional. 

Special federal courts are not in the regular system of federal courts. They hear special cases only, as classified by Congress, such as the Court of Claims and the Court of Customs Appeals. The courts of the District of Columbia try cases in the District of Columbia, and there are special courts in the territories and dependencies of the United States. 

Court procedure. How does a case get into court?

First, there must be two or more parties. In a civil case it is due to a dispute or misunderstanding between two or more persons. The court will determine which is right. In a criminal case the people as a whole are considered to be one party, and the criminal the other party.

There are two or more attorneys or sets of attorneys in each case, one for the plaintiff, the one who complains or brings the suit; the other for each defendant, who defends the suit. Witnesses will be

brought in by each side to testify as to the facts. 

The facts and evidence will then be decided on by a jury, if asked for; otherwise, the judge may decide the case without a jury.

Trial by jury is one of the most treasured rights of an American. There are two types of juries used in the United States. The grand jury first carefully investigates the case to see if there is sufficient evidence to indict a person, or bring him to trial. The petit jury is the one which hears the case and decides whether a person is innocent or guilty. If the petit jury decides that the person is guilty, the judge or the court passes the sentence. The verdict may be "guilty", or "not guilty"; and the judge may assess various kinds of punishment if the person is found "guilty".

Need for improvement. Although we have a good judicial system in the United States, there is need for improvement. Some of the judicial problems confronting us should be examined. 

1. Short terms of office. It has already been noted that federal judges are appointed for life. Judges in most of the state courts, however, are elected by the people for short terms of office. When this is true, the judge who wants another term must campaign for re-election. A judge should be fair and just in his decisions and not influenced by politics. 

2. Salaries of judges and attorneys. In order to get the best qualified officers the state must pay good salaries. Often the salaries paid to judges and at-

torneys are so low that the best men do not want the positions. The salaries should be high enough to attract the best men to the judicial positions, and to keep those who are already experienced in that field. 

This will build up the efficiency of the judicial department. 

3. Importance of jury service. Too many American citizens try to get out of jury services. The shirking of this duty means that often juries will not be composed of our best citizens. Jury service is a duty that we should perform. We should be willing to give this time and service to our government in order to help bring justice through an efficient court system. All citizens should learn the duty of public service. 

4. Delay and expense of court procedure. There is a need for simplification of court procedure so as to bring about more speedy justice. Often a case is in court for many years. These delays are not necessarily caused by the judges or by the courts, but are due to the system of rules or procedure. The rules are so numerous and so technical that many errors arise. There are ways of evading the law through legal technicalities. Therefore, we have re-trials and appeals, and some cases drag along for years before they are settled. The procedure should be simplified so that justice may be obtained more rapidly and at less cost. Delays and appeals greatly increase the cost of justice.

Our judicial system is of the greatest importance to us. It is a great protection to our rights, our

liberty, and our property. We must do all we can to make and to keep our courts efficient and above criticism.

CHAPTER XXII PAYING THE COST OF GOVERNMENT 

How the money is spent. In the previous chapters, we have been studying the many ways in which our governments serve and protect us. They build and support our schools and help to educate us. They make our cities and rural communities safe to live in, and they protect us from sickness and diseases. They build highways and bridges and dig canals. They help to conserve our natural resources and protect us from foreign invasion. These and many other things are done for us by our governments; for they protect us in all of our proper activities. 

It costs large sums of money to perform so many services. The more a government does for us, the more money we must pay for its support. We are under obligation to pay our just share; for it is only through the protection of the government that the individual may carry on his work. Some people think that the things the government does for us do not cost us anything. Everyone, however, must help to pay the cost of government. 

How governments secure money. Governments use five methods of securing money with which to pay for the services they provide: (1) levying and collecting taxes; (2) requiring the payment, called a fee, of a small sum for costs of court, and for such 308

Paying the Cost of Government 309 services as issuing licenses and recording deeds; (3) assessing and collecting penalties, such as fines; (4) selling directly certain articles and services, as land, water, gas, transportation, and postal service; (5) borrowing from banks and citizens through the sale of bonds.

Several governments must secure money. Since there are several governments providing services to the individual and to the group, it is necessary for each government to secure money with which to pay its bills. The national government, the state government, and the various local governments must be supported. The result is that each citizen must contribute to the support of more than one government. In addition to the national, state, and local governments there are frequently special governments, such as school districts, road districts, and water improvement districts that must be supported.

Methods of taxation. The greatest source of income for all governments is taxation. Every individual pays taxes of some kind, directly or indirectly. A direct tax is one that is paid by the person upon whom it is levied. Income taxes are direct taxes. An indirect tax is one that is not paid. by the person upon whom it is levied, but is transferred to others. Sales taxes are indirect taxes. 

Sources of revenue for local government. Counties, cities, towns, and districts, such as school districts, depend largely upon general property taxes for their incomes. People who own property pay a certain

rate upon the value of their property. Many local governments collect occupation taxes, school taxes, and other special taxes. 

Sources of revenue for state governments. Besides a tax on general property, states usually levy a tax on special property, such as stocks, bonds, and mortgages. It is difficult to tax property of this kind, however, for it cannot be located so easily as real estate.

Some states levy an income tax. An income tax is graduated, so that the more one makes, the higher is the rate of taxation. If one makes very little, one pays little or no tax. There is usually an inheritance tax, also, the rate on which varies, depending upon

Paying the Cost of Government 311 the value of the property, and from whom it is inherited.

Sometimes there is a tax placed on corporations and other businesses in a state. This is generally called a franchise tax. The business firms pay this tax or fee for the privilege of carrying on business and for the protection that the state gives them. 

States also receive money from licenses, fines, and court fees. The automobile license fee is a source of large income in most states. When a person is fined for violating a state law, the money goes into the state treasury.

Most states collect a poll tax from persons over twenty-one years of age before they are allowed to vote.

The sales tax is becoming a popular source of revenue in many states. This is a tax on the sale of merchandise. Some states furnish special tokens of small denomination to assist in making the correct change when paying the sales tax. 

Sources of revenue for the national government. 

The national government gets its revenue largely from import tariffs, income taxes, and the various internal-revenue taxes.

A tariff on imports is a tax paid on goods brought into this country from foreign lands. The tariff rates on the many articles imported are fixed by Congress, and vary from time to time. 

An income tax is levied by the national government and by some of the states. The national government was not allowed to pass an income tax law

until after the Sixteenth Amendment to the Constitution was adopted. 

Internal revenue is a form of indirect tax. Internal-revenue taxes are sometimes collected by selling stamps to be placed on articles that are sold. 

They are also collected from amusements, such as picture shows and ball games. We pay an internalrevenue tax on gasoline and on many manufactured articles.

Other methods of taxation proposed. It is difficult to determine the fairest system of taxing in order to raise money to operate the governments. Some people are of the opinion that our present systems of taxation have many faults. Other systems have been proposed from time to time. One is known as the single tax, which limits taxation to land only. 

Another, is the modified property tax, which fixes the rate of taxation according to various recognized classifications of values.

All in all, it takes a very large amount of money to run our governments. Our governments are attempting to do more for us all the time. We should not complain of paying taxes without careful thought of what the government does for us in return. 

Characteristics of a good tax. Without taxes we could not have the services which are furnished by the various departments or divisions of government. 

Our problems of taxation consist primarily of developing a uniform system which is adequate, fair, and just. Such a tax should be (1) based upon ability to pay; the wealthy individual can pay more

Budgets. Budgets have become particularly important of late years, since the population has increased, and since the government is attempting new ways of serving the people. A good budget system should be connected with the various executive departments. In order to spend efficiently, most governments have adopted a budget system. They should investigate all of the ways in which money may be fairly collected and wisely expended, and endeavor to strike a balance in order that the government may not run into burdensome debt.

The budget for the federal government is made up in the Treasury Department. Before each session of Congress, the Director of the Budget must report to the President upon the financial condition of the government: its expenses for the past year, its sources of income, its possible sources of new income, and the probable expenses for the next year. The 

President uses this report in making his recommendations to Congress for the appropriation bills. 

The individual citizen and the cost of government. 

Through the ballot, the individual citizen has a part in making decisions with respect to how much money will be spent by the various governments; what the money will be spent for; and how the governments will secure the money. This is done by voting for

Paying the Cost of Government 

315 candidates for membership of legislative and executive departments in the several governments, who will represent the will of the people in making decisions. This plan of governing places the responsibility of decisions upon the citizens, both as individuals and as groups.

PART FOUR THE DEVELOPMENT AND MEANING OF THE CONSTITUTION

Signing the Declaration of Independence.

CHAPTER XXIII MAKING THE CONSTITUTION 

After the discovery of America by Columbus, Spain claimed nearly all of the two continents of North and South America. By the year 1588, Phillip II 

of Spain had become so ambitious that he attempted to annex England to his vast domain. Angered because Queen Elizabeth of England refused to marry him, he sent his great fleet to conquer England. The defeat of the Spanish Armada by the small English fleet was a victory that influenced the whole world; for the English people became more powerful in the 

Old World, and more adventurous in the New.

Early English colonies. The English people were slow to establish colonies in the new country. Most of the people who came were interested only in the possibility of finding great treasures, and so it was not until 1607 that the first permanent English colony was settled at Jamestown, Virginia. There followed the colony in Massachusetts settled by the Puritans who were seeking religious freedom; one founded by Roger Williams in Rhode Island; one by 

William Penn in Pennsylvania; and the Maryland colony founded by Lord Baltimore.

Desire for freedom. These groups of colonists had sought refuge and freedom in a new land, and each colony was founded by people seeking different lib- 319

erties. Their desire for freedom from oppression in their religious and political life became the basis of the democratic ideals. 

King George III, who came to the English throne in 1742, interfered with the liberties of the people of England and the colonies even more than had the kings before him. This caused many more Englishmen to come to America, and also caused the colonists to be still more resentful of oppression. They were across the ocean from the Mother Country and they were not afraid. The colonists had hoped all along that they could save their liberty without declaring their independence from the Mother Country, but when tyranny became too great, they revolted against the king and the War of Independence began. 

The Declaration of Independence. After a year of warfare, the colonists resolved to become a completely independent nation. The war against England was directed by the Second Continental Congress, a body of representatives from all the colonies. 

At its meeting in Philadelphia on July 4, 1776, this body signed the Declaration of Independence. This historic document was written almost entirely by 

Thomas Jefferson.

A plan of union called the Articles of Confederation was then drawn up by the congress which became known as the Congress of the Confederation. 

All of the states adopted the plan by the early part of the year 1781.

Government under the Confederation. George 

Washington, who was Commander-in-Chief of the

Making the Constitution 

321 army during the war, defeated Lord Cornwallis, the British commander, on October 19, 1781; and with this victory, independence was won. The Americans had had six long years of war. Their food and clothing supplies were nearly exhausted. Jealousies had arisen among the states, and it was difficult for them to coöperate in any way. The Congress of the Confederation was not strong enough to furnish relief. It could not collect money or enforce the laws. It seemed that each state wanted to set up a separate government. 

Finally, through the efforts of George Washington, James Madison, and Alexander Hamilton, a convention was called to meet in Philadelphia to which each state was invited to send its delegates. 

George Washington was chosen as presiding officer. 

In attendance were many distinguished delegates representing all the states, except Rhode Island, which sent no delegate. Benjamin Franklin of 

Pennsylvania, although very old, was an outstanding delegate. Altogether, there were fifty-five members of the Convention. Madison, Hamilton, and Washington, all close friends, were in favor of a strong central government. 

The Convention was in session four months. The question which caused the most debate was whether to give more power to the central government or to the separate states. The problems of apportioning congressional representation according to the population, counting slaves as population, regulating commerce, method of selecting the President, and ways

Official U. S. Navy Photograph 

U. S. S. Constitution (Old Ironsides), famous guardian of the sea, was proudly named after the guardian of our rights.

Making the Constitution 323 and means of amending the Constitution, were also subjects of long argument and debate. 

After the new Constitution was finally agreed upon, the task of writing it in proper form was given to 

Gouverneur Morris of Pennsylvania. On September 

17, 1787, thirty-nine of the original fifty-five delegates signed the Constitution; some signed although they disapproved of parts of it, because they believed that it was the best thing they could agree upon; others refused altogether to sign.

The state conventions. The new Constitution provided that before it could go into effect it must be approved by state conventions in at least nine of the thirteen states. This was an interesting provision, since under the Articles of Confederation of 1781, it was necessary for all of the states to give their approval before the Confederation went into effect. 

The people of the various states elected delegates to state conventions, and by the summer of 1788 eleven state conventions had approved the Constitution. North Carolina and Rhode Island did not approve the Constitution, and thus join the union, until after the new government was under way. 

1. What was the leading European nation in America before 1588? What event occurred in that year to make England more important? 

2. What was the Declaration of Independence? 

Where was it signed, and when? Who wrote it? 

How would such an event be announced today?

3. Why do we celebrate the Fourth of July as a national holiday? 

4. When did the Articles of Confederation go into effect? What were they? 

5. When and where did the Constitutional Convention meet? Who presided over the Convention?

Who were the most important men in the Convention? What were the main problems of the Convention? When was the Constitution finally signed?

When did the Constitution go into effect?

CHAPTER XXIV THE PREAMBLE TO THE CONSTITUTION OF THE UNITED STATES 

We the people of the United States, in order to form a more perfect union, establish justice, insure domestic tranquillity, provide for the common defense, promote the general welfare, and secure the blessings of liberty to ourselves and our posterity, do ordain and establish this Constitution for the 

United States of America. 

We the people of the United States- In the history of government by the people, our forefathers were the first to establish a great national democracy. The expression, "We the people of the United States", is important for two reasons: first, it expresses the idea of nationality—all the people of the United States are included; and second, it shows the democratic character of our government-it was established by the people. The people acted through their representatives who composed the membership of the Federal Convention and proposed the Constitution. Later, their representatives as members of the various state conventions adopted the Constitution. We as a people govern ourselves by means of representatives. This makes our government a representative democracy. -do ordain and establish- Our Constitution is called the supreme law of the 325

land. Our constitutions are made by the people in their right as citizens. This means that all power or authority resides in the people through the ballot. 

The expression, "do ordain and establish", shows that the Constitution of the United States was established by the people in their original rights as citizens. -a more perfect union- When the colonies revolted from Great Britain in 1776, they became independent states. The states did not want to give up their rights; yet in order to live together as one nation, it was necessary to have a union. Hence, during the early part of the Revolutionary War, the states established a government under the Articles of Confederation, our first national constitution. It was a very weak organization, because the states refused to give it strong powers and to coöperate with one another to make it a success. "A more perfect union" means that one of the great purposes of the new Constitution was to improve and strengthen the one under which our forefathers lived from 1781 to 1789. The old union was one of states, while the new union was one of people and states. By making the new government rest or depend on the people as a foundation, it was made "more perfect", and became "an indissoluble union of indestructible states". -the establishment of justice- Justice and the prosperity and happiness of the citizens were the great ends to be achieved under the

Preamble to Constitution 

327 new democratic form of government. The government under the Articles of Confederation had been too weak to correct the evils and abuses of society. 

The government itself could not pay its debts. Its money became worthless. Merchants, investors, and creditors suffered great losses and conditions were such that business could not be conducted with any degree of certainty. Poor people were in debt and sound money was scarce and high. There were no federal courts to enforce honesty and fair play. It was, then, one of the chief objects of the new union to secure justice for all. —insure domestic tranquillity— 

The Englishman says "his home is his castle".

By this he means that he has the right to live quietly and peacefully in his own home, and no one without legal authority has the right to come into his home and disturb his life. He has the right to go peacefully about his work, his recreation, and his everyday life without disturbance or fear.

Again, there is always the possibility of social uprising, such as mobs, riots, and rebellions. Violence destroys property and the peace of the community. 

Matters such as these were in mind when our forefathers sought to insure "domestic tranquillity", or peace throughout the nation. -common defense- When our republic was established, practically all other nations were monarchies. Our forefathers expected to be forced to fight for the existence of

our democratic republic. It was necessary to make provision for military forces to maintain our form of government, and to defend it against foreign enemies. -general welfare- Possibly no phrase in the Constitution has come to mean more to our people than "to promote the general welfare". Its object is to develop the resources of the country, to improve conditions of health, education, industry, and agriculture, and in all practical ways, to promote the contentment and progress of the people. —liberty— The English Parliament had been regulating the lives and businesses of the colonists by a rigid set of laws. Therefore, our forefathers were primarily interested in individual liberty. They wanted selfgovernment in order to secure their personal and religious freedom.

Now, our country is noted for its great opportunities which have enabled both natives and naturalized citizens to become successful by virtue of their industry and ability. Under our plan of free selfgovernment, our industrial system has provided work for millions of our citizens; our political institutions have offered opportunities for the individual to rise to positions of service and distinction; and our program of education has brought self-reliance, enlightenment, and independence to our people.

Preamble to Constitution 

329 

1. Why is the new Union more perfect than the old Union? 

2. What is a constitution? What are the ad vantages of a written constitution? Who has the right to ordain and establish a constitution? 

3. Why is "the common defense" an important object of the Constitution? How is it secured? 

4. What are some of our liberties? Why is life more comfortable and richer than it was in the days of our forefathers? 

5. What is meant by an unwritten constitution? 

6. Convert your class into a constitutional convention, write a constitution, debate, and adopt it.

CHAPTER XXV STUDYING THE CONSTITUTION 

(Review Chapter XVIII as you study this part of the Constitution)

We have already studied the events which led to the making of the Constitution of the United States. 

In the study of the Preamble to the Constitution, we learned that the government was established by the people in order to secure justice, and preserve their liberties for themselves and their descendants. 

All boys and girls are interested in knowing not only the content but the language of the Constitution; and their interest will make them eager to read this document which has been the basis of our government for one hundred and fifty years. For this purpose, the Constitution in complete form is printed in the appendix of this book. 

In this chapter and those immediately following is presented, in simple language, the story of the Constitution. By studying these chapters first, you will find the Constitution itself to be an interesting document.

ARTICLE I. THE CONGRESS 

After the Revolutionary War, when the deputies or representatives of the states met to discuss and propose principles of government (the Constitution), they decided to use the English Parliament, 330

. 

with two houses, as a model for the lawmaking bodies. 

They called one group the House of Representatives and the other the Senate. Together, the houses are called the Congress. The word congress means to meet or to come together. This term was used by the early colonists in establishing a governing body during the War of Independence. All legislative or lawmaking powers for the nation were given to Congress by the Constitution.

House of Representatives. The House of Repre sentatives is composed of members who are elected in November of the even-numbered years. They are called representatives, and their term begins on 

January third, following the November elections. In order to be a representative, a person must be at least twenty-five years of age, a citizen of the state from which he is elected, and for seven years a citizen of the United States.

When the Constitution was being written, Rhode Island, Delaware, and other small states contended for an equal number of representatives from each state. After much debate, it was decided that the membership of the House should be based upon the population, but that each state, regardless of its size, should be allowed at least one representative; and that each state should have two senators. The House of Representatives is often called the lower house, and the Senate the upper house. 

A census of the United States is made every ten years, and the membership of the House of Representatives is fixed according to the population.

On account of the increase in population, the House of Representatives has grown from the original membership of 65 to 435. Each member represents a district with a population of approximately 

281,000. If for any reason a vacancy occurs in the

House, the governor of the state in which the vacancy occurs may call a special election for that district in order to elect another representative for the unexpired term.

In the House of Representatives, the Speaker is the chairman, or presiding officer. He is always a member of the political party which has the greatest number of representatives in the House. All officers are elected at the first of each new term. 

When an officer of the government is believed to be guilty of misconduct, or unfit for office, only the House of Representatives has the right to bring impeachment charges against him.

The Senate. The Vice-President of the United 

States is the presiding officer of the Senate. He votes on Senate measures only in case of a tie. If the President should die, the Vice-President would succeed him in office. The Senate elects a president pro tempore (temporary), to act as presiding officer if the Vice-President is absent. The other Senate officers are like those in the House.

The power to arrange the time, place, and manner of holding elections was given to the state legislatures; but Congress may, by law, change any of the regulations except those concerning places of choosing senators. Originally, senators were chosen by

the legislatures, but the Seventeenth Amendment gave the people the right to vote directly for senators, as they do for representatives. The people of each state elect their senators for terms of six years. 

The terms of all the members of the Senate are arranged so that one-third expire every second year.

When there is a vacancy in the Senate, the governor of the state in which the vacancy occurs may appoint a senator to fill the unexpired term, or he may call for a special election. 

If the House of Representatives should bring charges of impeachment against an officer, the Senate would act as the court to try the accused official. 

Only one President has had impeachment charges brought against him, but at the trial, the charges were unsupported by two-thirds of the senators.* 

If an official is convicted, he is removed from office and forbidden to hold office under the United States government.

Congress formerly met in December, but the 

Twentieth Amendment, adopted in 1932, changed the date to the third of January. Each house decides whether a newly elected member may hold office, in case his election is challenged. Each house makes its own rules, and must keep a journal or record of its daily work. A quorum is the least number of members necessary to hold a meeting. 

Senators and representatives receive a salary of $10,000 a year. They are allowed twenty cents a mile 

*Read in your history the story of President Johnson's impeachment trial.

from their homes to Washington and return to pay the expenses of traveling. They may send their mail free; and they cannot be arrested during a session of 

Congress, unless the offense is a very serious one. 

If a new position is created or the salary of an old one is raised by Congress, no member of that Congress may hold the position. 

How bills become laws. The procedure of making a law has already been explained in Chapter XIX. This is carefully provided for in the Constitution.

Cost of government. The cost of operating the government is very great and, as we have learned, is paid by taxation of various kinds. Congress may also borrow money on the credit of the United States. 

This is done by selling to the people bonds which bear interest, to be repaid at a certain time. 

Since the members of the House of Representatives are elected by the people and the members of the Senate were originally elected by the various state legislatures, it was believed wise to require all bills for revenue and taxation to originate or start in the House. This rule has not been changed, although the members of the Senate are now elected by the people. The House, whose members are elected for a term of only two years, remains closer to the people than the Senate, whose members are elected for six years. 

Additional powers. Congress has many other powers besides those of raising and borrowing money. 

It can regulate trade with foreign nations and be-

tween the states, but it does not have the power to regulate trade within a state. Laws may be passed that permit foreigners to become citizens of the United States.

One important power is that of coining money. 

In order to keep the values uniform, money may not be coined by states, but by the national government only. Anyone who makes counterfeit (false) money may be punished. During the time of the American 

Confederation, each state issued its own money. In the United States there are three mints where money is coined.

Congress can also determine our standard of measurements and weights. (See Chapter XVI.) 

It was the desire of the men who wrote the Constitution to stimulate interest and growth in invention and writing. They provided patent and copyright laws to protect those, who, through their industry and genius, produced new inventions or pamphlets and books. 

The United States postal system established by Congress is one of the most efficient public services in the world. Letters, postcards, money, packages, and various articles may be sent by mail for a very small fee. Special delivery, registered mail, and air mail are services which are performed by the United States postal system. There are air mail routes now to almost every part of the world. 

Further authority given to Congress by the Constitution includes the power to create federal courts with less authority than the Supreme Court. In order

to provide for protection and defense, Congress has been given many broad powers. It has the power to declare war; to raise, support, and regulate an army and navy; and to provide for punishment of 

Courtesy National Park Service, U. S. Department of Interior 

Wakefield House, George Washington's birthplace. crimes committed on the high seas. Congress has the right of exclusive legislation for territory belonging to the United States. In order to carry the powers into effect Congress can make any laws that may be necessary.

LIMITATIONS OF POWERS 

Congress. The authors of the Constitution wanted to establish a strong central government with ample

powers to carry on the business of the nation. However, the feeling of many of the deputies to the Constitutional Convention was in favor of giving the states more authority. As a compromise, certain limitations or restrictions were placed on Congress and certain important rules concerning the powers of states were very carefully written. 

It had been customary in England to hold in prison a person who was arrested for a crime until such time as he should be brought to trial. Often an innocent man was held for several years. The Constitution forbids this practice by allowing a person so held to be freed until the time set for his trial, provided one of his friends will secure for him a writ of habeas corpus, which means: "You must have the body" or, he may go free until tried. This privilege is always in force except in time of war, when it might be necessary to withhold it. 

All laws apply only to acts taking place after their passage. Therefore, Congress cannot pass a law to convict a person for a crime already committed. 

No person can be convicted of a crime except by a fair trial by a jury.

The Constitution provided that a direct tax could not be levied unless applied equally to all. This is a very difficult undertaking, and it was necessary to amend this provision before Congress could pass the income tax law.

No duty can be charged on goods going from one state to another, and Congress cannot pass laws which favor one part of the country as against another.

All of the money belonging to the government is safeguarded by the clause of the Constitution which provides that no money may be taken from the Treasury except with the consent of Congress. The spending of money is authorized by Congress according to the needs of the nation. Those persons by whom it is spent must make due account for it. 

The people did not want any titles of nobility, such as was the custom of England, France, and Spain; and so the Constitution forbids the granting of such titles.

The states. The Constitution not only restricts the authority of Congress but it also restricts the powers of the individual states. It declares that no state can make a treaty, grant permission to owners of private ships to fight and seize enemy ships, coin money, issue bills of credit, take away property of a person convicted of crime, pass a law impairing or changing the obligation of contracts or grant any title of nobility. 

There were other things denied to the states which the national government could do; for example, unless invaded or attacked, the states cannot keep troops in time of peace, or engage in war. 

By naming both the powers and the limitations or restrictions of Congress, the Constitution has prevented many misunderstandings between the central government and the various states. 

1. Divide the class into a Senate and a House of 

Representatives. Let the teacher be the President.

Pass a bill through both houses of Congress. Send it to the teacher, who vetoes it. See if it can be passed over the veto. 

2. Debate: Resolved: That the Senate is more important than the House of Representatives. 

3. Go to your post office and find out how to open a postal savings account. 

4. Find out some of the important bills that were before Congress in the last session. Find in the Constitution under what powers these laws were proposed.

CHAPTER XXVI STUDYING THE CONSTITUTION (Continued) (Review Chapters XIX and XX in studying this part of the Constitution.)

Just as we learned that it was necessary to have a leader in any group government, our forefathers recognized the necessity of making one person responsible for the operation of the national government and seeing that the laws are enforced. They placed this power in the President of the United States. (See Chapter XVIII for the procedure in electing the President and Vice-President.)

ARTICLE II. THE PRESIDENT AND HIS POWERS 

The President. As the Constitution was originally written, the candidate who received a majority of electoral votes became President; the one who received the second largest number became Vice- President. However, this could result in the election of a candidate from an opposing party to the vicepresidency. After the first three presidents were elected, this was changed by the Twelfth Amendment.

The President must be at least thirty-five years of age, and must be a "natural-born" citizen. You will recall that a person may become a representative or senator even if he is a naturalized citizen, pro- 340

vided he has lived in the United States a certain number of years.

If the President should die, the Vice-President would become President, and the Senate would select from its members a new Vice-President. 

The salary of the President of the United States is $75,000 a year. In addition to this, he is given an allowance in order that he may maintain himself in a manner expected of a President. He has a great deal of entertaining and traveling to do in connection with his duties as President. However, he spends much less money in this way than do the heads of some other nations.

When a new President is inaugurated (placed in office), the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court gives him the oath of office. This ceremony usually takes place on the front steps of the National Capitol before a great crowd of people. The new President then delivers his "Inaugural Address", or message, to the people. Since 1924, this speech and a description of the ceremonies have been broadcast by radio to the people all over the world. 

Presidents formerly were inaugurated on March 4, after their election in the preceding November, but a recent amendment changed the date to January 20, following the November election.

Powers of the President. The President is the highest officer in both the army and the navy. His title is Commander-in-Chief. He may use our soldiers to enforce the laws of the country or to resist an attack or invasion. He may order our land and

sea forces to go anywhere in the world. The President may grant pardons for offenses against the 

United States, except in cases of impeachment.

The President, as we have learned, may appoint his Cabinet members, each of whom is at the head of an executive department and has the title of 

Secretary, except the Postmaster-General and Attorney-General. As a rule, the President calls the members of his Cabinet for a conference twice each week. 

In this way, he keeps informed about the affairs of the government and is enabled to discuss them with the Cabinet.

For many years, there were only five executive departments, but now there are ten. The President may ask Congress to create new departments as he sees the need for them.

Executive departments. The Department of State takes care of our relations with other nations. We have ambassadors, ministers, and consuls, who live in countries all over the world and look after our foreign affairs. These representatives are directly responsible to the Department of State. This department keeps a record of all laws passed by Congress.

The chief duties of the Treasury Department are to coin and print money, collect taxes, prepare the budget, and pay the bills of the nation. 

The War and Navy Departments control the military affairs of the nation. The army affairs are controlled by the War Department. Both the army and navy have airplane divisions. One of their duties

is to look after some of our island possessions in the 

Atlantic and Pacific oceans.

The head of the Department of Justice is the Attorney-General of the United States, the chief law 

Official U. S. Navy Photograph 

Battleships and destroyers in formal naval drill. officer of the government, and is one of the two Cabinet-Department group who is not called "Secretary". His department has charge of the legal affairs of the government. The Secret Service agents and the G-men serve in this department. 

The head of the Post Office Department is known as the Postmaster-General. His department has charge of the postal service, including regular and

special mail, parcel post, postal saving banks, and air mail service.

The Department of the Interior takes care of some of the business within the nation, such as land service, education, national parks, Indian affairs, and reclamation service.

All the agricultural affairs of the nation are administered by the Department of Agriculture. 

The Department of Commerce makes surveys of business conditions in the nation in order to help commerce and trade. It makes a census or enumeration of the United States every ten years to secure information concerning the population. 

In the Department of Labor are many bureaus for collecting information concerning the problems of working people, as well as of those who are unemployed. One division provides services for the care of children. This department is also in charge of the business of immigration and naturalization, a service that provides a record of foreigners within our country and those who apply to enter it. These ten branches of the executive division of government render great service to the nation. 

There are, besides, a large number of boards and commissions in the government service which do not belong to any of the executive departments. Some of these are the Government Printing Office, Interstate Commerce Commission, Inland Waterways Corporation, Civil Service Commission, Federal Reserve Board, and various other independent boards and commissions.

Powers with Congress. Besides the powers of the 

President which we have already mentioned, there are some that he shares with Congress. One of these is his power to make treaties with foreign countries, subject to the approval of the Senate. He also appoints ambassadors, and other officers, with the approval of the Senate. 

Most of the positions in the government service, except those that are filled by appointment of the President, are under the Civil Service. This means that the persons holding such positions have passed tests to show that they are qualified to do the work; and they may not be removed so long as they do their work well. There are over 800,000 Civil Service employees.

When Congress convenes in January, the President either sends a message to be read or delivers it in person. He reports upon the affairs of the nation and, from time to time during the session of Congress, he suggests laws that should be passed. 

If an emergency should arise when Congress is not in session, the President may call the members to meet in a special session. 

Just as we send ambassadors and representatives to other countries to look after our affairs, so do they send representatives to our country to look after their affairs. The President receives these ambassadors upon their arrival in Washington and recognizes them as the official representatives of their country. However, he is not forced to receive them

if they represent a country which is unfriendly to the 

United States.

If the President should be found guilty of treason against the United States, or of bribery, or other high crimes, he could be impeached and removed from office.

ARTICLE III. THE SYSTEM OF FEDERAL COURTS (Review Chapter XXI as you study the judicial branch of the national government.) 

One of the weaknesses of the government under the Confederation was the lack of a judicial system to settle disputes and explain the laws. The makers of the Constitution had recognized this weakness, and they determined to remedy it by establishing a judicial system with a Supreme Court as final authority.

Supreme Court. The chief duty of the Supreme 

Court is to settle cases which have been tried in lower courts and have been appealed to the Supreme Court as final authority. Congress may establish any lower federal courts that it thinks are necessary. 

Federal judges are appointed by the President and they may serve as long as they do their work well. 

The United States Supreme Court judges take the following oath of office:

I do solemnly swear that I will administer justice. without respect to persons, and do equal right to the poor and to the rich; and that I will faithfully discharge all the duties incumbent on me as Judge, according to the best of my abilities and understanding, agreeably to the Constitution and laws. of the United States.

. 

Authority and duty. The Constitution defines the authority of the Supreme Court by naming the kinds of cases that may be appealed to it, and listing the few which may originate in it without first being tried in a lower court. Cases which may be appealed to the Supreme Court are those based upon acts of Congress, treaties, and interpretations of the Constitution of the United States. Cases that concern ambassadors, ministers, and consuls in foreign countries, cases between a state and the citizens of another state, and disputes between states, may be begun in the Supreme Court. 

The Constitution provides the right of trial by jury. This is one of the common laws brought over by the colonists from England. Since women now vote, many states permit them to serve as jurors. In some cases, according to the crime, the jury verdict must be unanimous; or, it may be based on a majority of the votes of the members of the jury. There is no uniform number for the membership of a jury. 

The Constitution defines treason and gives Congress the right to set the punishment. A person who discloses American military secrets to another government is guilty of treason. The trial of Aaron 

Burr in President Jefferson's time is the most famous treason trial ever held in this country. He was not convicted, because the court charge against him was not proved.

1. Let the members of the class work together and see how many pictures of presidents they can find. 

2. Write a story, telling what you know about the man who is now President. Do the same for the Vice-President. 

3. Debate: Resolved: That the President of the 

United States be elected for a seven-year term and that he be allowed to serve only one term. 

4. Have you ever heard the President make a speech? If so, tell the class about it. 

5. Find out the names of the heads of the departments who are in the President's Cabinet. 

6. Go to the library in your neighborhood and ask them to let you see the latest census report. How many people live in your town, county, and state? 

7. Find out the names of some of the members of the Supreme Court. If you can, find a picture of the Supreme Court. 

8. Find out where your federal district court and circuit court of appeals hold their meetings.

CHAPTER XXVII STUDYING THE CONSTITUTION (Continued) ARTICLE IV. THE STATES AND THE FEDERAL GOVERNMENT 

When the Constitution was being written, the question of whether certain powers should be given to the central government or to the separate states caused much debate and many compromises. Finally, the central government was given only certain powers necessary for its operation, and all other powers were reserved to the states and to the people. Each state must recognize the acts, courts, and records, of every other state, and the citizens of each state are entitled to the privileges of the citizens of the other states.

Sometimes, after a criminal has committed a crime, he runs away into another state. The governor of the state from which he escaped may write the governor of the state where the criminal is living and request that he be arrested and returned for trial to the state in which he committed the crime. This action is called extradition. 

Every state is guaranteed a republican (representative) form of government. Since no state may have an army of its own, the federal government guarantees protection against invasion. 

States and territories. There were only thirteen states at the time of the writing of the Constitution. 

The people had no idea how vast or important 349

were the territories that lay beyond them. If you will look at a map and compare the size of the original thirteen states with the size of the thirty-five that have joined the Union since that time, you can see that the provision to admit other states was indeed wise. Many disputes arose, for some of the states claimed that their boundaries extended as far as the Pacific Ocean.

All the new country that was explored was claimed for the United States, and that portion within our boundaries finally was divided into sections or territories and later admitted as states. Arizona and 

New Mexico were the last states to be admitted into the Union. However, we still have territories beyond our borders that are not states. They are all islands, excepting Alaska. Various departments of the federal government direct and supervise these territories.

Alaska and Hawaii are completely organized territories and have governments of their own, subject to the Constitution and laws of the United States. 

The President appoints the governors and judges for each of them. Their citizens are also citizens of the United States.

Alaska was purchased from Russia in 1867 for $7,200,000. It has more than paid for itself in the quantities of fish, furs, lumber, gold, and other minerals which have been shipped to the United States. 

Hawaii became a territory of the United States by treaty in 1898, and it is very important for our ships as a place to renew coal, oil, and other supplies.
